# Blockwright — plan

Native ANSI/IRC art editor. C++20, Qt 6 Widgets, Linux first, then macOS and Windows.
Feature baseline is Moebius; the additions are what IRC block art needs:
layers, script-generated layers, a live IRC byte budget and a craft lint built into the editor.

## 1. Goals and non-goals

**Goals**
- Moebius-level drawing tools, keyboard-first.
- Layers as real composited stacks, not draw-order functions.
- Generator layers: a script produces cells, the editor shows the result as a layer, re-runs it when parameters change.
- Our own project format that holds layers, scripts, references and metadata.
- Export `.ans` (with SAUCE) and IRC `.txt` (mIRC codes) that fit IRC line limits, checked live while drawing.
- A headless CLI from the same core, so scripts can render, check and export without the GUI.

**Non-goals for v1**
- Collaboration server (Moebius has one; nobody here uses it).
- RIPscrip, PETSCII, Sixel.
- Animation (see Phase 8).
- Bitmap font editor.

## 2. Why build it (what Moebius lacks)

- Unmaintained: last release 1.0.29 in April 2021, Electron 28, crashes on Ubuntu 24.04 (#245).
- No layers (#148), no TheDraw fonts (#226), no 24-bit editing (#157), no UTF-8 import (#160).
- Its UTF-8 export has a light-grey-becomes-white bug (#173).
- It opened our pieces as blank canvases when the SAUCE FileSize was 0.
- Nothing knows about IRC: no byte meter, no mIRC export, no preview of how a line gets cut.

Icy Draw (Rust, egui) is the closest modern editor and has layers, TDF fonts and Lua.
It is the reference design for layers and the file format, not a base to fork.

## 3. Lessons from our ANSI projects

These are what make it more than a Moebius clone.

| Lesson (source) | What the editor does about it |
|---|---|
| Half-block pixels and ░▒▓ shade cells collide: a pixel plotted into a shade cell leaves a fallback speck; outlines break shading; `plot` erases text | Separate layer kinds: **pixel layer** (2× vertical resolution, resolved to ▀▄█ at composite time), **cell layer** (glyph + fg + bg), **text layer**. Compositing rules are defined once, in the core. |
| Every piece ends with hand-written byte-budget hacks: iCE space runs, ▀/▄ flips, "settle to left neighbour" (`downfall-of-numenor`, `funkpower-joust`) | **Live per-row IRC byte meter** in the gutter, red past the budget. The optimisations become export options, not per-piece work: iCE space runs and ▀/▄ flips are the encoder's own and always on; settling to a neighbour is fit-to-budget (§8). |
| Raw `.ans` line length overstates IRC cost by 50–100 bytes; budgets were measured three different ways | One encoder (Blockwright's own, in `core/`) is the only source of truth for byte counts, in the meter, the lint and the export. |
| Budget is 407 bytes per line on EFnet via WeeChat; 512 minus `:mask PRIVMSG target :\r\n` in general | Budget set per project: preset "EFnet/WeeChat 407", or computed from a nick mask + channel. |
| Lettering most often fails on a letter that misreads at 1× (C/G, R/A, 0/D) | A **1× preview dock** that is always visible, plus an **IRC View** dock (and `render --irc`) in the mIRC palette with WeeChat's cuts (§8). |
| Speckle, stray cells, same-colour merges, dotted diagonals and flat areas spoil a piece | **Lint dock** running the lint rules live (`lint::lintCanvas`, as `cli lint` does, §9), each finding clickable to its cell. |
| IRC drops trailing black cells and blank edge rows, so frames on the last column vanish | Lint warning (`trailing-black`, `blank-edge-rows`) + export option to pad with black-on-black █ (`--pad`, View > IRC, §8). |
| `★ … “”` have no CP437 code and save as `?`; `○ ◙ ♪ → ←` are CP437 0x09, 0x0A, 0x0D, 0x1A and 0x1B, which `.ans` reads as TAB, LF, CR, SUB and ESC, so an `.ans` saves them as `?` too (XBIN and BIN keep them) | Glyph picker only offers mappable glyphs in CP437 projects; typing refuses both kinds and says which ("○ is CP437 0x09, which .ans reads as TAB; not typed", "★ has no CP437 code; not typed"); save, `convert` and lint (`no-cp437`) flag both and tell them apart, naming the control codes (`ansControlByte`, #300, #306). |
| Shading ladder: dither only between ramp neighbours, 1–3 cells per rung | Shade brush and gradient tool walk a named **ramp** (fire, sunset, ice, steel, skin), never arbitrary colour pairs. |
| Mirrored shapes come out a cell off with half-to-even rounding; small circles need tuned radii | Shape rasterisers in the core with tests for symmetry; mirror mode is a canvas transform, not a re-draw. |
| A piece is worked as named layers, each looked at alone and in partial stacks | Layers keep that model, and an imported scene (§7) opens with each of its layers an editor layer. |
| A piece carries notes, a log and earlier versions | Project format has room for notes and versions/snapshots (§6). |

## 4. Architecture

```
blockwright/
  core/      C++20 library, Qt-free except QtCore types where it helps
             cell model, palettes, CP437 tables, layers + compositor,
             ANSI/SAUCE/XBIN/mIRC/UTF-8 codecs, shape rasterisers, lint,
             project I/O (libzip, zstd)
  render/    glyph atlas + canvas renderer interface (QPainter first)
  app/       Qt 6 Widgets GUI: tools, docks, undo stack, preferences
  cli/       headless: render PNG, convert, lint, export, run generators
  tests/     Catch2; golden files from funkpower's pieces
```

- **Cell model:** codepoint (Unicode internally, CP437 mapping at the edges), fg/bg as palette index *or* RGB, attribute bits, transparent flag.
- **Renderer:** pre-built glyph atlas per font, `drawImage` from source rects, dirty-region repaint, integer zoom with smoothing off. Behind an interface so a `QRhiWidget` shader renderer can replace it if large canvases get slow. Avoids the GPU-driver problems that hit Moebius (Electron) and Icy Draw (wgpu). In `render/`: `CanvasRenderer` (the interface) and `AtlasRenderer`, which keeps a 1× cache filled from a `GlyphAtlas` (256 glyphs × 16 palette colours, 24-bit colours made on demand) and repaints only the cells marked dirty; views scale the cache by the zoom. Views draw in view pixels, each a whole block of `max(1, floor(dpr))` device pixels starting on the first whole device pixel inside the widget (`app/device_grid.h`), so under fractional display scaling (1.25, 1.5) every cell still covers a whole number of device pixels at every zoom and the 1× preview shows one block per canvas pixel; at 1.5 that makes the art a little smaller than the UI around it. It must draw exactly what `renderCanvas` (the CLI's PNG renderer) draws, and the tests compare the two.
- **Undo:** `QUndoStack` with commands that store cell deltas per layer (a live text layer's text, settings, face and style stack undo as property commands instead, §15). An edit is the active layer's delta (`LayerEditCommand`); the layer panel's changes are commands too (§5). The deltas are `CellEdit`s from `core/` (`edit.h`): keyboard edits are core functions that return one plus the next cursor, and the canvas view sends every edit through one `commit()`, which pushes it on the document's stack as one command (one per key; an edit that changes no cell is not pushed). Undo and redo repaint only the cells the edit changed and put the cursor back where the edit was made or left it. The modified flag is the stack's clean state: save marks it clean and open/new clear the stack, so undoing back to the saved cells clears the flag. Edit > Undo/Redo use the platform keys (Ctrl+Z, Ctrl+Shift+Z / Ctrl+Y).
- **SAUCE, iCE and letter spacing** are document state with undo. File > SAUCE... edits title (35), author (20), group (20), date (CCYYMMDD) and the COMNT lines (64 each, up to 255); the fields take only text CP437 can store, and OK waits until every field fits. A field (or the comment block) left as shown comes back byte for byte and is not checked, so a malformed date from a file (`00000000`) doesn't block editing the rest, and trailing blank comment lines from the record stay. View > iCE Colours and View > 9 px Letter Spacing are checkable. Each change is one undo step (`FormatCommand`, the SAUCE record and iCE before and after), so it sets the modified flag; the record's other fields (font, aspect, the flags not being changed) are kept, and a dialog accepted unchanged adds nothing. iCE follows Moebius and PabloDraw: blink and a bright background are one bit (`iceCell` in `core/`), so the cells stay as drawn and the toggle changes how they show and save. With iCE off a bright background shows as blink on its dark colour, drawn in its visible phase as `renderCanvas` draws blink, and saves as SGR 5; with iCE on a blink cell shows and saves as the bright background. A file whose iCE flag is off but which sets bright backgrounds anyway (SGR 100–107) opens with iCE on, as `writeAnsi` and `cli render` already treat it; its blinking cells lose the blink on open and keep their dark background, as `writeAnsi` writes them and `renderCanvas` draws them, and until the next save, saving warns that that many cells lost their blink when the file was opened with iCE colours and that the save doesn't put it back, and is refused from `saveFile` unless lossy saves are allowed. The open marks each lost cell (`Cell::lostBlink`, an editor-only bit inside the cell, #82), and `Document::encode` counts the marked cells the save still writes without a blink (`lostBlinkCount`, which `cli shade` uses too). Moved and copied cells keep their own mark (a paste, a stamp, a move, a flip or a turn, `CellEdit::moves`), so the mark goes where the cell goes: a lost cell moved, flipped, rotated or pasted stays lost, one made to blink again (in place or after a move) is back, and undo and redo restore the cells and their marks with them. A cell the edit lifts leaves a blank behind, so its claim goes with it: flipping a lost cell with its look-alike moves the claim and doesn't copy it. A drawn cell carries no mark of its own, whatever the brush built it from (colorize recolours the cell itself, despeckle copies a neighbour): `settleMarks`, which `Document::edit` and `cli shade` run on every edit, clears it on every drawn cell, so no other cell stands in for a lost one: a cell elsewhere that happens to save as the lost cell did, or that the flip of a selection puts in its place, earns it nothing. The mark lives only in the editor: no file, export, renderer or lint reads it, and `Cell::operator==` leaves it out, so cells that look the same are equal to lint, previews and tests; the edit builders compare with `identical()`, which sees it, so moving a lost cell onto one that looks the same still moves the mark. An edit that writes over a lost cell ends its claim: erasing it, drawing something else on it, shading or recolouring it is the artist's own edit, not a loss the open caused. The one exception is a cell, drawn, pasted or moved, that the save writes alike with the lost cell it covers when that cell stays put (`writesAlike`: the same CP437 byte, so ß and β are one glyph, the same palette colours and blink bit, other attribute bits ignored): it keeps that cell's claim (`writtenOver`), since nothing the artist can see changed. A cell an edit changes twice (mirror mode) is judged against what it was before the edit. A lost cell drawn over blinking with iCE on saves as the bright background iCE shows, which is what the artist sees, so it doesn't count. The ramp gradient's dither mark (`Cell::dithered`, §10) is a second bit beside it, kept the same way (moved and copied with its cell, left out of `==`, seen by `identical()`), with two differences: a drawn cell keeps it while it looks the same, and drops it on any other look, and a project saves it (`"dither"`, §5). Copying a lost cell copies the mark, so each copy counts while it doesn't blink, and the count can be more than the cells that lost their blink on open. Mirror mode makes the image of a moved, flipped or turned lost cell such a copy: `mirrorEdit` pastes the image as the moved cell flipped, mark and all, so moving a lost cell can leave its mirror image counted as a lost blink too, and the save warning can count more blinks than the save drops. The image isn't left unmarked instead, because two mirrored lost cells moved together would then keep one claim between them, and the save would drop the other blink without a warning. Counting it exactly needs a move to record which cell each cell came from (`CellEdit` doesn't), so that the image can take the mark of the cell it moves from on its own side (#98). The count holds until the next successful save, after which the file written has no blink left to lose; reopening a file or New resets it. The warning says nothing about the original file, which a save to a new path leaves alone. Blink with iCE off hides and shows the foreground, so on a blank glyph (a space, NUL or 0xFF) it shows nothing: neither the open (`openAsEditor`) nor `writeAnsi` counts it as lost, and `cli convert` and `cli shade` write such a file without `--allow-lossy`. `writeAnsi` doesn't trim a trailing blinking space as a blank when it writes without iCE, so a bright-black background survives an iCE-off save; with iCE on the blink is dropped and the space is trimmed like any black space, as the editor writes it. 9 px redraws the canvas, preview and zoomed view with 9-px cells; turning 9 px on for a canvas too large for the 1× cache at 9 px leaves it at 8 px with a status-bar message, and opening a file that asks for 9 px and is too large at 9 px is refused, like any canvas too large to display. None of the three has a shortcut.
- **Keyboard mode** follows Moebius:
  - F1–F10 type the glyphs of the current F-key set in the pen's colours. The sets are Moebius's 16 (from PabloDraw, `core/glyph_sets.h`), all CP437 glyphs `.ans` can hold; set 6 (░▒▓█▀▄▌▐■·) is the default. Under the Unicode typing policy (§16, Cursor and typing: the IRC target or a UTF-8 `.ans`, #350 slice 2) five Unicode sets follow them, each glyph narrow by the width table and drawn by Unifont where CP437 has no code for it: 17 the quadrants ▖▗▘▝▚▞▙▛▜▟, 18 the lower eighths ▁▂▃▄▅▆▇█ with ▔▕, 19 the left eighths ▏▎▍▌▋▊▉█ with ▐▕, 20 the wedges ◢◣◤◥, ◆◇○● and ★☆, 21 the sparkles ✦✧, the stars ✶✷✸✹⋆, the asterisks ✱✺ and ❖ (#399, Decisions). A set has no name but its number and glyphs: the Glyphs menu and the glyph bar's list read "Set 21: ✦✧✶✷✸✹⋆✱✺❖" and the status bar "F-keys set 21: ✦ ✧ ✶ ✷ ✸ ✹ ⋆ ✱ ✺ ❖". A new set goes after the last, so a stored set index and the `glyphSet<N>` names kept keys use keep their meaning. Ctrl+, and Ctrl+. step to the previous and next set (wrapping), Ctrl+/ goes back to the default, and Ctrl+Shift+F1–F10 pick sets 1–10 (Shift+F1 is Help > What's This?, §11; Shift+F2–F10 type like F2–F10); sets 11–16 (and 17–21) have no direct chord and are picked from the Glyphs menu, which lists the sets the policy offers, or by stepping, which wraps within them. The status bar shows the current set and its tooltip the keys. The current set is kept between runs (`glyphSet/index`, 0–20); a stored value that is not a whole number in range reads as set 6, not the nearest set (#307), and so does a Unicode set while the policy is CP437 (an ANSI document saved as CP437), which keeps the stored value, so the Unicode set comes back with an IRC or UTF-8 document. Sets 17–21 stay actions of the app while the policy hides them, by the maintainer's ruling (#400): the Glyphs menu leaves them out, but `--list-actions`, Preferences > Shortcuts and the keys kept at startup include them, and a key the user gives one is kept (Quick tool keys, §11).
  - The direct chords deviate from Moebius on purpose. Moebius uses Alt+F1–F10 and Alt+Shift+F-keys, but desktops take those before the editor sees them: Alt+F4 closes the window on GNOME, KDE Plasma and Xfce, and Alt+F2/F5–F8/F10 are GNOME window commands (Alt+F6–F11 on Xfce, Alt+F3 on KDE). Ctrl+F1–F4 switch desktops on KDE (Ctrl+F1–F12 on Xfce), and Ctrl+Alt+F-keys switch Linux VTs. None of the three desktops binds Ctrl+Shift+F-keys by default. On macOS Qt maps Ctrl to Cmd, so the chord is Cmd+Shift+F-key (with fn on keyboards whose top row defaults to media keys); macOS binds Control+Shift+F6 but no Cmd+Shift+F-key. A test keeps every shortcut off Alt+F-keys and bare Ctrl+F-keys.
  - The palette dock shows the 16 colours and the pen: a left click picks the foreground, a right click the background. Ctrl+Up/Down step the foreground, Ctrl+Left/Right the background (wrapping), Ctrl+D resets the pen to grey on black. The pen is kept between runs (`pen/foreground`, `pen/background`): a palette colour as its index 0–15, a 24-bit colour the eyedropper took as `#rrggbb`, so it comes back as it was rather than as the palette colour nearest it. Anything else stored (an index out of range, a malformed `#` value) reads as that colour's default, grey or black, not the nearest colour. Nothing is changed for iCE: a bright background comes back bright into an iCE-off document, as a pen picked there stays bright, and draws the same cells (blink on its dark colour, as the iCE entry above says) (#307). Under the IRC target (§6) the dock shows mIRC's 99: the 16, then 16–98 as smaller swatches in rows of 12 under the pen, each picking mIRC colour n as its exact RGB (`swatchColor`, tooltip "mIRC n (#rrggbb)"), and Ctrl+arrows step through all 99 (16 under ANSI; an RGB pen steps from the swatch with its RGB, or under ANSI from the palette entry nearest it). The six mIRC colours that share an RGB with one of 0–15 (52 is 4, 54 8, 58 11, 62 13, 88 1, 98 0) export as the shorter code (#350). Under IRC the dock also has B, I and U toggles under the palette (#350 slice 3a): the pen's formatting (`Pen::attrs`), which the glyph brush and typing (keys, the F-key sets and input-method commits; #350 slice 3b) write with the pen's colours on a cells layer (not on a Pixels layer, and not under ANSI, where the toggles hide and the pen keeps its formatting for when the target is IRC again), and which the eyedropper's left button picks from the cell under it. It is not kept between runs. The half-block tools, fill, shade and colorize write colours, not glyphs, and leave a cell's formatting alone. A mark typed onto a cluster keeps that cell's formatting, and Backspace and Delete clear to a blank without any.
  - Typing on the last column leaves the cursor there, past the glyph: Backspace then clears that cell instead of the one to its left, and any move ends it.
  - A key is text when it gives printable text, unless Ctrl or Meta is held without Alt. So AltGr (Ctrl+Alt on Windows) and macOS Option characters type, and Ctrl/Cmd chords stay shortcuts.
- **Tools:** the canvas view takes Alt+left-drag (mouse or pen) for moving the active layer (§5), and a plain left drag too while a tool whose `movesLayer()` is true (the Move/Transform tool) is active, before handing other mouse and key events (in cells) to the active tool; whatever it doesn't take goes on to keyboard mode. A `Tool` (`app/tool.h`) has an id and a name, `activate`/`deactivate`, `mousePress`/`mouseMove`/`mouseRelease`/`keyPress` returning whether it took the event, `cancel` (drop what is in progress without an edit), `cancelDrag` (end a drag the mouse can no longer finish, without an edit), `dragging` (whether a drag is in progress), `settingsChanged` (the view's mirror mode or selection changed: a drag in progress redoes its edit and preview) and `finish` (write it as an edit), `paintCells` for cells in progress (drawn over the canvas, under the reference layers and the guides; `layerCells` gives the active layer's cells it draws changed, which cover the reference layers under it as the edit will), `paint` for its marks (over everything but the cell cursor) and a `changed` signal. It edits through `CanvasView::applyEdit`, so every edit is one undo command and mirror mode applies to it; a tool that has already mirrored its edit (the drawing tools) passes `mirrored` so the view doesn't mirror it again, and `CanvasView::mirrored` gives a tool the view's mirror of an edit for its preview. The view calls `cancel` when the document changes under the tool (an undo or redo that changes cells or the active layer's cells, an edit the view didn't push, a new canvas); a layer step or an iCE switch that leaves the active layer, its cells and its place as they were (a hide or a show, a mode or a style, another layer's text, move, add or delete), or its undo or redo, leaves the tool as it was (`Document::cellsKept` while `cellsChanged` is emitted). It calls `cancelDrag` when it loses focus during a drag the tool took, a popup opening included: a floating selection being dragged goes back to where the drag picked it up and stays floating, and a new selection rectangle ends as far as it got. A popup that opens during a mouse drag (the mouse's or the pen's synthesized mouse events) ends the drag at once, as focus loss does, with no edit: once the popup has had a mouse event Qt no longer sends the drag's release to the canvas, so the drag can't be carried on after it closes. A stylus stroke driven by tablet events goes on: Qt sends the pen's tablet events to the canvas, popup or not, so its own release ends it. That holds for a menu opened from the keyboard too: Alt+F takes the focus for the popup and then for the menu bar, and neither ends the stroke (#47), nor does Alt tapped alone, which takes it for the menu bar; a mouse drag ends at the first. While the menu bar has the focus the canvas sees no focus-out, so the view watches the application's focus: the focus going anywhere but back to the canvas, the menu bar or a menu (a window switch, a dialog, another widget) ends the stroke with no edit, and the window passes the undo key to the view, which ends the stroke and undoes nothing else. A floating selection with no drag in progress stays, so the menus can still act on it. The undo key during a selection drag ends it the same way and undoes nothing, as it does for the drawing tools. It calls `finish` before an edit of its own (typing, Backspace, Delete, F-keys), which then lands on top, and the window calls it before Save and Save As write and before the unsaved-changes check that New, Open and closing the window make. So a floating selection is stamped before any save or unsaved-changes check (one that can't land refuses the save instead, §4 Selections), and stays stamped (an ordinary undoable edit) if the user then backs out: Cancel on the unsaved-changes prompt, the Open dialog or the lossy-save warning, or a lossy save that is refused. A paint that throws leaves the rest of that paint out (the canvas too, if the renderer threw) rather than escaping `paintEvent`; the cell cursor is still drawn. A `ToolRegistry` holds the tools and the active one; the Tools menu is built from it (Keyboard Ctrl+K, Select Ctrl+E, Move/Transform Ctrl+Shift+J, and the drawing tools below). A `ToolEvent` carries the cell and, for the pixel tools, the half-block row under the pointer (`halfRow`, twice the cell row plus one in a cell's bottom half). A tool's `status()` text shows in the status bar while it is active.
- **Selections** (`core/selection.h`, `app/select_tool.h`): a rectangle for now, with a mask possible later behind `Selection::contains`. The selection belongs to the view (`CanvasView::selection`, `selectionChanged`), which draws its outline, so it lasts across tool switches; a new canvas clears it. Drag to select; drag inside the selection or press an arrow to lift its cells into a floating selection, which is only drawn (as the stamp would leave the canvas, rendered at 1× and scaled as it paints) until Enter or a click stamps it as one edit; Esc ends any drag and puts it back, or deselects. A floating selection on a Pixels layer may hold ▌ ▐, which the layer can't hold (a Library stamp of them, a paste from a cells layer, a pixel lift turned a quarter): it floats and shows as it is, but every landing checks it and refuses while it still holds one, whatever the paste mode, as Under mode would otherwise skip a ▌ over a filled cell and land the lift as an erase of its source (`SelectTool::stamp`, `landingProblem`, `withSideways`, #433). The landing is also refused, for the layer's reason as the Library gives it (`Document::editProblem`: "the layer is locked", "the layer is hidden", "a live Text layer is drawn from its text", …), when the layer takes no edit: the undo or redo of a layer step (a lock, a hide, Bake Text, a Generator's Bake Layer) can leave a float on such a layer, and a lift doesn't check the layer. Before, such a float was dropped at Enter without a word. The status bar says why, "Could not place <file>: a Pixels layer holds no ▌; rotate it (Selection > Rotate) or press Esc to drop it" ("Could not put the floating selection down: …" for a paste or lift; "press Esc" alone when no turn would land it), the float stays where it is and nothing is written; a quarter turn either way makes ▌ ▐ into ▀ ▄ and lets it land, a flip or a half turn doesn't. Enter, a click on or off the float, Select All, Deselect, a new stamp or paste, a tool switch (the registry keeps the Select tool), typing and F-keys, a layer move, Save, Save As, a snapshot, Canvas Size and IRC export are refused that way (Save and the rest say "Not saved: the floating selection can't land: …" or the like); New, Open and closing the window ask, even on an unchanged document, saying Save can't keep it, and Discard drops it with the document's other changes. What can't be refused drops it with nothing written: a Layers panel step (a switch of active layer; a layer added, deleted, moved, hidden, shown, locked or unlocked; any other property change; an iCE switch that restyles a layer; not a rename, the alpha lock or a plain iCE switch, which keep it), with the status "Dropped the floating selection, which can't land: …" (the layer it floated on may not be the one it would land on after, #451), an undo or redo that changes cells or the active layer's cells (an edit under a layer that covers it, a covered layer's move), and Esc. Delete clears it as before: a lift's source is cleared, as one undo step, on a layer that takes edits. A floating selection never outlives a change it didn't make: switching tools, typing or saving stamps it first, and so does the unsaved-changes check, so a paste on a clean canvas counts as an unsaved change; an undo or redo that changes cells, or a new canvas, cancels it, leaving the canvas as the undo left it and the selection on the cells it was lifted from (or none, for a paste); so does the undo or redo of a layer step that changes the active layer (an add, a delete) or changes its cells where the composite doesn't show it (Bake Layer of a layer style, an edit under a covering layer, a covered layer's move: `Document::activeCellsReplaced`). The undo or redo of a layer step that leaves the active layer and its cells as they were (a lock, a hide, Bake Text, a Generator's Bake Layer, another layer's move) keeps it, and its landing is checked. Ctrl+X/C/V cut, copy and paste through the tool's own clipboard (cells with colours; nothing goes to the system clipboard); a paste floats at the cell cursor. With nothing floating, Copy and Cut take the active layer's cells (`layerCanvas()`, §5 Editing): another layer's cells are left out and a clear cell stays clear, unlike Save to Library, which takes what the visible layers show (§12 Sources, #434); with a selection floating they take the float. Delete clears the selection (in keyboard mode it still clears the cell). The Selection menu has Select All (Ctrl+A), Deselect (Ctrl+Shift+A), Flip Horizontally/Vertically (Ctrl+Shift+H/V), Rotate Clockwise/Counterclockwise (Ctrl+R, Ctrl+Shift+R) and the paste mode: normal; transparent (the block's blank cells, a space or NUL on black without blink, don't write); under (only the canvas's blank cells are written). Flips remap the glyphs that face one way (▀↔▄, ▌↔▐, box corners and tees, slashes, brackets, arrows); rotation turns half blocks and box drawing a quarter turn (▀→▐→▄→▌) and keeps the selection's top-left corner. Glyphs that only flip turn in 4-cycles that make a half turn equal both flips for all 256 CP437 glyphs, and four turns the identity: brackets ( [ ) ], < « > », { ≤ } ≥, and ¬ ⌡ ⌐ ⌠ (« » flip sideways, ⌠ ⌡ upside down). For the three bracket cycles a turn and a flip don't commute as they would on a picture (Rotate Clockwise then Flip Horizontally makes ( a ], Flip Horizontally then Rotate Counterclockwise makes it a [), and no mapping can fix that: a vertical flip leaves those glyphs alone, so their half turn is just the sideways flip. Undo restores cells, not the selection.
- **Drawing tools** (`app/draw_tools.h`, Phase 2), in the Tools menu: Half-Block Brush (Ctrl+B), Shade Brush (Ctrl+Shift+B), Ramp Shade Brush (Ctrl+T), Form Brush (Ctrl+Shift+T), Soft Brush (Ctrl+Shift+N), Despeckle Brush (Ctrl+Shift+D), Glyph Brush (Ctrl+G), Colorize Brush (Ctrl+Shift+C), Line (Ctrl+L), Rectangle (Ctrl+U), Ellipse (Ctrl+Shift+O), Fill (Ctrl+J), Eyedropper (Ctrl+I), Rim Light Brush (Ctrl+Shift+L), Texture Stamp (Ctrl+Shift+P), Clone Stamp (Ctrl+Shift+K) and Glyph Spray (Ctrl+Shift+Y), with Filled Shapes (Ctrl+Shift+F) toggling rectangle and ellipse between outline and filled. The same tools sit on a Tools toolbar. Smaller Brush (Ctrl+[) and Larger Brush (Ctrl+]) step the brush size a cell. Ctrl+Shift+U is left alone because IBus takes it for Unicode entry, and Ctrl+F is kept for Find; no two actions share a shortcut (a test checks it). Every one is a drag: it is previewed over the canvas and lands on release as one undo step, and the preview is the edit that lands: toggling mirror mode or Filled Shapes, or changing the selection, mid-drag redoes the preview at once. Esc drops it, and so does the undo key (Ctrl+Z) pressed during the drag, which undoes nothing else, even when there is nothing to undo; any undo or redo (from the menu or the stack), a format change (iCE, 9 px) and its undo or redo also drop it; switching tools lands it. Fill floods once, from where it is pressed; the release point doesn't move it. The preview renders only the cells the edit changes, and a filled shape recomputes only the shape on each move. The left button draws with the pen's foreground, the right with its background; the shade brush builds up one rung a pass with the left button and takes one down with the right (a space, ░, ▒, ▓ or █ moves, written in the pen's colours both ways, as Moebius does; text, ▀, ▄ and every other glyph is left alone, both ways), the glyph brush writes its glyph with the left and erases to a blank cell with the right (a transparent cell above the bottom layer, §5; the alpha lock keeps it off opaque cells), and the colorize brush sets the foreground with the left and the background with the right, keeping the glyphs. The glyph brush's glyph is █ until F1–F10 pick one from the current F-key set while it is active; the status bar shows it. The shade, ramp shade, form, soft, despeckle, glyph and colorize brushes paint with the Brush dock's settings (`BrushDock`, on the view like the pen): size (1–256 cells), square or round tip, strength, what a stylus's pressure scales (nothing, size or strength; size by default, which a mouse, always at full pressure, never changes), the ramp the ramp brushes walk (the named ramps, steel by default) and the alpha lock; the rim-light brush and the texture stamp walk the same ramp. The dock also holds the light direction (eight compass points, NW by default), one setting that lights the rim-light, form and texture brushes, the texture stamp's kind, density and seed, and for the shading brushes (§10) the form (dome, cylinder, plane), the form's rim light (off, labelled Form rim light so it isn't taken for the Rim Light brush) and glint (on), and the soft brush's hardness (0%); then the symmetry (off, mirror X, mirror Y, mirror X and Y, radial with 2–8 folds about the canvas centre or a set cell, its spins limited to the canvas and pulled in when a smaller canvas opens) and the pencil's 4-connected toggle. The half-block brush stays a one-pixel pencil. The ramp shade brush lightens along the ramp with the left button and darkens with the right; strength is the share of a whole colour (four rungs) a pass moves, rounded and never below a rung, so the default of 25% is one rung a pass and 100% a colour. Changing any of these mid-drag redoes the preview; the brush keeps the pen the drag was pressed with, so a pen change mid-stroke waits for the next one. A stylus drives the tool through `QTabletEvent` with its pressure in the `ToolEvent`; an event the tool doesn't take is left unaccepted, so Qt's synthesized mouse event handles it, and a release (which reads no pressure) lands with the stroke's last pressure; whether an event is a release is the view's call (below), not Qt's. The view decides the type and the button of every stylus tablet event by its own record of the pen's buttons (those of the pen's last tablet event at the view), not by Qt's, which an event the canvas takes through a popup leaves as it was: a release under a popup leaves the button held, so that Qt sends the next press as a move or as a press of the button that was let go, and a press under a popup comes again with the next event. A button the event holds that the record doesn't is pressed, one the record holds that the event doesn't was let go, and one both hold is held on. A button let go was let go at the event only if Qt says the event is its release; otherwise (Qt saw it go up elsewhere, or the event doesn't show) it was let go where the view last saw the pen. The view also keeps what it last knew of Qt's record, and whether Qt's tablet grab is its own (a press Qt sends it takes the grab, a release of the last button lets it go): with the grab and a popup open, an event comes through the popup, and Qt's record takes the buttons of every event but one the canvas takes that way. One no widget takes through the popup Qt passes again through the canvas's window, and so to the canvas, which sees it twice; the second time it is at most a button the tool declined, let go and pressed again, which the tool declines again. A button both records hold that Qt says is pressed went up out of view, so it was let go where the view last saw the pen, and is pressed again. An event Qt has the wrong type or button for that the tool doesn't take is handled by the view as the mouse event with the right ones would be (a press moves the cell cursor) and accepted, so Qt makes no mouse event of it with the wrong button; any other event the tool doesn't take is left unaccepted. A press Qt sent as a move sets no tablet grab, so its release can go to another widget; a button let go out of view lands the stylus's drag where the view last saw the pen. The view has tablet tracking on, so that happens as soon as the pen hovers back over the canvas, or at the latest when it presses on it again. The device whose press the tool took owns the drag until the drag ends, however it ends (a release, Esc, a lost focus) and whatever buttons are still held: the stylus's tablet events, the mouse's events, or the mouse events Qt synthesizes from the stylus (a mouse event from a stylus device), when it was one of those the tool took. Another device's presses, moves and releases are ignored, and mouse events from the stylus during a tablet drag neither restart it nor add to it; during a drag the stylus's synthesized events drive, its tablet events stay unaccepted so Qt goes on making them. A drag the mouse's events drive (the mouse's, or the pen's synthesized ones) keeps the other device's mouse events from every window until it ends, however it ends: the view filters the application's events for the drag and drops them before Qt routes them. Qt sends a press's moves and release to the widget pressed only until a release with no buttons held, and another device's release is one, so without the filter the owner's later moves and release would reach no widget and the drag would stay live with no edit (#45). So another device's click elsewhere in the application reaches no widget, rather than being taken by the widget under it. The view takes no mouse grab (`QWidget::grabMouse`) for this: a grab is the window system's too, which Wayland grants only to a popup, warning at every grab and release (#101), and the owner's moves and release out of the view or out of the window reach the canvas without one, by Qt's record of the press and the window system's implicit grab on it. So a popup that opens during a drag keeps its own pointer grab, and a click outside still closes it. The filter follows the tool's drag wherever the drag ends: the view syncs it on every `Tool::changed`, which a drag ending emits, so a menu action that ends a drag (Paste, Cut, Delete, Select All, Deselect, a Library stamp, undo or redo, a new canvas) stops it too. A view that isn't shown filters nothing. A drag driven by tablet events needs no filter: Qt sends the pen's tablet events to the widget its press went to, whatever the mouse does. Selections clip every tool: the view's selection becomes the stroke's mask (`selectionMask` in `brush.h`).
- **Half-block pixels** (`core/halfblock.h`, `core/shapes.h`): the half-block brush, line, rectangle, ellipse, fill and eyedropper work on pixels two to a cell. A cell holds pixels when it is a space, █, ▀ or ▄; plotting into any other cell replaces it, its background colour filling the other half. Cells are written in one canonical way: one colour is a space on a dark background or █ on black for a bright one, two colours are ▀ unless only ▄ keeps the background dark; two bright colours need a bright background (iCE). Every tool reads a cell by the colours it shows: with iCE off a bright background shows as its dark colour (and blinks), so the halves, the fill's colour test and the eyedropper all see the dark one. With iCE off a write puts the bright colour in the foreground, and when both halves would be different bright colours the half already there keeps its colour and the one being painted shows as its dark counterpart (only one bright fits; painting both halves of a cell gives the colour itself). A pixel write keeps the cell's other attributes (Blink included) and makes a transparent cell opaque; locked cells are never written. The pencil joins its points with 4-connected lines, or with 8-connected ones (`line8`, one pixel per step of the longer axis) when the Brush dock's Pencil 4-connected is off. A line is drawn the same whichever end it starts from (its ends are ordered top to bottom first). An ellipse is the box's pixels whose centres lie within the ellipse through the box's outer pixel centres, each radius r tuned to sqrt(r(r + 1)) so small circles read round (5 × 5 gives rows 3, 5, 5, 5, 3), and its outline is the filled pixels with a neighbour (of eight) outside, which is 4-connected. Fill floods the 4-connected pixels of one colour, through pixel cells only, so text and shades bound it. The eyedropper picks the half's colour in a pixel cell and otherwise the cell's foreground (left button) or background (right) into the pen.
- **Mirror mode** (Edit > Mirror Mode, Ctrl+M) turns symmetry on; Edit > Symmetry and the Brush dock pick what it repeats a stroke as: Mirror X (the default), Mirror Y, Mirror X and Y, or Radial (§10). Picking a mode turns mirror mode on, and Off in the dock turns it off; the mode is kept while it is off. The drawing tools repeat what they paint through the core's stroke options (`StrokeOptions::symmetry`), so the preview, the selection, the lock and the undo step treat the images as part of the stroke. The brushes repeat their dabs: each image is painted from the image cell's own value, with the tip and the glyph flipped as the image is, so the shade brush steps the image cell's own level, the colorize brush recolours the image cell's own glyph, and the glyph brush writes its glyph flipped (▌ as ▐ under mirror X). The shading brushes (§10) go through the same images: the form brush with nothing selected shades each image as its own form, from the cells that image has covered; the soft brush bands the cells all the images have covered as one shape, so images far apart band as the stroke alone would; the despeckle brush cleans each image's own cells. The pixel tools (half-block brush, line, rectangle, ellipse, fill) repeat pixels, not cells: an image pixel goes into its cell's own half and the other half stays as it was, and fill paints the images of the region it flooded. The selection and the lock clip each image on its own, so a stroke outside the selection still paints its image inside it. Edits made outside the drawing tools (typing, Backspace, Delete, paste) are a transform of the edit as before: in mirror X a change at column x is repeated at width − 1 − x with its glyph flipped horizontally, where the edit's own change wins on the mirrored cell; in the other modes they are not repeated.
- **Guides** are a view overlay, never saved: View > Guides draws a line after column 80 and every 25, 40 or 50 rows. **Reference images** are reference layers (§5), saved with the project (§6): File > Open Reference Image… adds one showing a picture (a PNG or JPEG kept as it is, any other allowed format kept as a PNG of it as decoded, so at most 2^24 px) across the canvas width at its own aspect (its full size's, however it was decoded), top-left on the canvas's, at 50 % opacity, on top of the stack and made active, one undo step; Layer > Reference Opacity sets the active one's (25–100 %, an undo step), and its eye in the Layers dock hides it. The view draws each visible one, bottom first, in its place in the stack: over the art under it and under the visible art layers above it, which cover it by half cells (`coverageAbove` in `core/composite.h`: an opaque cell of a cells, text or generator layer covers the whole cell in any mode, a pixel layer the halves it sets but none of a text cell, which its pixels never cover, a styled layer by its styled cells, and another reference layer nothing), so a reference raised to the top is over all the art and one at the bottom shows only where nothing is drawn; a tool's cells in progress cover the ones under the active layer where they will once it lands, by half cells, and are under the ones above it, and an art layer being moved covers them where the drag has it. It is drawn at its place and size in canvas pixels (a canvas pixel is `zoom` view pixels), from a copy of the whole picture scaled to the size it shows at (averaged when reduced, read and converted at most 2^20 px at a time, a row wider than that in pieces; interpolated when enlarged). The copies of all the visible ones are at most 2^24 px together, shared evenly, each drawn scaled up past its share; a copy too thin to keep its aspect stays 1 px on its short side and its long side is cut to its share, so the cap holds after rounding. Each picture is retained once however many layers show it, at most 2^24 px, and only while a visible layer shows it; Open Reference Image hands its decoded copy to the view. Before any decoding, the header must give a valid size. Handlers with native scaled decoding (JPEG) decode scaled toward that budget. Other handlers (including PNG and BMP) decode in full only when the header is at most 2^26 px, then shrink to at most 2^24 px; larger pictures are refused before decoding. PNG, JPEG and BMP headers bound their decoded raster. The format is the one Qt detects in the bytes, never the file suffix, and only an allowlist (`referenceDecode` in `app/reference_scale.cpp`) is read at all: GIF, WebP, TIFF, ICO, PBM, PGM, PPM, XBM and XPM, whose handlers were each checked on Qt 6.12 to fail a 1024×1024 picture under a 1 MiB allocation limit (the tests decode a small and an oversized picture in each format Qt can also write, all of them but GIF), run under a Qt allocation cap of 256 MiB: Qt accounts for at least 4 bytes per pixel, so a raster one of these handlers decodes, an ICO's embedded PNG included, cannot exceed 2^26 px even when the outer header understates its size. Detecting the format runs under the cap too, since it runs every handler's detector on the bytes and XBM's decodes the whole picture (the others in Qt 6.12 read a header or magic bytes). Every other format is refused like an oversized picture, with a message naming it: PDF (its plugin decodes past the limit), SVG and SVGZ (an `<image>` data URL inside is decoded through `QImage::fromData` in whatever format its bytes are, PDF included, past the allowlist, so the cap bounds only the outer raster), ICNS (its handler reports no size, so there is none to check before decoding), anything unrecognised, and any format a plugin adds later until its handler is checked the same way. Vector references come back only once nested decoding can be bounded as a whole. Setting the limit and reading it back proves nothing about a handler. Higher-depth images can reach that byte cap below the pixel ceiling. A stricter caller limit is kept; reference reads are serialized and restore the previous process-wide limit on every exit. If QT_IMAGEIO_MAXALLOC prevents enforcing the cap, those formats are refused, and so are bytes the XBM detector might decode (a `#define` in the first 4 KiB), before detection; small containers remain supported when the cap can be enforced. Import and project display use the same guard. Import reports a refusal in the status bar; a project may still hold those bytes, but the view skips the picture, caches the failure and reports it in the status bar. Qt's own allocation limit may refuse a smaller picture too. If a copy can't be made the status bar says so and it isn't retried until the zoom, the layer's size or its picture changes. Tools don't paint a reference layer. Alt+drag, or a plain drag with the Move/Transform tool (Ctrl+Shift+J), moves one by canvas pixels; with the Move/Transform tool, a drag from one of the four corner handles it draws around the active visible reference resizes it from the opposite corner, keeping its aspect unless Shift is held (Shift going down or up mid-drag redraws the frame at once, so a release lands what is shown; each side at least 1 px, at most 2^24). The drag previews live by drawing the cached copy stretched to the new frame, shows dx/dy or the new size in the status bar, and commits one "Move Layer" or "Resize Layer" step, or none if nothing changed; Esc drops it. The tool's Tool Options give X, Y, Width and Height in canvas pixels, Keep aspect (on by default: a new width or height holds one aspect, the picture's own when the size is that to the pixel and the size's own otherwise; a scale sets it to the picture's own whether or not it moves a pixel, and any other change, a free edit, a drag, undo or redo, or another layer, works it out again from the size there is, so kept edits there and back land where they began; a kept edit that would take the other side past 2^24 px is cut so the aspect holds) and Scale (a percentage of the picture's own size, to two decimals from 0.01 %, rotated by its EXIF orientation, each scale sizing both sides from the picture's own, both kept under 2^24 px and at least 1 px; the scale shown is never past the one that puts the long side at 2^24 px, and is the one asked for while it still gives the size there is, so arrow steps that round to the same size add up, else the roundest that gives it, mid-way through the scales that do, else, when no scale to two decimals gives it (a size kept below what 0.01 % gives, a long side between two hundredths, a stretched size), the nearest, marked "≈"; arrow steps work from the size, not the number: a step adds the step to the number shown, and where that would move a side the wrong way, or from a "≈" number move none, it goes on to the first scale whose size has every side on the step's side of this one's and one past it, shown as that size's, so a step down never grows a side and one up never shrinks one, the first step from a stretched size taking the picture's own aspect back at the largest size inside it or the smallest around it; where the box's end that way, the 2^24 px limit or 0.01 %, gives no such size, the arrow is off and a step does nothing); each change is one undo step, and a locked, hidden or empty reference disables them with a note saying why.
- **Library implementation notes** (Phase 2c.2; the design is §12): `core/` `library.h` reads, writes and checks stamps; `app/library_dock.h` is the dock. A stamp is `<slug>.ans` (at most 400 × 5000 cells, as `.ans` parses; SAUCE title = its name: 1–35 CP437 bytes, no NUL, not ending in a space, checked when written and when read; 0x01–0x1F are glyphs here, ☺ ♥ …) (read as CP437, never by detection, §8; a user-library stamp CP437 can't hold is UTF-8, below) beside `<slug>.json`, which holds exactly `{"category": "...", "tags": ["...", ...], "ircBytes": N}`, and `"encoding": "utf-8"` after them for a UTF-8 stamp (an `"insets"` key before that makes a stamp resizable, §12 Resizable stamps): a non-empty category; tags may be none, but no tag is empty or holds a comma; neither holds a control character; N is the IRC bytes of the widest row as `irc::encodeCanvas` encodes the `.ans` as it parses. Any other key, a key given twice, a wrong type, an N that disagrees with the art, or art whose cells are all blank is refused. Libraries are directories scanned recursively; an art file is any `.ans` with the extension's ASCII case folded (`X.ANS` too), paired with `X.json` or, failing that, an `X.json` in any case, the same rule Save to Library's slots use; a symbolic link to a file is read through, one to a folder is not followed. That rule keeps a link from being followed by accident; it is not a security boundary (a folder swapped for a link between being listed and opened is followed, and whoever can do that inside the user's library can already write stamps there). Files are read through the opened handle: on POSIX it is opened non-blocking and refused unless `fstat` says regular file, so a FIFO swapped in after a path check can't block the reader. A `.ans` without a sidecar, a sidecar without a `.ans`, a stamp that fails the check, a broken link, a link to a folder, a folder that can't be read, and a `.ans` or `.json` that isn't a regular file (a FIFO, a device; never opened) are listed as problems (a file in a folder that can be listed but not searched is "can't be read") (`library list` and the dock's status show them as warnings, `library check` fails on them). The bundled library is `<prefix>/share/blockwright/library/` (found from the binary's directory; the repo's `library/` installs there). The user library is `QStandardPaths::GenericDataLocation` + `/Blockwright/library`, the same for the GUI and the CLI: on Linux `~/.local/share/Blockwright/library`, which on Linux and macOS is the GUI's `AppDataLocation` + `/library`; on Windows it is under `AppData/Local`, not the roaming `AppData`. `BLOCKWRIGHT_BUNDLED_LIBRARY` and `BLOCKWRIGHT_USER_LIBRARY` override them. Placing a stamp (double-click, Enter, or a drop on the canvas) stamps whatever already floats, in its own mode, and floats the stamp's cells at the cell cursor or the drop cell through the paste path in transparent mode for that float only (`SelectTool::floatBlock` with a mode): the Paste Mode setting is not changed, the Selection menu shows the float's mode while it floats, and a Paste Mode pick during the float applies to it and becomes the setting. Selection > Save to Library… writes the selection, or the floating selection, without stamping it, with the document's iCE (cells as `Document::encode` writes them), in CP437 when CP437 holds every cell as it is and otherwise in UTF-8 (below), and refuses cells neither can hold (a control character, a wide glyph with no continuation, a lone mark), blink it would lose, and an all-blank block. The file is `<user library>/<slug>.ans`, the slug being the name's ASCII letters and digits (lower case, `-` between runs, at most 48), or, for a name with none, its glyphs' code points (`♥` is `u2665`); of `<slug>.ans`, `<slug>-2.ans`, `<slug>-3.ans`, … the one holding a stamp with the same title is replaced, after asking; otherwise the lowest one with no `.ans` or `.json` of any kind is used, so a name never replaces another title and is never saved twice. File names are compared with ASCII case folded (on a case-insensitive volume `OAK-TREE.ans` is `oak-tree.ans`; a same-titled one is replaced under its own name), and the chosen new name is checked to be free just before it is used. A library folder that can't be listed is refused rather than taken as empty. Paths stay native (`std::filesystem::path`) from the scan to the write, and the dock identifies a stamp, in lookups and in its drag payload, by its path's native bytes; a drop is placed only if its payload is the key of a stamp in the current scan (it is never decoded into a path, since any application can offer the MIME type), so file and folder names that aren't UTF-8 are written as themselves and never mistaken for one another. Both files are written to temporaries first and then renamed into place, sidecar first, so a failure leaves the old stamp as it was (if the last rename fails the old sidecar is put back, and the error says if that failed too); a `.ans` or `.json` that is a symbolic link, or anything but a regular file, is refused rather than written through. A replaced file keeps its permission bits (0777 only: set-user-ID, set-group-ID and sticky are not kept; a new file gets 0666 less the umask), and a write that fails part way removes its temporary file and closes its descriptor. Thumbnails are `renderCanvas` at 1×; one that doesn't fit the 96×64 icon box is scaled down by averaging the 1× pixels under each icon pixel, rendered a band of rows (about 2^22 px) at a time so a large stamp's whole 1× image is never held. Only the icon is cached, in memory, keyed by the cells and glyph width it draws, and a rescan drops the icons of stamps that changed or are gone. `blockwright-cli library list [dir]` prints one entry a line, tab-separated: kind (`stamp` or `text-style`, §15), category, name, width x height, widest IRC row in bytes, `ice` for a text style that needs iCE colours (`-` otherwise), tags (comma-separated, which is unambiguous because a tag can't hold a comma), path; with no dir it lists the bundled then the user library, each library's stamps then its text styles, and problems are warnings (exit 0). `blockwright-cli library check <dir>` reports every problem, and any stamp whose widest row is over the 407-byte budget as WeeChat's ascii script sends it (`irc::txtSentBytes`, as Export measures it; the sidecar's `ircBytes` stays the size as written, and a row written over the budget is reported too), and exits 1 if there was one. The bundled set (Phase 2c.3) is `library/<category>/<slug>.ans` beside its `.json`, in eleven categories (buildings, fire, frames, land, plants, sky, smoke, symbols, things, trees, water), every stamp signed funkpower; a test holds each one to the IRC budget and to the shape rule: trimmed to its part (a drawn cell on every edge) and, unless it is a frame, at least one blank cell on its outer ring, so it is never a filled rectangle; a frame instead has an opening: its centre cell is blank, and the blank region joined to it stays inside the ring and covers at least half of the cells within it. A stamp drawn only in black (a silhouette) is tagged `silhouette` and `dark`, since its thumbnail on black shows nothing. **UTF-8 stamps** (#336 slice 2): a stamp is CP437, byte for byte as before #336, when CP437 holds every cell as it is: nothing `writeAnsi` lists as unwritable and no cluster, not even one CP437 composes to a byte (e + U+0301 written as é would read back as U+00E9). Otherwise `encodeStamp` writes it as UTF-8, always starting with a byte order mark (`writeAnsi`'s `bom`), and its sidecar gains `"encoding": "utf-8"`, so wide glyphs (a lead and its continuation) and clusters read back as they were saved. `readStamp` reads a stamp as UTF-8 only when its `.ans` starts with the mark and its sidecar says `utf-8`; a sidecar that says so over an `.ans` with no mark is a problem, and so is an `.ans` that starts with the mark under a sidecar that doesn't (`.ans starts with a byte order mark, but its sidecar has no "encoding": "utf-8"`): every CP437 stamp Blockwright writes starts with ESC, so that is a UTF-8 stamp that lost its key, and reading it as CP437 would place it misread, ∩╗┐ and all; only a hand-made file whose first glyphs are ∩╗┐ goes from read to refused, and earlier builds still read it. No stamp is read by detection, so a CP437 stamp whose bytes look like UTF-8 (Σ╕¡ is `E4 B8 AD`, 中) stays CP437. The sidecar key is what an older build trips on: it reads every stamp as CP437 (the mark as ∩╗┐ and each UTF-8 sequence as two to four glyphs) and checks `ircBytes` against that read, which can agree when the widest row is plain ASCII, so the mark alone would let it place the stamp misread; it refuses a sidecar with a key it doesn't know, so it lists a UTF-8 stamp as a problem (`sidecar has an unknown key "encoding"`) and never places it. Only the user library holds UTF-8 stamps: `bw-make-stamps` refuses to write one, and `test_library_bundled` fails on a bundled stamp with a mark, an `encoding` key, or cells that don't save again as the same CP437 file.
- **Fonts:** bundle the CP437 VGA 8×16 / 9×16 family first; Amiga fonts later once their licences are checked.
- **Build:** CMake + Qt 6.8 LTS. Qt 6.7 is the minimum (#263): the code uses Qt 6.5–6.7 APIs, and rather than carry fallbacks for Qt 6.4 the native Linux packages target only distros with Qt 6.7 or newer (Debian 13 and later, Ubuntu 26.04 and later, current Fedora and Arch); Debian 12 and Ubuntu 24.04, which have Qt 6.4, get the AppImage, which bundles its own Qt (#20). `vcpkg` or system Qt on Linux. No Rust toolchain and no submodules: the IRC encoder is C++ in `core/` (§8). CI on Forgejo Actions (`.forgejo/workflows/ci.yml`): configure, build and the whole `ctest` suite on Linux, in a container built from `.forgejo/ci/arch.Dockerfile` (current Qt 6) on a self-hosted runner, and on FreeBSD 14 with its packaged Qt 6 on a second self-hosted runner, a virtual machine that runs the job directly with no container (#288). It runs on pushes to this repository's branches, never on pull requests; maintainers' PR branches live in the repository, so a push covers them. That is not what keeps strangers' code off the runner: a fork or AGit pull request runs the workflows in its own head commit. What does is the forge's controlled registration (the maintainer decides who gets an account) plus Forgejo's approval gate for pull requests from users without write access: Forgejo holds a run for approval when neither the PR's poster nor the user whose action fired the event has write access or is marked "always trust", however many of their PRs were merged before, and a held run's jobs are never offered to a runner. The maintainer accepts that arrangement as the runner's protection. Runs that skip the gate: a PR the maintainer opens from a fork branch, the maintainer's own fork included, and every later push to that branch, by anyone; a writer's push to a fork's PR branch, the PR page's "Update branch" button included; an approved run, or one by an author marked "always trust"; and any fork or AGit PR run that is re-run, all jobs or one. Forgejo clears a run's hold whenever it cancels it, and re-run never checks approval; a held run is cancelled by Deny, by Cancel in the web UI or the API, by the PR being closed (its author can close it), by a newer run in the same concurrency group, which by default is every later push to the same PR, and by revoking trust, and none of these leaves a mark that the run was held, so every push to an open fork or AGit PR leaves a cancelled run that can be re-run. So the maintainer never re-runs a fork or AGit PR run (`is_fork_pull_request: true`) but deletes it instead, deletes a fork or AGit PR's superseded runs while it is open and all its runs once it closes, denies held runs and deletes them, and doesn't approve one, "always trust" its author, open a PR from someone else's fork branch, or push to or update a fork's PR branch (their commits are pulled into a branch of this repository instead). Each job's token can write to the repository; `main`'s branch protection refuses direct pushes, the job token's included, and PRs still merge through the API. Jobs have outbound internet access, including to the project's own public address, so a job reaches whatever that address publishes, and its traffic carries that address, so anything that trusts the address trusts a job; the maintainer accepts this. Jobs run on one fixed docker network that the dev machine, which hosts the runner, firewalls: nothing in the private, carrier-grade NAT, link-local or multicast ranges is reachable directly, no IPv6, and nothing on the dev machine itself, its published ports included. DNS goes through docker's embedded resolver, which forwards to the host's, so a job can resolve names the host can. The rules are in their own nftables table, applied by a system unit before docker starts and re-checked every five minutes. The FreeBSD machine's traffic is fenced the same way, outside it. macOS/Windows jobs are added in Phase 6. A third job, `linux-qt-min`, runs the same build and suite on the same runner against the minimum: Qt's own build of Qt 6.7.3 (aqtinstall) baked into a second image, `.forgejo/ci/qt-min.Dockerfile`, which has no Arch Qt. It is 6.7.3 and not 6.7.0 because 6.7.0 to 6.7.2 don't build with a current GCC (their `qcomparehelpers.h` defines `IsFloatType_v<_Float16>` without `inline`, so the link finds it in every object file); patch releases add no API. The job fails if `find_package(Qt6 …)` asks for another minor version than the image has, so raising the minimum means a new image.
- **Licence:** GPL-3.0-or-later. Compatible with Apache-2.0 (Moebius) and MIT/Apache (Icy Draw) if code is borrowed; Qt is LGPLv3, linked dynamically.

## 5. Layers

Modelled on Icy Draw's layer record, trimmed to what we need. In the core: `layer.h` (the model and the stack) and `composite.h` (the compositor).

| Property | Notes |
|---|---|
| id | `LayerId`, unique in the stack and never given again, so undo and the project file can name a layer across adds, removes and moves; ids read from a file are kept and new ones go above them. `nextId` only moves up and is 0 for good once the last id is taken; the manifest saves it (`reserveIds` restores it), so a removed layer's id (and its `scripts/<id>/`) isn't reused after a reload |
| name | Free text |
| kind | `cells`, `pixels`, `text` (live text in a font, with a text style: §15; a live one's cells are drawn from its source and tools don't edit them, and one without a source is cells read as text), `generator` (a program's output, §7: it composites as a cells layer from its cached output, saved in `.cells`, so a reader without the generator still composites and exports it; tools don't edit it); `reference` (a picture to trace, §4: no cells; its picture, its place and size in canvas pixels and its opacity are under `"reference"` in its JSON, §6; tools don't paint it, Alt+drag and the Move/Transform tool move and resize it in canvas pixels, the view draws it in its place in the stack, under the art layers above it, and the compositor skips it, so it is never exported) |
| offset x/y, width/height | Layers can be smaller than the canvas, run off it and be moved; the offset is in cells (a pixel-row offset for pixel layers is deferred; if it comes it is an optional field defaulting to 0, not a format break) |
| visible, locked, alpha-locked | Locked and alpha-locked are for editing (`layerPaintable`; alpha-locked is `StrokeOptions::lockTransparent`, `layerStrokeOptions`, which for a pixel layer also sets `StrokeOptions::pixelLayer` so the lock is per pixel, `layerPixelPaintable`; `fitLayerEdit` holds every edit to it); neither changes the composite |
| mode | normal, chars-only, colours-only (repaint colours without touching glyphs); pixel layers ignore it, but it is saved. The layer panel doesn't show it yet; when it does, it greys it out for pixel layers |
| opacity rule | Cells are opaque or transparent; no blending, because the output is 16 colours |
| group | Deferred: the stack is flat for now; groups, when they come, are pass-through only |

- **Model.** A `Layer` is its `LayerProperties` (what `layers/<id>.json` will hold) and a `Canvas` of its own size (what `layers/<id>.cells` will hold); a new layer is all transparent cells. A `LayerStack` is the layers bottom first on a canvas of a given size, with add (at an index, on top by default), remove, move and find by id. `LayerStack::fromCanvas` is one opaque cells layer, "Background", which is how a flat `.ans` opens. Kinds and modes have stable names for the file (`layerKindName`, `layerModeName`).
- **Cells by kind.** A cells layer's cells are ordinary cells. A text layer's are too (a grid of characters for now); TDF live text (Phase 5) will add a source property that renders into those cells, so the compositor and the `.cells` file don't change when it lands. A pixel layer stores two half-block pixels a cell, each a palette colour or transparent: an opaque cell is a pixel cell as `halfblock.h` writes it with iCE on (both halves set), a transparent ▀ holds only its top pixel and a transparent ▄ only its bottom one (`layerPixels`, `pixelLayerCell`, `setLayerPixel`). So every kind is edited, undone and saved as cells through `CellEdit`, and `.cells` stores transparent cells in full (glyph and foreground: a transparent ▀/▄ is a pixel). `toLayerEdit` turns a tool's canvas-cell edit into the layer's own cells: changes outside the layer are dropped, each `before` is the layer's own cell (not the composite the tool saw), a cell changed more than once is one change to its last value, and changes that leave it as it is are dropped, so undo restores the layer exactly. `dirtyRect` gives the canvas cells to recomposite. A tool writing a pixel layer writes with `setLayerPixel` (keeping the other half) and checks each pixel with `layerPixelPaintable`.
- **Composite** is defined bottom-up per cell, in `composite.h` only. Each cell starts as the ground (a blank cell) and takes each visible cells, text, generator or pixel layer over it in turn; a transparent cell adds nothing. A cells, text or generator layer's opaque cell replaces the cell (normal), writes its glyph over the colours under it (chars-only) or its colours and attributes under the glyph (colours-only); nothing is re-picked, so 24-bit colours pass through. A text layer's cell (normal or chars-only) makes the cell text, and no pixel layer above covers it: plot doesn't erase text, whichever is on top. Being text goes with the glyph, so a cells layer above that writes its glyph (normal or chars-only) makes it plain cells again; an opaque space in a text layer is text and blocks pixels (Phase 5's TDF renderer should write a letter's empty background as transparent cells, not spaces). A `pixels` layer replaces the halves it sets and re-picks ▀/▄ with the right fg/bg (`halfBlockCell`, the background kept dark where it can be); a half it leaves transparent shows the half under it: a pixel cell's own half, the colour a shade mostly shows (░ its background, ▓ its foreground, ▒ its darker colour on top and lighter below, the colour a reader takes a cut shade for; context-free, so a cell depends only on itself) instead of the fallback that leaves a speck, and any other cell's background. Cells under pixels are read by the colours they show under iCE. With iCE off only one bright colour fits a cell, and the pixel, on top, keeps its colour: the half under shows dark, a shade's background (always dark with iCE off) or a pixel half's dark counterpart; two bright pixels of one layer keep the top's. This is the opposite of the half-block tools' rule (§4: the half already in the cell keeps its colour) on purpose: across layers the upper layer is in front, so its shape keeps its colours wherever it is moved. A half-covered cell keeps the under cell's blink only where the half under was blinking (it showed the foreground, a colour other than the background's) and keeps its colour: that half stays the foreground, written ▀ or ▄ as needed, unless the pixel is bright with iCE off and has to be; a half under that showed the background doesn't start to blink. A half-covered 24-bit cell is quantised, as the half-block tools do. After the cells are composited one by one, one pass shows a wide pair only where both its halves do, the half that shows otherwise a space (§16).
- **Incremental.** A composited cell depends only on the layers' cells at that cell and the cells either side of it (a wide pair shows only where both its halves do, §16), so `recomposite(stack, out, rect)`, which takes in a column each side of the rect, brings a composite up to date after an edit (its `dirtyRect`), a move (both bounds, united clipped to the canvas, `unite(a, b, canvas)`, so a far offset loses nothing), or a change of visibility, mode or place in the stack (the layer's bounds). 160 × 100 with 8 layers composites in about 1 ms on the dev machine, an 8 × 8 rect in about 4 µs.
- **Editing (Phase 3b).** The document (`app/document.h`) holds the stack and one active layer; `canvas()` is the composite, what the views draw and `save()` flattens to `.ans`, and `layerCanvas()` is the active layer's cells at their place on a canvas-sized grid, transparent round it (`layerCanvas` in the core), which is what every tool reads and builds its edit on. `Document::edit` fits the edit to the layer (`fitLayerEdit`: `toLayerEdit`, then every transparent cell of a cells or text layer stored as the plain `transparentCell()` unless it holds a half pixel (below), a pixel layer's cells stored as their pixels, and the alpha lock applied) and pushes it as one command holding the layer's delta; the composite is brought up to date over the cells it changed and only the cells whose composite changed are repainted. The tool previews show what the composite would become (`previewEdit`). An edit is refused, and nothing pushed, when the active layer is hidden or locked, or is a pixel layer and the edit writes a cell that isn't pixels (typing, a shade, a glyph other than █ ▀ ▄ and space, a half block on its side). Erasing (Backspace, Delete, the glyph brush's right button, clearing or moving a selection) leaves a blank cell on the bottom layer when it is a cells layer, so a flat `.ans` stays opaque, and a transparent cell anywhere else, so the layer beneath shows (`Document::blankCell`). A pixel layer's tools write single pixels (`StrokeOptions::pixelLayer`): a pixel painted into a transparent cell leaves the other half transparent, the alpha lock is per pixel, and fill takes the transparent pixels as a region of their own. The alpha lock freezes what is opaque both ways, for every edit: no transparent cell or pixel is painted and no opaque cell or set pixel is cleared, whether by a brush (`Stroke::set`, `Stroke::paintable`), a half-block tool (`PixelPainter`), clearing, cutting, moving or pasting a selection, typing, Backspace or Delete. `fitLayerEdit` drops each change that would make a cell of the locked layer opaque or transparent (on a pixel layer, change which of its pixels are set), so an edit lands on the cells it may change and nothing else; one left with no change is not pushed. A paste is clipped that way: it lands on the opaque cells and not on the clear ones. A move is not: moving, flipping or turning a selection lifts and stamps it as one edit, and one that would clear a cell of a locked layer (or a set pixel) is refused whole, since the lock would keep what it lifts and drop what lands on clear cells (`B C .` moved right would become `B B .`). Nothing changes and the selection stays on the cells it was lifted from. On the bottom cells layer a lift leaves blanks, which are opaque, so a move there goes through. Erasing on the bottom cells layer writes a blank, which is opaque, so it still erases there. A copied block keeps a pixel layer's half pixels (a transparent cell with a pixel is not a hole when pasted), and the pasted pixel is plotted into the cell under it as the half-block tools plot a pixel on that layer (`halfPixelOver`, `Document::pastePixels`): its clear half keeps what the cell under it shows as a pixel, a pixel cell's other half (a red top pixel moved onto blue over blue is red over blue) or any other opaque cell's background (pasted over a letter on black it is red over black). On a pixel layer that is `setLayerPixel`, the layer's cells read with iCE on as it stores them, and a half pixel pasted over a hole stays a half pixel. On a cells or text layer it is `setHalfBlock` with the document's iCE setting: the cell under is read by the colours it shows (a blinking letter's bright background with iCE on), and with iCE off a bright pixel that would share the cell with a different bright colour is written dark, so no bright background is written and the save writes no blink. A cells or text layer can't hold a half pixel, so on a clear cell it goes over what the layers below show at that cell (`Document::shownBelow`, `compositeCellBelow`; the blank ground under the bottom layer), the same way: an opaque cell, never a hole, and the bottom layer stays opaque. The transparent paste mode still skips half pixels, as it skips every cell `isBlank` calls blank. A block copied or lifted off a pixel layer is marked as one (`CellBlock::pixelLayer`, kept through flips and turns), and its whole pixel cells, which the layer stores read with iCE on, are written onto a cells or text layer as the compositor shows them with the document's iCE setting (`pixelCellOnCells`, `halfBlockCell`): with iCE off two different bright colours keep the top one and the bottom goes dark, so no bright background is written; a quarter-turned ▌ or ▐ keeps its glyph with its background dark. Onto a pixel layer they are written as stored; a quarter-turned one there is ▌ or ▐, which the layer can't hold, so it floats but can't land until another quarter turn (§4 Selections). That holds for a lone half pixel too: turned, it is a transparent ▌ or ▐, which holds no pixel and which the paste leaves out, so the landing check reads it as the glyph it is (`SelectTool::withSideways`) rather than letting it land as an erase of the cells it was lifted from. Saving such a float to the Library writes its cells as a paste onto blank cells would (`pasteCell`), so a half pixel is kept over black and nothing blinks that didn't.
- **Moving a layer.** With any tool active, Alt+left-drag (mouse or pen) on the canvas moves the active layer by whole cells, changing its offset without rewriting cells. The press first lands what the tool holds (a drag or floating selection) as an ordinary undoable edit, even for a zero-distance click; the layer move is a separate undo step. The canvas and 1× preview show the move live, recompositing the union of the previously shown and new bounds, clipped to the canvas (`unite(a, b, canvas)`). The status bar shows dx/dy and the resulting x/y. The preview leaves the stored properties and undo history alone; release commits one "Move Layer" property command, or none if it ends where it started. Esc or the undo key drops the preview and undoes nothing else. Focus loss (a popup included), a tool switch, another document command, or save preparation drops it too. Locked or hidden layers refuse the gesture with a status message that clears on release or cancellation, and so does a reference layer with no picture. A reference layer moves by canvas pixels, not cells, by changing its picture's place; with the Move/Transform tool its corner handles resize it (§4). The Move/Transform tool takes the same gesture without Alt. Raise/Lower Layer still change stack order; Ctrl+click still picks the clone source. Alt keys alone, AltGr text and Alt+F menu focus keep their key handling.
- **Canvas Size** (#397 slice 1; `core/include/blockwright/canvas_size.h`, `app/canvas_size_dialog.h`): Format > Canvas Size..., above Target, with no default key, like Target, SAUCE... and the layer commands. Photoshop's Ctrl+Alt+C is AltGr+C on Windows, which types (§4, keyboard mode), and Preferences > Shortcuts can give it any key. The dialog has a width (1 to 400 columns) and a height (1 to 5000 rows), starting at the canvas's size. Those are File > New's limits, `kMaxColumns` and `kMaxRows` in `ansi.h`, which the readers hold a file to as well, so a resized canvas is one that can be saved and opened again. A 3×3 grid of anchors picks where the old canvas sits in the new one. It starts at top-left, because a piece is read from there and grows down, so that anchor keeps every cell's coordinates. A line under the grid says what each side gains or loses ("2 columns added on the left; 3 columns added on the right"), and a note says that cut cells come back with Undo. Crop to Selection and Trim (#397's later slices) are this resize with a size and shift they work out themselves (`resizeCanvas` takes the shift, not the anchor).
  - **The shift** (`anchorShift`) is where the old top-left cell lands. It is 0 at a left or top anchor and the whole change at a right or bottom one. At the centre it is half the change, rounded toward 0, so the odd column is added or cut on the right (the odd row at the bottom), and growing by n and shrinking by n at the same anchor gives the old canvas back.
  - **Layers that follow the canvas.** A cells or pixels layer whose bounds are the old canvas exactly (at (0, 0), the canvas's size) is made the new canvas's size at (0, 0), its cells moved by the shift. The cells past the new edges are dropped, and the step's undo gives them back. Such a layer is the canvas's own art: keeping it at the old size would leave every new cell to the ground and keep `flatHoldsAll` false (§6), so an `.ans` save would no longer be the document's file. Cells it gains are the blank the document gives there (`Document::blankCell`): on the bottom layer when it is a cells layer, `Cell{}`, a space (U+0020) in grey (palette 7) on black (palette 0), opaque, no attributes, as File > New makes and Delete clears to; on every other layer, `transparentCell()`, the same cell with its transparent flag set. A pair the new left edge cuts keeps its continuation as a space in its own colours, and a pair the new right edge cuts keeps its lead as a space in its colours (§16: a continuation in column 0 becomes a space, and no clip at the edge makes a lone wide cell).
  - **Every other layer only moves.** A smaller, moved or larger layer, and every text, generator and reference layer, keeps all its cells and moves by the shift, so a resize loses nothing it doesn't have to, and growing the canvas again shows it all. §5 already lets layers run off the canvas. A text layer's source and a generator's cached output stay as they are, and a generator doesn't run (§7, Trust): its next run gets the new size. A reference layer is placed by its picture, in canvas pixels, so its picture moves by the shift at the cell size it is drawn at, 8 (or 9 with 9 px letter spacing) by 16 pixels a cell, held to `ReferenceImage::kMaxPlace`. Ids, order, the next id, names, visibility, locks, modes and styles stay as they are. Locks don't stop it, because a resize is a change of canvas, not a tool's edit.
