  - **Refusals.** A size outside the limits is refused, and so is one after which the layers would hold more cells together than a project holds (`project::kMaxTotalCells`), or one the renderer can't draw (over 2^28 pixels: 400×5000 fits at 8 px but not at 9). A refusal says why in a message box and changes nothing. The same size does nothing and pushes no step.
  - **Undo.** The whole resize is one step, "Canvas Size" (`CanvasSizeCommand`). It keeps the stack from before, so undo puts back every cell, offset and picture place exactly, and redo works out the same result again from it with the cell width it was made at. Neither puts back the stack's next id: an id given after the resize, or before its undo, isn't given again (the id row above). Because the step holds whole stacks, a generator result that arrives for a step under it, done or undone, is dropped, as one for a step a newer step on its layer came after is (§7). The selection and the cell cursor are part of the step. After the resize the selection moves by the shift and is cut to the canvas (none if nothing of it is left), and the cursor moves by the shift and is kept on the canvas. It stays past the end of its row while its cell is still the row's last (§16, Cursor and typing), and a cursor the shift leaves on a continuation in column 0, from a layer moved left past a pair's lead, takes the cell after it. On a one-column canvas that continuation is the only cell: the cursor stays on it, the canvas shows it as a space (§16), and a combining mark typed there or just after it is refused (`typeMarks`), because its lead is off the canvas and writing over half the pair would blank the other half. Undo puts both back as they were. A tool's drag or floating selection is landed first, as for any command. The view keeps its zoom. Its scroll stays where Qt holds it unless the step moves the cursor out of view, when it scrolls as far as it must to show the cursor, as after any step that moves the cursor. A cursor whose cell the resize leaves in place (top-left, or the side away from it) stays where it was, out of view if it was: nothing under it moved, so the view the user scrolled to is kept.
  - **Elsewhere.** The project saves the stack as it is, and snapshots are the document's state, so neither needs anything new. A flat `.ans` takes the composite at the new size, and SAUCE's width and height are written from the canvas at save (`TInfo1`, `TInfo2`), so the record follows. The IRC row meter measures its rows again (`canvasResized`), and lint and the IRC View follow the step's change of every cell.
  - **In a steps file (#420).** The CLI's commands read, convert and report, and edits go through `replay` steps (§9, Replay), so a resize there is a `canvasSize` step (§9, Canvas size steps). It runs the same core path as the dialog: `canvasSizeProblem`, `anchorShift`, `resizeCanvas` at the document's cell width (`canvasCellWidth`, 9 px with SAUCE letter spacing, else 8) and `resizedSelection` for the selection, which `Document::resizeCanvas` calls too.
- **Layer panel** (`app/layers_dock.h`, the Layers dock and the Layer menu): the layers top first, each row the layer's visibility box and name (double-click to rename), its alpha lock and its kind; the current row is the active layer. New Cells Layer, New Pixels Layer, Delete Layer, Raise Layer and Lower Layer are buttons under the list and the Layer menu's items, with no shortcuts. A new layer is the canvas's size, all transparent, named "Layer N" (the lowest N free), goes just above the active layer and becomes active. Deleting makes the layer below active (the new bottom when the bottom goes); the last layer can't be deleted. Each change is one undo step (`LayerStackCommand` for add, delete and move, `LayerPropsCommand` for the properties), so it sets the modified flag; undoing an add or a delete makes the layer that was active active again. Which layer is active is not an undo step. Before any panel change or a change of active layer, a tool lands what it holds (a drag, a floating selection) on the layer it was editing. The Tool Options alpha lock is the active layer's, so it follows the layer picked. Under the native macOS style the panel's boxes and the Layer Style dialog's effect boxes are drawn through `ItemCheckStyle` (`app/item_check_style.h`): QMacStyle in Qt releases without the fix for QTBUG-150017 (6.8.3, which the macOS build ships, among them) draws only the box at the view's top left on macOS 27. Its base is a second QMacStyle, which Qt never polishes with a new palette, so the view passes each application palette change (a light or dark switch) on to it, once per palette: a top-level view sees the change as `ApplicationPaletteChange`, a child view, as the Layers tree and the effect list are, as `PaletteChange`. On macOS 27.2 with Qt 6.8.3 the boxes show no stale look after a switch even without this; it is kept because QMacStyle drops its cached native controls only in `polish(QPalette&)`. Not yet in the panel: the locked flag, the mode, dragging rows to reorder.
- **Sample merged** (Tool Options, for the eyedropper and fill): off, they read the active layer (the eyedropper picks nothing from a transparent cell or pixel, and fill floods the layer's own pixels); on, they read the composite, and fill paints the region it found there into the active layer, and only that region: a pixel it paints into a transparent cell of a cells layer keeps the other half as the layers below the active one show it (`Document::shownBelow`), which is what that clear cell showed through to. It takes nothing from the layers above the active one, so hiding, deleting or moving them never shows a copy of their colours in the active layer (on a pixel layer that half stays transparent and shows through). A steps file sets it with `"sampleMerged"` on a fill or eyedropper step (§9, #419).
- **Bake** turns a text or generator layer into a cells layer (`bake`), a generator its cached output (its `scripts/<id>/` stays in the project), and a styled layer will bake its styled result (§15). In the app it is Layer > Bake Layer (§15, Bake), which takes a generator layer too since #438. A baked generator's `scripts/<id>/` stays in the project so that undo gives the layer back whole; nothing reads it after (only generator layers run, and an id is never given out twice), and save and reopen keep it byte for byte as any kept file. `import-scene --bake` uses the same `bake` but drops the files, because a built artifact shouldn't name the scene (§14, #308).
- Exports always flatten the visible composite (`flatten`).
- The done-when test (`test_composite.cpp`) puts black, yellow and red pixel shapes, their edges on odd pixel rows, over a steel ramp field shaded down and across the canvas: the composite passes `lint::checkLadder` (speckle included) and the lint's noise score with iCE on and off, and in every cell a shape cuts, the covered half shows its pixel's colour and the free half the colour the half-block rule asks for (with iCE off, the dark colour where two bright ones would share the cell); plotting the same pixels into the flat field leaves 18 to 35 cells that don't. The ladder lint and the noise can't see those specks (a cut cell is a half block, off the ladder, and noise skips pixels beside shade cells), so the test checks them itself; a lint rule for them is a follow-up. `test_app_layers.cpp` does the same through the app: a shade band drawn with the shade brush, a pixel line drawn over it with the half-block brush on a pixel layer, and the band (░, ▒ and ▓) shaded again under it; the band keeps its shades, the pixel layer its pixels, and every cut cell shows the pixel below and above it the colour the shade mostly shows (░ its background, ▒ its darker colour, ▓ and █ their foreground). The same line plotted flat into the band fails that check in the ▓ cells, which the test asserts, so it catches specks.

## 6. Project format

Single file, zip container (like `.ora`/`.kra`), extension `.bwp`, with an unpacked-directory form of the same layout for git and other tools. `core/include/blockwright/project.h` is the reference.

```
manifest.json      "format": "blockwright", "version": "1.3" ("1.4" for big text, "1.5" for a reference picture, "1.6" for Big rainbow, "1.7" for dither marks, "1.8" for a Glow brightness, or "2.0", "2.1", "2.2", "2.3", "2.4", "3.0", "4.0" or "5.0", below),
                   width, height,
                   palette ("cga"), ice, ircBudget {profile, bytes},
                   ansEncoding ("utf-8"; absent for cp437),
                   target ("irc" or "micron"; absent for ansi),
                   sauce (null or title, author, group, date, font, comments,
                   letterSpacing 8/9/"reserved"/null, aspectRatio, tinfo3,
                   tinfo4, reservedFlags), layers (ids, bottom first),
                   nextLayerId
layers/<id>.json   layer properties (LayerProperties) and size; a live text
                   layer's source (§15) under "source", or "tdfSource" when
                   its face is a TheDraw font, or "bigSource" for whole-cell big text
                   ("bigRainbowSource" for Big rainbow);
                   a text style (§15) under "style";
                   a reference layer's picture under "reference";
                   the gradient's dither marks under "dither"
layers/<id>.cells  binary cell data (versioned, zstd)
fonts/<sha256>.tdf a copy of each TheDraw font file a text layer uses
scripts/<id>/      generator.json, params.json and the generator's own files
                   (its cached output is layers/<id>.cells)
refs/<sha256>.png  each reference layer's picture (.jpg for a JPEG)
notes.md           brief, plan, notes; absent when empty
snapshots/r<N>.ans saved rounds, each the composite flattened to .ans
thumbnail.png
```

- `fonts/<sha256>.tdf` (1.2): each TheDraw font file a live text layer's source names, once however many layers use it, named by the sha256 of its bytes (64 lowercase hex digits). A project holds at most 16 (`kMaxFontFiles`, counted before any is read, since each can make up to 2^20 cells of glyphs; the writer refuses more). Reading a zip or a directory, each `fonts/` entry is counted and its size (`kMaxTdfBytes`) checked from what the archive or the disk declares, before it is read, so a deflated 256 MiB entry is never inflated; the writer refuses a copy over 4 MiB before it touches the destination. A reader checks each one's size, that its bytes hash to its name and that it reads as a font file, and refuses the project otherwise; a source naming a file the project doesn't hold, or a font the file doesn't have at that index and name, is refused. The writer writes the files the layers use and drops the rest. A TheDraw source is under `"tdfSource"`, not `"source"`, because a 1.1 reader refuses a face kind it doesn't know under `"source"`: it drops the unknown key, opens the layer as cells read as text, showing the same cells, and keeps the `fonts/` files byte for byte as files it doesn't know. A layer with both keys, or a face kind under the other key, is refused.
- `refs/<sha256>.png` or `.jpg` (1.5): each reference layer's picture, once however many layers show it, named by the sha256 of its bytes and the type its signature says (PNG or JPEG; nothing else is kept). A reference layer's JSON has `"reference": {"image", "x", "y", "width", "height", "opacity"}`: the path, the place and size in canvas pixels (each at most 2^24 either way, the size at least 1) and the opacity in percent. A reader checks that the file is there, hashes to its name and has the type it says, and refuses the project otherwise; the key on any other kind of layer, or a value out of range, is refused, and the writer refuses the same before it writes. The core never decodes a picture. A 1.3 reader knows the kind and its compositor skips it, so it shows the same art: it drops the key, opens the layer as a reference layer with no picture, and keeps the picture as a file it doesn't know. The writer writes each picture once; its reference bytes replace any opaque kept file at the same path. It drops reference files no layer shows, except opaque kept files it does not recognise.
- `"dither"` (1.7): the cells of a layer the ramp gradient's Bayer dither laid (`Cell::dithered`, §10) as base64 of a bitmap, a bit a cell in reading order (cell i is bit i % 8 of byte i / 8, low bit first), `(width × height + 7) / 8` bytes; the key is left out when no cell carries the mark. A bitmap of another length, a bit set past the last cell or anything but a base64 string is refused. An older reader drops the key and shows the same cells; only the lint changes, calling those cells specks again.
- `.cells`: `BWCL`, u16 version (1, or 2 for a layer with wide glyphs or clusters, §16), u16 reserved (0), u32 width, u32 height, then one zstd frame (compressed at level 3 by a save; raw blocks, the bytes as they are, from `bw-make-samples`, `CellsFrame::Raw`, so no zstd version changes them; a reader takes either) of width × height 14-byte cells row by row: u32 code point; fg and bg as u8 kind (0 palette index, then index and two zeros; 1 RGB, then r, g, b); u8 attributes (bit 0 blink; since 5.0 bit 1 bold, bit 2 italic, bit 3 underline; bit 4 is kept for strikethrough and bits 4–7 are refused from 5.0 on; in a project before 5.0 every bit but blink is cleared on load, #350, and the cells cleared are counted: the app says so in its status bar, every CLI command that reads the project in a `note:` line on stderr naming the file and the count, never on stdout and with the exit status unchanged, #370, and `blockwright --screenshot` for its start as `replay` does, #383); u8 flags (bit 0 transparent; in version 2, bit 1 continuation and bit 2 cluster). Version 2 puts the cluster table after the cells in the same frame (§16). Little-endian. Anything else is refused.
- `ansEncoding` (#302): the text encoding the app's Save As writes the project's next flat `.ans` in: that of the `.ans` the project was made from (`project::fromAnsi`, so `pack` and `unpack` of a UTF-8 `.ans` keep it) or the document's when the app saved the project (`Document::textEncoding`). Written only as `"utf-8"`; with no key (every project before it) it is CP437, and `"cp437"` is read too; any other string (a value a later build may add) is read as CP437 rather than refused, since a preference this build can't follow is no reason to refuse the art, while a value that isn't a string is a malformed manifest and is refused. It is a preference, not art: no cell reads differently, so a reader that drops it shows the same project, and it bumps no version (an older build saving the project again drops it, and the next `.ans` from it is CP437 unless picked in Save As). An export of a project to an `.ans` in the other encoding doesn't change it. Snapshots follow it (#336): `project::takeSnapshot` writes the project's `ansEncoding` unless its caller names one, so a UTF-8 piece's rounds keep its wide glyphs and clusters. The CLI's `convert` doesn't follow it: it writes CP437 unless told otherwise (§8). `cli info` of a project prints it as `encoding:`.
- `target` (#350): what the document is drawn for, ANSI (an `.ans`: 16 colours, as every document was before) or IRC (IRC text, which carries mIRC's 99 colours as well), or Micron (a NomadNet page, §17). Written only as `"irc"` or `"micron"`; with no key, `"ansi"`, or any other string it is ANSI, and a value that isn't a string is refused, as for `ansEncoding`. Like it, it is a preference, not art: it changes what the editor offers (the palette, lint's rules, whether iCE folds an RGB cell for IRC), never what a cell holds, and bumps no version. Format > Target sets it as an undo step ("Target"); the status bar shows it ("ANSI" or "IRC") beside the encoding; Edit > Preferences > Documents sets the target of a new document (`document/defaultTarget`, ANSI by default and then not stored: the key is written only for IRC), File > New, the start screen's New and closing the start screen with Esc all take it, and a new IRC document's `.ans` encoding is UTF-8. A flat file opens as ANSI, and an `.ans` can't hold an IRC target, so a document under IRC saves to an `.ans` as an export, not as its file (`flatHoldsAll`). `cli pack` and `unpack --target ansi|irc|micron` set it (default: a project's own, else ANSI); `cli info` of a project prints it as `target:`.
- Version is `major.minor`. Readers refuse unknown major versions instead of dropping data on re-save (Icy Draw's rule). Within a major a reader ignores keys and files it doesn't know; the files are kept byte for byte and saved back, the keys are not, so a minor version may only add what an older reader can drop without changing what the project shows. A repeated JSON key is refused.
- Paths are relative and `/`-separated; an entry with an empty, `.`, `..` or backslash, colon or control-character part, a part over 255 bytes or a path over 1024, an encrypted or oversized entry or a repeated one is refused. So are two paths a case-insensitive or Unicode-normalising disk takes for one (compared as NFD of the full case fold of NFD, so `ß`, `ẞ` and `ss` are one), a directory spelled two ways and a path that is both a file and a directory. Any entry that is not a plain file or directory is refused: a symlink, FIFO, device or socket in a directory, and in a zip any entry whose UNIX mode says so, hidden or not. Hidden files and directories (leading `.`) in a directory are skipped on read whatever they are, before their type is looked at, and a hidden directory isn't entered (#136: a set-aside `.bw-old-…` link a save couldn't remove mustn't leave the project unreadable); a directory save never touches one it didn't make. A directory read opens each file without following a link or waiting on a FIFO, and reads it only if it is still a plain file of the size the walk saw. A zip project is opened once (on POSIX, without waiting on a FIFO), refused unless `fstat` says the handle is a regular file (a socket can't be opened, and is refused as one), and libzip reads that same descriptor (through `fdopen` and `zip_source_filep_create`, not `zip_fdopen`, whose copy of it would not be close-on-exec), so a FIFO, device, socket or directory put at the path after the check for a directory is refused promptly (`<path> is a FIFO, not a project zip`) and never waited on, and the file checked is the file read; `isProject` reads the zip signature through such a handle too, and calls anything else no project (#448). Windows has no FIFOs, and opens by path as before. Every layer file must be listed in the manifest and every listed layer must have both files.
- Versions so far: 1.0; 1.1 adds a live text layer's source; 1.2 a source in a TheDraw font and the font copies; 1.3 a live text layer's style; 2.0 a style on a cells or pixels layer, which the compositor draws, so a 1.x reader that dropped it would show the layer unstyled; 1.4 a live whole-cell big text source (`bigSource`); 1.5 a reference layer's picture, and 2.1 the same in a project that needs 2.x; 1.6 a live Big rainbow source (`bigRainbowSource`, since a 1.4 reader refuses a big style it doesn't know under `bigSource`), and 2.2 the same in a project that needs 2.x; 1.7 a layer's dither marks (`"dither"`, #160), and 2.3 the same in a project that needs 2.x; 1.8 a Glow whose brightness differs from its width (`"brightness"`, #184, §15) on a live text layer, whose saved cells already show it, and 2.4 the same in a project that needs 2.x; 3.0 any Glow on a cells or pixels layer, which the compositor draws as the file opens: a 2.x reader would drop the key and light the ring next to the letters at the width's rung, and, whatever the brightness, would draw each ring by its distance alone, so a counter the halo fills would fade darker inside the inner ring where 3.x holds it at the inner ring's rung (a change to what an older reader of the same major draws as it opens the file is a major bump, as 2.0 was). A writer writes 3.0 only when a cells or pixels layer's style, switched-off effects included, has a Glow, and 2.x only when a layer needs it; else 1.8 or 2.4 when a live text layer's style, switched-off effects included, has a Glow whose brightness differs from its width; else 1.7 or 2.3 when a layer has dither marks; else 1.6 or 2.2 when a text layer is Big rainbow; else 1.5 or 2.1 when a reference layer has a picture; else 1.4 when a 1.x project has a live big text layer; else 1.3 or 2.0. 4.0 a layer with a wide glyph's pair or a cluster (`.cells` version 2, §16), which an older reader would refuse or show in other columns; a writer writes 4.0 whenever a layer needs it, whatever else the project holds, and otherwise the version it wrote before. 5.0 (#350 slice 3a) a layer with a cell in bold, italic or underline (`attr::Formatting`), which a 4.x reader keeps as bits it doesn't know and draws plain; a writer writes 5.0 whenever a layer has one, whatever else the project holds, and otherwise the version it wrote before, so a project without formatting saves as it did. A reader reads 1.x through 5.x, refuses a cell with an attribute bit nothing defines (bit 4, the reserved strikethrough, included) in 5.0 and later, and in a project below 5.0, which no build wrote anything but blink into, clears every other bit on load and says how many cells it cleared (the app's status bar; `project::ReadNotes`); it refuses a version 2 `.cells` in a project below 4.0, and a 1.x or 2.x file with what its version can't hold (a styled cells or pixels layer in 1.x, a Glow on one with a brightness apart from its width in 2.x). A 2.x file from before 3.0 with a Glow on a cells or pixels layer, the brightness absent or the width's, still opens; this build draws its counters filled, which is the point of the change (#184 Q4a).
- Limits, checked before anything is allocated or decoded: 10 000 entries, 256 MiB an entry, 1 GiB in all, 1024 layers, 2 000 000 cells a layer (the canvas limit) and 16 000 000 cells across the layers (about 256 MiB decoded), the last read from the `.cells` headers before any is decoded; 4 000 000 code points in the cluster tables across the layers, each table counted as ⌊B/4⌋ for its B bytes (the frame's content size less the cells), read from the frame headers before any is decoded and again per layer before its frame is; 1 MiB for `manifest.json` and each `layers/<id>.json`, checked before it is parsed (a JSON parse takes about ten times the text).
- Writers encode the whole project first and refuse what a reader would refuse (the limits and paths above, and SAUCE text longer than its field) before the destination is touched, so a save or `snapshot` that would cross a limit leaves the original as it was.
- Zip saves are deterministic (fixed entry time; `.cells` and `.png` stored, the rest deflated) and replace the file whole. A directory save writes every file to a temporary file beside it, created exclusively (`O_EXCL`, `O_NOFOLLOW`, a random hidden name), and only once all are written renames each over its file, the manifest last, then renames each file the project no longer has aside to a hidden `.bw-old-…` name, which a read skips, and only once all are aside removes them (never a file the save wrote, whatever name the disk lists it under, so a disk that folds names some other way can only make it keep a file; a file is known by the descriptor it was written through, and a link to one is not one, so a link found at an entry's own name is removed too); it refuses a symlink at an entry or on a directory on the way to one, a non-empty directory with no manifest, and, before writing anything, an empty path or a path (the directory's own included) longer than the system opens. What a directory save promises, on POSIX (#114): it never writes, follows a link or deletes outside the project directory (but for removing, on a failure, the directories it made on the way to it); it never deletes a file it didn't make or that isn't a stale project entry; and a save that fails for any reason, I/O errors and running out of descriptors or memory included, leaves the directory as it found it. Out of scope: anyone else writing in the project directory while the save runs (another save included; saves aren't locked against each other), and systems other than POSIX, where all of this is done by path, looking before each step. How it keeps them: the given path is followed as given, once, up to the first directory the save makes; past that every directory is opened once, when the save first reaches it (`O_NOFOLLOW`), and everything after, the rollback's included, goes through that descriptor (`*at` calls): telling a project from a non-empty directory, writing, renaming, listing and classifying what is in each directory for the stale-file scan, setting files aside, putting them back, removing them, and removing the directories the save made, each made parent through the directory above it. So the save and its rollback stay in the directories it found or made even if one is moved or swapped for a link meanwhile. A rename that mustn't replace anything can't: `renameat2(RENAME_NOREPLACE)`, else (macOS, a file system without it) `linkat` then `unlinkat` of the old name, or of the new one if that fails (if both fail, the second name is recorded as the save's, and the rollback removes it or the error names it); on a disk with neither the rename, and so the save, is refused rather than made by a rename that could replace. A hidden name the save didn't make is never touched, a `.bw-old-…` file an earlier save couldn't remove included: that stays, hidden, where a read doesn't look. Each change the save makes (a directory, a temporary, a file set aside, a second name a file is left under) is recorded before it is made, or with nothing that can fail between, the room for the record taken first, so nothing can fail between the change and the rollback knowing of it (a test fails the first allocation after each change in turn). A failed save renames the files set aside back, removes the temporaries, gives a file already replaced the bytes it held before the save (read as each temporary is written: a file whose bytes can't be read, or anything at an entry's name that is neither a file nor a directory, refuses the save before anything is replaced), removes a file the save added, and removes every directory it made, making one on the way to the project's included (never one it found there), so the retry isn't refused; a directory is removed only while it is empty and its device and inode are still the ones it had when the save made it (looked at just before, so one put in its place in that moment can be removed only if empty; a directory is the save's from the moment it is made, so one whose device and inode couldn't be looked at is removed too, only if empty). A save over a project therefore leaves the old project readable, never new layer files under the old manifest. The scan holds open every directory it looks in, so a large tree can run out of descriptors; one is held from before the first file is replaced and let go of first by a rollback, which needs one at a time, so running out fails the save and puts everything back. Only a step of the rollback itself failing (writing a file's old bytes back, a set-aside file that can't go back without replacing something, a second name, a temporary or a directory the save made that can't be looked at or removed) leaves anything otherwise, and the error names each such file or directory and where a file's bytes are; that error is a `ProjectError` whatever the save failed with (`std::bad_alloc`, say, named in its message), and a failure the rollback leaves nothing after is thrown as it was. Running out of memory at any allocation, the rollback's own included, changes none of this: every descriptor and libzip archive or entry the save or a read opens is owned from the call that returns it, so none is left open; the rollback allocates nothing unless a step fails, takes every step whatever the others did (how each failed is kept in its record), and only then names what it left, each as far as memory lets, counting in the error any it can't name (tests fail every allocation of a save, a rollback, and a zip save and read in turn).
- A flat file becomes a project as the editor opens it (a non-iCE file with bright backgrounds turns iCE on, its blink dropped and reported). Its SAUCE keeps every field an `.ans` save keeps; the type, size and iCE flag are the project's.
- Snapshots and every export flatten the composite through the compositor and show each cell with the project's iCE, as the editor saves; the CLI refuses a lossy one unless asked.
- CLI: `pack` / `unpack` converts between zip and directory (or from a flat file to a project; an XBIN's palette and font, which a project can't hold, are dropped only with `--allow-lossy`, §8); `snapshot` saves the next round, in the project's `ansEncoding` or the one `--output-encoding` gives; `info` describes a project; every command that reads art reads a project as its flattened composite.
- App (`app/document.h`, `app/main_window_project.cpp`): File > Open… takes a `.bwp` (or any file `isProject` says is one) and File > Open Project Directory… a directory, each with its layer stack (next layer id included), iCE and SAUCE fields and IRC budget (the meter's and Export's, §8); notes, snapshots and kept files ride along unseen (`ProjectExtras`) and are saved back. A project that can't be read, or whose canvas is too large to display, leaves the open document as it was. Save writes the form the document was opened or last saved in (`FileForm`: `.ans`, zip or directory). Save As keeps the form too (a directory picker for a project directory, else the file dialog, whose filter picks `.ans` or `.bwp` and the suffix added); File > Save As Project Directory… (Save As File… for a directory) changes it. A flat `.ans` save flattens through `project::toAnsi`, so it writes what an export or snapshot writes, and never goes over a project, as in the CLI. The document counts as saved only when its file holds all of it. An `.ans` holds all of it when there is one visible cells layer at (0, 0), the canvas's size, compositing normally, and no IRC budget, notes, snapshots or kept files (`Document::flatHoldsAll`; the layer's name, id and locks aren't art and don't count). Then a flat save makes the `.ans` the document's file and marks it clean, as before. Otherwise Save As `.ans` from a project is an export: the file is written, and the project stays the document's file with its modified flag as it was, so an edit or a snapshot taken since the last project save still makes New, Open and closing ask. A flat document (an opened `.ans` or a new one) given layers writes its `.ans` on Save or Save As, takes that name, and stays modified, with no clean state left in its undo stack, until it is saved as a project; the unsaved-changes prompt says so (for a new, untitled one: that saved as an `.ans` it is written flattened), and its Save leaves the window open with the layers rather than dropping them. The app has no separate Export command. A project save keeps every glyph, so only blink lost when an `.ans` was opened gates it. File > Take Snapshot (projects only) runs `project::takeSnapshot` on the document in its encoding (`Document::textEncoding`, #336), asks before a lossy one, and adds the round as an undo step ("Snapshot rN"): it marks the project modified and the next save writes it, and undoing past it takes it off again. A flat file keeps no snapshots, so a snapshot undone and then redone after a save made the document an `.ans` is dropped from the undo stack rather than added (#115).

## 7. Scripts and generator layers

Recommended: **external-process generators first, embedded Lua later.**

- A generator is any executable that reads `params.json` + canvas info on stdin and writes an `.ans` on stdout, read as CP437 whatever its bytes (§8, UTF-8 `.ans`). Black-on-black space is opaque; transparency comes from a chosen key colour (a layer mask may come later).
- **Built (core, `generator.h`; part of #17):** a generator layer's files are `scripts/<id>/` in the project, kept byte for byte: `generator.json` (`{"command": [program, args…], "key": 0-15, "params": [...]}`, the key optional and a palette index as cells number them, the params optional), an optional `params.json` (a JSON object) and whatever else it needs. `"params"` declares the parameters the editor's form shows, each `{"name", "type", "label", "min", "max", "step", "default"}` with only the name and type needed: an `integer`, a `number`, a `boolean`, a `colour` (0-15), a `ramp` (a named ramp) or `text`. min and max bound an integer or a number, an integer's values, bounds and default are whole numbers from -2147483648 to 2147483647 (what the form's 32-bit fields hold), a number's values, bounds, step and default are from -1e12 to 1e12 with at most six decimal places (what its decimal field holds: written with six places and read back unchanged), and a declaration or a stored value past those is refused with the reason rather than shown clamped or rounded; step is a number's step in the form, and default is the value when `params.json` lacks the key (else the minimum or 0, false, light grey, the first named ramp or ""). A spec is read strictly: an unknown type, a bound or step on the wrong type, min above max, a default the param can't take or a name given twice refuses the whole `generator.json`. A run fills each declared param `params.json` lacks with its default and refuses one it holds that the param can't take; keys it doesn't declare pass through as they are. A run copies that directory to a fresh temporary directory and starts the command there: a bare program name is looked up on `PATH`, a relative path (`./gen.sh`) is the generator's own file, an absolute one is used as it is. stdin is one line of JSON, `{"canvas": {width, height, ice}, "layer": {id, name, x, y, width, height}, "params": {…}}`. stdout is parsed at the layer's width (unless its SAUCE gives one) and opened as the editor opens a file; the cells become the layer's (its size is the output's, its place and properties stay), and with a key colour every blank, non-blinking cell on that colour is transparent. A run that can't start, exits non-zero or crashes (the end of its stderr in the message, a path under the temporary directory in it written as `./name` and the directory itself as `<generator dir>`, as given or with links resolved, so a traceback names `./gen.py` and not a random path the author doesn't have, #441), takes longer than its timeout (30 s by default) or writes over 32 MiB is refused and leaves the project as it was. The program leads a process group of its own and the run ends by killing that group, whichever way it ends: on a timeout or the cap at once, while output is still arriving (it is read as it comes, so a flood holds no more than the cap), and after a success too, so a background process the program leaves behind is stopped and what it would have written is not read. A process that leaves the group on purpose (`setsid`, a daemon) is not followed. The group is signalled only while the program is unreaped, so its id can't be another group's: the run sees the program end without reaping it (`waitid` with `WNOWAIT`), kills the group a last time, retires it, and only then reaps the program; nothing signals that number after. That needs the program to stay unreaped until the run reaps it, so a run requires that the process doesn't ignore SIGCHLD (`SIG_IGN` or `SA_NOCLDWAIT`, when the kernel reaps each child as it exits) and that nothing else in it waits for a child it didn't start (`waitpid(-1)`, `waitpid(0)`). The first is checked: a run refuses before it starts anything while SIGCHLD is ignored. The second can't be, and neither the CLI nor the app does it (Qt's `QProcess` waits only for its own children). An ignored SIGCHLD survives `exec`, so a parent can hand one down; the CLI puts SIGCHLD back to its default before it runs a generator. The CLI kills the running generator's group before SIGINT, SIGTERM or SIGHUP ends it, since the terminal's ^C no longer reaches a separate group (`killRunning`). From that call on no run starts, so `run-generators` starts no further layer. Signals are blocked on the running thread from just before the program's start until its group is published, so one that comes while it starts is handled with the group known; and a handler on another thread that found nothing running can't miss a run that was about to start: it sets a stop flag before it reads the running group, the run marks itself starting before it reads the flag, so either the handler sees the run and kills its group, or the run sees the flag and starts nothing. stdin is a file, so a program that never reads it can't stall the run.
- **Trust:** a generator runs with the user's rights, so nothing runs one unless asked: opening, saving and exporting a project only read its cached cells. The CLI runs them on request (`add-generator`, `run-generators`, §9); `run-generators` without `--layer` runs only the visible generator layers, and a hidden one runs only when `--layer` names it (#440, §14). The app runs one layer at a time, the one its form's Run or a parameter commit names, hidden or not, so it has no run of every layer to align. The app asks before a project's generators first run in a session (a warning box naming the command, No the default) and then runs them without asking for that project, by its canonical path, until it quits; a project saved under another name stays trusted, and any other project, or a new document, is asked about again. A declined prompt runs nothing and asks again at the next run. A refused or failed run leaves the layer's cached cells as they were and says why in the status bar.
- Existing Python `ansidraw` scenes work unchanged: one scene layer becomes one generator layer. **Built (part of #17):** ansidraw has two layer modes that write nothing to the piece: `scene.py --list-layers` prints `{"width", "rows", "title", "layers": [{"name"}, {"name", "key"}…]}` bottom first, and `scene.py --layer-ans=NAME --key=K` writes that one layer's `.ans` to stdout, every cell the layer leaves empty a blank on colour K. The scene picks each layer's key from the colours none of its blank cells use and fails loudly when none is free; the bottom layer is drawn whole and has no key. In those modes the scene's own file changes on the piece's directory (and anywhere outside the system temporary directory) go to a private overlay removed at exit: `open`, `os.open` and `os.truncate` write a copy, removal leaves a tombstone, `rename`/`replace` and `mkdir`/`rmdir` act on the overlay, `chmod`/`chown`/`utime`/xattr changes go to the copy (or do nothing to a file only on disk), by path or through a descriptor (`fchmod`, `fchown`, `ftruncate` too: a descriptor on a guarded file on disk is matched to its path and treated as the path is), and `stat`/`listdir` see the overlay, so the scene observes what it would run flat. Links resolve as the overlay sees them (a name it holds or removed is no link, and nothing on disk under a directory it holds or removed is seen); an exclusive create (`O_EXCL`, `open(..., 'x')`) fails on any entry at the name, a dangling symlink included; a move never writes through a symlink, and a link moved into the overlay becomes a copy of its target's overlay copy, not the stale file on disk. The guard's own nested calls are exempt per thread only. The threat model is deliberately narrow: a scene is the artist's own trusted code, run only behind the trust question, and the guard exists so that the scene's ordinary saving (its `.ans`, previews, sidecars, comparison images) can't overwrite the piece while it is imported. It is not a sandbox against hostile code. Outside it: programs the scene starts, ctypes and C extensions, the raw `posix`/`_io`/`io.FileIO` modules, functions bound before ansidraw is imported, `mmap` of a descriptor the guard never saw, `os.link`/`symlink`/`mkfifo`/`mknod`, changes by other processes between the guard's check and its act, `os.scandir`/`os.walk`/`glob`, which list the directory on disk, a descriptor opened for reading on a file on disk before the scene first changes it (`os.read` and `os.fstat` on it still see the disk), and symlinks in the overlay (a link to a missing file moved in leaves the name missing, where flat `lexists` and `listdir` would still show it); a directory move, and a link to a directory moved into the overlay, are refused. `blockwright-cli import-scene <scene.py> <out.bwp|dir>` (§9) and File > Open Scene… in the app (after the trust question names the scene's command; the result is an untitled, unsaved project whose generators are then trusted) read the list and add one generator layer per scene layer, named after it, with the command `<python> -B <scene.py's canonical path> --layer-ans=NAME --key=K`, and run each once (`scene_import.h`). `<python>` is `python3` on Linux and macOS, as it always was and unprobed. On Windows (#250) it is the first of `py -3`, `python` and `python3` on PATH that answers `--version` with `Python 3.` on stdout (Python 3.4 and later; 2.x and 3.0-3.3 print it on stderr and are passed over), each looked up as a layer's run is (`scene::findPython`): python.org's installer provides `py.exe` and `python.exe` but no `python3.exe`, and stock Windows' App Execution Aliases in WindowsApps stand in for a Store Python and, with none installed, exit 9009, so a bare `python3` there either isn't found or fails as an exit-code error. Each probe may take 5 s; the one found is kept for the rest of the process, and a failure is not, so the next Open Scene looks again. A probe runs as `generator::probeCommand`, recorded apart from the generator running now, so a probe while a generator runs (another window's, or one that outlived its window) never hides that generator from `killRunning`, and `killRunning` kills both. The one found is the command stored in each layer's `generator.json`, so the layers run again the same way; the app finds it before the trust question, which names it, and refuses Open Scene while the window's generator runs before it asks or probes anything. With none, the error lists each command tried and why it was passed over, and says to install Python 3 from python.org. Known limitation: a project imported on Windows before #250 stores `python3` and is not migrated; its layers still fail where `python3` is the WindowsApps alias, and opening the scene again stores the Python found. The stand-ins in the tests (sh scripts on Linux, copies of `bw-gen-helper.exe` under wine) check the order and the probe; a real Windows run belongs to the #20 CI matrix. Both the listing and the layer commands run with `-B`, so the scene's imports write no `__pycache__` beside it. The canvas is the scene's width and rows, iCE on unless the listing says `"ice": false` (an optional bool, true when left out; anything else is refused), the SAUCE title the scene's when CP437 holds it. A scene's layer `.ans` files carry an iCE-off SAUCE record when it is off, so each layer parses as the flat piece does. The project refers to the scene where it is, by absolute path, rather than copying it: the scene stays the one source (an edit there shows at the next Run) and finds its `locked/` art and references beside itself as it always has. Tradeoff: a run needs the scene, the Python it was opened with and `ansidraw` where they were at import, so a project moved to another machine opens and exports its cached cells but can't run its layers there. A key is fixed at import; if the scene is later edited so a layer's blanks use its key, the run fails and says to open the scene again, which picks a free one. The composite of the imported layers looks the same as the scene's own flat `.ans` (a test fixture).
- **Built (app; part of #17):** the generator form (`app/generator_form.h`) in the Layers dock under its buttons, shown while the active layer is a generator (§11). It has a field for each declared param, with the value from `params.json`: a slider and spin box for an integer with both bounds, a spin box for any other number, a check box, a palette colour list, a ramp list with each ramp's swatch, and a line of text. A field commits when it is let go (the slider released, Enter or focus leaving a spin box or the text, an entry picked), and Run runs the generator again as it is. A commit is one undo step through the layer-properties command (`LayerPropsCommand` with a generator step), which writes the new `params.json` into the kept files at once. The run that follows (`app/generator_runner.h`) waits 300 ms so a burst of changes runs once, then runs on a worker thread on a copy of the project, one run at a time. When its cells land they become the step's cells after, so undo and redo put back both the params and the cells and run nothing. A result is dropped if it is out of date: the layer's files changed since it started, a newer step on the layer came (even one undone since, which holds the step's cells as its cells before), or the document was replaced. A result that gives the step the cells it already has changes nothing, so a document saved while it ran stays clean. A field keeps an edit not yet committed (typed text, a spin box's digits) when a run lands; undo and redo still put back the value they change, and opening or starting another document drops it. A number field shows a stored value finer than its declaration's step as it is. The cached cells are the layer's `.cells` like any layer's, so saving keeps them with `params.json`, and opening, saving and exporting run nothing. A step whose run is overtaken by a newer change before it lands keeps the cells from before it. A Run's result is dropped too when an edit was undone while it ran (a parameter step back to the params it read, or any other edit), so it never discards what redo would give back; one asked for with edits already undone replaces them as any edit does. Quitting the app kills a running generator's group (`killRunning`) rather than waiting for it. Layer > Bake Layer makes a generator layer a cells layer of its cached cells (§5, §15 Bake); the runner starts no run for a layer that is not a generator (baked or deleted) when the run's turn comes, judged then rather than while another run is in flight, so a layer an undo has brought back still runs its newest step; a run in flight when its layer is baked is dropped when it lands.
- Tradeoff: process spawn per run (tens of ms) and Python as a runtime dependency, against zero embedding work and full reuse of the scene's own Python.
- Phase 8: embedded Lua 5.4 via sol2 (MIT, header-only) for in-editor brushes and fast generators; sandboxed, no interpreter to ship. QJSEngine is the fallback if sol2's slow release pace becomes a problem.

## 8. Formats

| Format | Read | Write | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `.ans` + SAUCE | v1 | v1 | Real FileSize; title 35 / author 20 bytes; iCE, 8/9 px, aspect flags; COMNT block; trailing-black trim; CRLF rules as common writers have them. Parse semantics follow libansilove (`src/loaders/ansi.c` @ `ced66a0`): CR doesn't move the cursor; TAB, CUF and a CUP column can take the cursor to the width, not the last column, and the next byte that draws or moves the cursor first wraps it to the next row; EL is skipped; ED 2 clears and homes; an ESC not followed by `[` skips only itself; SGR and CSI t skip empty fields, so `ESC[m` does nothing; SGR 7 as its invert: background = foreground without its bright bit, foreground = background plus the foreground bright bit, with PabloDraw CSI t colours set aside while reversed; SGR 1 drops a CSI t foreground and SGR 5 a CSI t background, iCE or not, and a dropped one stays dropped after SGR 27; CSI t acts only on selector 0 (background) or 1 (foreground), reads a missing channel or one past 255 as 0, and black (0,0,0) means no 24-bit colour. Divergences kept on purpose, each pinned by a test: (1) when a glyph fills the last column, a LF after nothing but CRs, SGRs and CSI t ends that row instead of adding a blank one; libansilove wraps on the CR and the LF adds a row, which would put a blank row under every row of the 20 avatars, comic-lulzy, baud-dudes, hb-burnlootmurder and indian-tier. Any other escape in between (EL, an unknown or private CSI, a malformed CSI, a lone ESC) wraps first, as libansilove does on every ESC, so `80×X ESC[K CR LF Y` is 3 rows high. After a CUF or TAB to the edge, CR LF adds the row as in libansilove. (2) Where libansilove computes values past the palette we stay in it: bold on an iCE bright background (`1;5;31;44;7`) ORs the bright bit (libansilove adds it and reaches index 20), a repeated SGR 1 or 5 doesn't add 8 again, and a CUP row or column of 0 is read as 1 (libansilove computes -1). (3) SGRs libansilove doesn't read keep their usual meaning: 21 and 22 bold off, 25 blink off, 39 and 49 default colours, 90–97 and 100–107, 38/48;5;n and 38/48;2;r;g;b (libansilove reads the sub-parameters as SGRs of their own). (4) An SGR field that isn't plain digits is skipped wherever it sits and the other fields still apply: a private field (`?999`, `>1`) and a colon-form colour (`38:5:n`, `38:2:r:g:b`) alike. libansilove reads such a field as 0 and resets everything, so `ESC[31m ESC[999;?999m A` and `ESC[31m ESC[38:5:2;44m A` stay red here. Plain digits too large for libansilove (`4294967296`) read as 0 and reset there too; here they are an unknown SGR, like `999`, and do nothing. (5) A cursor move with more than one parameter (`ESC[2;5C`) uses the first; libansilove's strtonum fails on the whole field and moves 1. (6) A CSI longer than 14 bytes acts as usual; libansilove looks for the final byte only in the first 14 (`ANSI_SEQUENCE_MAX_LENGTH`, `ansi.c:40/220`) and otherwise draws the sequence as text. (7) An unknown final byte ends the CSI, which is skipped; libansilove scans on past it for a known final byte, so `ESC[1zAB` moves up and draws only B there. SAUCE TInfo2 is the minimum height. Rows exist only once something is drawn on them, so parse time follows the bytes and cells drawn, not how far the cursor moves. A bright background forces iCE on when writing (100–107 aren't written); blink lost that way, or a glyph written as `?`, makes `cli convert` refuse without `--allow-lossy` |
| IRC `.txt` (mIRC codes) | v1 | v1 | Encoder = Blockwright's own (`core/`, below); per-line budget; optional fit-to-budget with least-visible merges |
| Micron `.mu` (NomadNet) | — | #475 (built) | `micron.h`, below; CLI `export --micron`, File > Export Micron... |
| PNG | — | v1 | 1× and scaled; IRC-view variant (`render --irc`, below). `render/` draws with the bundled IBM VGA 8×16: 9 px when SAUCE letter spacing is 9 px (or `--font-width 9`), the 9th column repeating the 8th for 0xC0–0xDF only; iCE off draws blink cells in their visible phase with the dark background, as libansilove does; 24-bit cells draw their RGB; integer scale 1–8, nearest neighbour. The legacy aspect flag is not applied (square pixels; `cli render` prints a note). Golden tests compare cells with libansilove output and allow only the 28 glyphs where its built-in font differs from the IBM VGA one (`tests/data/render/ansilove/MANIFEST`) |
| UTF-8 ANSI | v1 (#302, built) | v1 (#302, built) | For terminals and paste; read and written as below |
| `.bin` / `.xb` | Phase 6 | Phase 6 | XBIN header + RLE; BIN width from SAUCE (below) |
| TDF fonts | Phase 5 | — | Text layers (§15), read from a fonts folder set in Preferences and from the copies a project holds (§6) |
| PabloDraw 24-bit `CSI t` | v1 (import, quantised) | later | |

**Reading a file** (#453): every file a user names is read through one handle checked by `fstat`, `bw::readRegular` (`core/` `regular_file.h`): the path is opened once with `O_NONBLOCK | O_NOCTTY | O_CLOEXEC`, anything `fstat` doesn't call a regular file is refused before a byte is read, naming what it is (`<path>: is a FIFO, not a regular file`; a directory, a device, a socket), and that handle is then read blocking, held to a limit `fstat` checks first and the read checks again as the file grows. So a FIFO at the path, there from the start or swapped in after a check, is refused at once and never waited on, and the file checked is the file read. Windows has no FIFOs, so there the path is checked and then read. The readers and their limits: a flat file (`.ans`, XBIN, BIN, ADF, IDF, PCB, `.asc`, `.txt`) in the CLI's `info`, `convert`, `render`, `export`, `shade` and the rest (`bwcli::readFile`) and in the app's Open (`Document::read`), `project::kMaxEntryBytes` (256 MiB), what a project's entry may hold; a steps file (`replay`, `--screenshot`), 16 MiB; each file a steps file's later step reads, its step's own limit (§9); a TheDraw font, alone or in the fonts folder's scan (`readTdfBytes`), `kMaxTdfBytes` (4 MiB); a reference picture (Open Reference Image), `project::kMaxEntryBytes`; a generator's files (`cli/generators.cpp`), `kMaxGeneratorBytes` (64 MiB) together; Library stamps and sidecars (`library::readRegularFile`, a wrapper that keeps the Library's own messages), `kMaxSidecarBytes` for a sidecar and `kMaxFrameStyleBytes` for a frame style. A project zip checks its own handle the same way and hands it to libzip (#448, §6); `replay` tells a project from a flat file by its first four bytes, read the same way (`readRegularHead`). A file that is missing or can't be opened is refused in the system's words (`No such file or directory`, `Permission denied`), as before. A pipe is a FIFO too, so a CLI input given as `/dev/stdin` from a pipe or as a process substitution (`<(…)`) is refused like any other FIFO; the CLI reads no input from a pipe, only from a file. Not routed through it: the app's own resources (`:/` paths: the logos, the help, the tool icons, the UI font), which are compiled in, and a scene (Open Scene), which the app only checks is a regular file and hands to Python, whose run the generator's time limit stops (§7).

**XBIN and BIN** (`core/` `xbin.h`) read into and write from an `AnsiDocument`, as `.ans` does, so the app, the CLI and every command that reads an `.ans` read them too. A file is read as XBIN when it starts with the XBIN magic (`XBIN` 0x1A), whatever its name; otherwise `.xb` / `.xbin` is XBIN, `.bin` BIN and anything else `.ans` (any case). A file is written in the format its name gives (`artFormatFor`), in the app's Save As (an XBIN and a BIN filter, the suffix following the filter) and in the CLI's `convert` and `shade`. Those two refuse a `.bwp` output (any case) before reading the input, pointing to `pack` (#298): the app opens any `.bwp` as a project zip, so art bytes under that name would not open. `export --irc` (IRC text) and `render` (a PNG, with or without `--irc`) refuse one the same way, through the same check, saying what they write instead (#316). An output that already is a project, by any name, is refused too (exit 1 for all of them). The commands that write a project may write one under a `.bwp` name: `pack`, `unpack` and `import-scene`, and `snapshot`, `add-generator` and `run-generators`, which save the project they read.
- *XBIN*: the 11-byte header (size, font height, flags), then the palette (48 bytes of 6-bit RGB, widened to 8 bits as `v << 2 | v >> 4`, so it writes back as it was read), the font (256 × height bytes, height 1–32) and the cells. The non-blink flag is iCE. A 512-character font is refused (its second 256 glyphs have no CP437 byte). Compressed data follows the XBin specification and Moebius (`libtextmode/xbin.js`): a run's top two bits are its kind (none repeated, the character, the attribute, both), the low six its length − 1. The reader lets a run cross the end of a row, fills cells the data stops short of with blanks and ignores bytes after the last cell. It decodes the cells before it looks for SAUCE, and looks only in the bytes after the last cell (`splitArtSauce`), so cells that spell `SAUCE` 128 bytes from the end are cells and a last `0x1A` attribute is a cell, not SAUCE's EOF; only a file cut short of its cells takes a record at its end as SAUCE, as `.ans` does, and reads the cells from what is before it. The writer compresses each row on its own (no run crosses a row end, as Moebius writes it), in the fewest bytes the four run kinds allow (a dynamic programme over the row); of runs that cost the same it takes both repeated, then the character, then the attribute, then none, then the longest, so its output is fixed. SAUCE is XBin (DataType 6, FileType 0, TInfo1–2 the size, TFlags and TInfoS 0): the header already holds the size, iCE and font, so an `.xb` names no SAUCE font. The header has no letter spacing or aspect ratio either, so an XBIN save leaves out a record's 9 px letter spacing, its aspect ratio and a font name other than IBM VGA (or IBM VGA 437), and counts each as a loss (`letterSpacingDropped`, `aspectDropped`, `fontNameDropped`, #299): warned and refused unless allowed like the others below, and once the document is saved as that XBIN it no longer has them. They are format state the undo stack holds (`FormatCommand`, §4), so the drop is an undo step of its own ("Save as XBIN", #319) pushed just before the save marks the document clean: undo brings them back and shows the document modified, redo drops them again at the saved state, and a save that drops none adds no step. 8 px, an unset flag and IBM VGA are what an XBIN without its own font draws, so they lose nothing. Writing TFlags or TInfoS into the XBin record would keep them for Blockwright but break the SAUCE spec other readers follow, so they are reported instead.
- *BIN*: bare character/attribute pairs. The width is SAUCE's FileType × 2 when SAUCE is BinaryText (DataType 5), with iCE from its flags; without one the width is 160 and iCE is on. An odd last byte (SAUCE's EOF) is dropped, and the last row is padded with blank cells. With no header to bound the cells, a record whose FileSize is more than the bytes before it can't describe them and is read as cells; a record that spells `SAUCE` with a FileSize that fits is still taken as one. A BIN is an even number of columns wide, so an odd-width canvas is written with a blank column on its right. SAUCE is BinaryText with the record's flags (iCE set as written) and font name, so `.ans` → `.bin` → `.ans` is the same file.
- Both write every glyph with a CP437 byte as that byte, NUL and control codes included, and a glyph without one as `?` (`unwritable`, as `.ans`); iCE and dropped blink follow `writtenIce`, as `.ans`.
- An XBIN's palette and font are kept on the document (`AnsiDocument::palette`, `font`) and written back by an XBIN save, but nothing draws with them yet: the canvas and PNG render in the standard palette and the bundled font. `.ans`, BIN and a project can't hold them, so saving to one counts as lossy (`paletteDropped`, `fontDropped`; a palette equal to the standard one isn't counted) and is refused unless allowed, like any other loss (`convert`, `shade`, and `pack` and `unpack` of an XBIN, each with `--allow-lossy`); once the document is saved to one of them it no longer has them. `blockwright-cli info` prints an XBIN's `format`, `palette` and `font`, and a BIN's `format`, and checks FileSize against the bytes the cells take (`splitArtSauce`).

**UTF-8 `.ans`** (`core/` `ansi.h`; #302, done): an `.ans` is the same escapes and controls in either text encoding, so one parser reads both and `ParseOptions::encoding` picks the text (nothing: `detectEncoding` decides). A file is read as UTF-8 when its content (before SAUCE and the first 0x1A; SAUCE's own fields have no say) starts with a byte order mark, or when every byte from 0x80 up belongs to a well-formed UTF-8 sequence (RFC 3629: no overlong form, no surrogate, nothing past U+10FFFF, none cut short) and at least one sequence is three or four bytes long; anything else is CP437, as every `.ans` was before. The three-byte rule is there because CP437 art is full of pairs that are valid two-byte UTF-8 (╔╗ is `C9 BB`, ╚╝ `C8 BC`), while the block, shade and box glyphs a UTF-8 piece draws with are all three bytes; across a corpus of 875 CP437 `.ans` files it takes none for UTF-8, and it finds every one of 800 UTF-8 files. In UTF-8 a byte below 0x80 is what it is in CP437 (controls act, 0x01–0x1F and 0x7F are their pictographs, NUL a space), a well-formed sequence is one cell holding its code point (any code point, not only CP437's; one cell each, wide and combining characters included), each byte of a bad sequence is one U+FFFD (as the IRC view's `decodeLine` reads one), and a leading byte order mark is skipped. SAUCE's text fields are CP437 bytes in either case, as the SAUCE specification has them, and FileSize counts the UTF-8 bytes. `AnsiDocument::encoding` records which it was read as. Only files a user opens are detected: the app's Open (and anything else through `parseArt`) and every CLI command that reads art; the CLI's `--encoding auto|cp437|utf-8` (info, convert, render, export, shade, lint, pack, unpack) overrides it, and `info` prints an `.ans`'s `encoding`. Files Blockwright writes for itself are read as CP437, never detected, so their meaning can't change with their bytes: library stamps (§12) and a generator's stdout (§7). `writeAnsi(doc, encoding)` writes either; CP437 is the default, and the document's `encoding` is not consulted, so each caller picks. As UTF-8 the escapes and line ends are the same bytes as in CP437, 0x20–0x7E are themselves and NUL is a space; every other cell is its code point's UTF-8 sequence, so ○ ◙ ♪ → ← (CP437 0x09, 0x0A, 0x0D, 0x1A, 0x1B, which a CP437 `.ans` can't hold) and glyphs CP437 lacks (★) are kept. Only what no UTF-8 `.ans` can hold is written as `?` and listed in `unwritable` (`utf8Writable`): a C0 control or DEL as a code point, a C1 control (U+0080–U+009F: a terminal that honours C1 takes `C2 9B` for a CSI, and the IRC export writes them as `?` too), a surrogate, anything past U+10FFFF. A byte order mark is written only when the content would not be detected as UTF-8 without one (only ASCII, or only two-byte sequences such as é), so whatever is written reads back as UTF-8 with the same cells, and a piece with a block or box glyph has none. SAUCE stays the CP437 record the specification defines (its text fields are CP437 bytes whatever the art's encoding); FileSize counts the UTF-8 content, a byte order mark included. A CP437 piece written as UTF-8 and read back has the cells it had (`tests/data/utf8/tux-utf8.ans` is `golden/tux.ans` written so). XBIN and BIN are CP437 by format (`writeArt` ignores the `.ans` encoding for them). Who writes which: the CLI's `convert` and `shade` take `--output-encoding cp437|utf-8` (default cp437; with utf-8 an `.xb` or `.bin` output is refused, exit 2), `replay` (#208) takes it for its captures, CP437 by default whatever the start, as `convert` is, and `snapshot` takes it too, defaulting to the project's `ansEncoding` (#336); the app's Save writes an `.ans` in the encoding the document was opened in or last saved in (`Document::textEncoding`; a new document is CP437, a project the `ansEncoding` it was saved with, §6), and Save As has an "ANSI art, UTF-8 (*.ans)" filter beside "ANSI art (*.ans)", preselected for a UTF-8 document, so picking one changes it for later Saves too; a save to XBIN or BIN makes the document CP437. Library stamps stay CP437 whatever the document's encoding, unless CP437 can't hold a user-library stamp's cells (#336, §12). Snapshots, which only Blockwright reads, follow the project's encoding (#336). In the app (part 3): File > Open's filters include "ANSI art, CP437 (*.ans)" and "ANSI art, UTF-8 (*.ans)", which read an `.ans` as that encoding; "ANSI art (*.ans)" and the others detect it. Because a platform dialog may merge filters with one pattern, File > Reopen with Encoding (Auto, CP437, UTF-8) reads the open flat `.ans` again in the one picked, after asking to save unsaved edits; it is disabled for a new document, an XBIN or BIN, and a project. The status bar shows the document's encoding ("CP437" or "UTF-8"), following open, Save, Save As, a project save and New. A save's loss warning words what it loses for the encoding: for a CP437 `.ans`, glyphs with no CP437 code and those whose byte is a control code, and, when a UTF-8 `.ans` would keep some of them, how many, and to Save As "ANSI art, UTF-8 (*.ans)"; for a UTF-8 `.ans`, control characters a UTF-8 `.ans` can't hold (never "no CP437 code"). A project save, an XBIN and a BIN get no UTF-8 hint. The no-code list and the UTF-8 control list name what they lose (`lossDescription`): up to three distinct glyphs in the order first met, each with its cell as column,row from 0 as the status bar shows it (`★ at 0,0`) or, when several cells hold it, the count and the first (`★ in 2 cells, first at 0,0`), then "and N more glyphs". A control or invisible character, one that acts or shows nothing visible printed on its own (`unprintableCodePoint`: a C0 or C1 control, DEL, a format character such as a bidi mark, embedding, override or isolate, a line or paragraph separator, a non-spacing or enclosing mark such as a variation selector (the second cell of ❤️, U+FE0F), the combining grapheme joiner or a lone accent, a Hangul filler (U+115F, U+1160, U+3164, U+FFA0), a noncharacter (U+FDD0–FDEF, U+xFFFE, U+xFFFF), a code point Unicode leaves unassigned, a surrogate or anything past U+10FFFF), is named as `U+XXXX` (`quotedGlyph`), never shown raw; a space or a private-use glyph is shown as itself. The CLI's `convert` warning, lint (§10) and the typing refusal in the status bar quote it the same way, the refusal setting a code off from the glyphs beside it with a space (`★ U+202E has no CP437 code; not typed`). Wide (East Asian) and combining characters stay one cell per code point, read and written; drawing them two cells wide or on the cell before is not done yet: §16 (#334) is the plan for it.

**The IRC encoder** is Blockwright's own C++ in `core/` (`mirc.h`, `mirc.cpp`), so the meter, lint and export share one encoder. `encodeMircRow` writes a row of cells in mIRC colours as the line that draws it in the fewest bytes as the ascii script sends it (`irc::txtSentBytes`, which the budget, meter, Fit and Export all hold lines to), and of those the fewest as written; the line shortest as written can send more, since the script rewrites every colour code with two digits and writes none in front of a space on the background it already has (#411). Its count of each step's bytes as sent follows the script's writer exactly. It also searches by bytes as written and keeps that line instead where it sends fewer (on a row with bold, italic or underline the slack can prune the line that sends least), and where the other line sends more at some budget: over its budget the script sends a line in its own shortest encoding where that is shorter, so the line kept must send no more than the line shortest as written at every budget. A row with a cell the count can't follow (a cell the script joins to the one before it, a cluster, a control) is written shortest as written and its bytes as sent measured. It searches every way of drawing each cell (a space as a block in its background colour, a half block as the other half with the colours swapped, a cell whose colours match as solid, and with `swapShades` ░ as ▓ reversed and ▒ as itself reversed), lets a colour code that has to set a colour its glyph doesn't show (a space's foreground, a block's background) set the one the next cell needs, so that cell needs no code of its own (#401), writes a colour number with one digit unless a digit follows, ends a colour code before a comma with `^B^B` where that is shorter than repeating the background, and ends a line whose last glyph is a space with `^O`; with `solidEnd` a last cell drawn as a solid colour is drawn as a block (█), so a client that trims trailing spaces keeps it. `nearestMirc` is the nearest of mIRC's 99 by squared RGB distance, the lower number on a tie. A code point that is not a Unicode scalar value or a colour over 98 throws `IrcError`; `irc::encodeRow` never passes either. `tests/data/golden-irc/` is the fixed oracle for its output.
- Export = Blockwright's parser (above) → canvas → the row encoder, so the CLI export, the editor's export and the meter all measure the same canvas. Export encodes the cells as they are: the 16-colour palette, since the parser quantises 256-colour (`38/48;5;n`), 24-bit (`38/48;2;r;g;b`) and PabloDraw `CSI t` colours to it as it reads them (the table above). A palette index maps to the mIRC colour of the same name and shade (the PC's dark red to mIRC brown, its brown to mIRC orange, its grey to light grey). The encoder also maps an RGB cell to the nearest of mIRC's 99, and since the IRC target (#350) the palette dock puts mIRC colours 16–98 in cells as their exact RGB, which export as their own code. A cell is sent as it shows (`irc::sentCell`): with iCE off a bright background is folded to blink on its dark colour, which IRC shows dark, but under the IRC target an RGB background is sent as it is, since iCE belongs to the `.ans` and folding it would send mIRC 80 as CGA green; an indexed cell folds under either target, so a document of the 16 sends the same bytes under both (`test_irc_target.cpp` encodes every golden piece under each). An `.ans`, XBIN or BIN writes an RGB colour as the nearest of the 16, and counts the cells where one shows and isn't exactly a palette entry (`AnsiOutput::coloursQuantised`, `quantisedColourCount`): it is a loss, under either target, so Save asks, `convert` and `shade` need `--allow-lossy`, and a snapshot is refused without it. Transparent cells export blank, as `.ans` writes them, and a C0 control or DEL in a cell exports as its CP437 glyph (NUL as a space), never as a raw byte; a C1 control (U+0080–U+009F) exports as `?`, since some clients read `C2 9B` as a CSI. Trailing black spaces and the blank rows above and below the art are dropped as the ans2irc CLI drops them, unless the lines are padded (below). Divergences from the ans2irc CLI, which parses the file itself: (1) colours: ans2irc keeps 256-colour and 24-bit colours and maps them to the nearest of mIRC's 99 (`38;5;208` → mIRC 53, `38;2;0;128;255` → 59), where Blockwright exports its 16-colour quantisation (mIRC 7 and 10), pinned in `tests/test_irc.cpp`; (2) every parse divergence above where Blockwright follows libansilove and ans2irc does not changes the IRC output too: ans2irc stops a CUF at the last column, libansilove (and Blockwright) at the width, so text after `ESC[nC` to the edge wraps differently, and the CR and TAB rules above (a CR doesn't move the cursor, a TAB can reach the width) give different rows for a file that relies on them. Over the library corpus, which has no 256-colour, 24-bit or `CSI t` colours, the CUF wrap is the only difference seen (1 of 509 files).
- **Formatting** (#350 slice 3a): a cell's bold, italic and underline (`attr::Bold`, `Italic`, `Underline`) are written as `^B`, `^]` and `^_`, a byte a toggle, in that order (ascii.py's `ATTR_CODES`), switched where they change between cells and never left off a cell that has them, after any colour code (so a code's digits end first); a line ends with them as they are, since the next line starts plain. The search's states carry the attributes, and its slack is 9 bytes rather than 6 on a row with any formatting, so a row without formatting is searched with the slack it had before formatting. An underlined cell has one way, its own glyph in its own colours, since the line shows the foreground (a space as `█` would move it); an underlined black space at a row's end is not trimmed, and `fit` leaves a formatted cell alone. The encoder writes the formatting a cell has even where it draws nothing (lint's `wasted-format` reports it). `decodeLine` toggles them per cell and `^O` clears them; `^^` and `^Q` are read and draw nothing. The sender (`irc_view.cpp`, a port of ascii.py 1.32.2) parses, encodes and cuts them as ascii.py does: its `encode` writes the toggles before the colour code where they change, a space is visible when underlined (`SPACE_VISIBLE`), and `encode_irc_min` falls back to `encode` for a line with any formatting. The renderer draws them (`formattedRows`, the same in `renderCanvas` and the atlas): bold ORs each row with itself one pixel right, italic shears one pixel right for every four rows above the bottom, underline lights the bottom row (row 15, row 7 in an 8 px font) in the foreground, nothing past the cell. An `.ans`, XBIN or BIN can't hold them, so each counts the cells that have them (`AnsiOutput::formattingDropped`, `formattedCellCount`; transparent cells and continuations aside) as a loss under either target, as for a quantised colour.
- `cli export --irc` encodes losslessly and never cuts: lines over the budget, measured as the ascii script sends them (`irc::txtSentBytes`, #364), are listed with their size as written and as sent, and the file is not written (exit 3) unless `--allow-over-budget`. The budget is `--budget`, else a project's own (`ircBudget`, §6), else 407.
- **Budgets** (`irc.h`): a relayed line is `:mask PRIVMSG target :text\r\n` in 512 bytes, so the text has `498 − |mask| − |target|` (`irc::budgetFor`). The presets are named profiles (`irc::kBudgetPresets`; today `efnet-weechat`, 407 bytes, room for a long EFnet mask); any other count is profile `custom`. The project format keeps `{profile, bytes}` as it was. A budget is 1 to 512 bytes (`irc::kMinBudget`, `irc::kMaxBudget`, `irc::validBudget`), the one range the project format, the Budget dialog and the CLI's `--budget` take (#303): a line over 512 bytes can't be sent at all.
- **Export options** (`irc::EncodeOptions`, the CLI's `--swap-shades`, `--pad`, `--fit`, and View > IRC in the editor; none is on by default, so the default output stays `golden-irc`'s): *swap shades* lets the encoder draw ░ as ▓ reversed. *Pad* keeps trailing black cells and the blank rows above and below the art, and ends each row whose last cell is solid black on a black █ (`solidEnd`), so a client that trims trailing spaces or drops empty lines keeps the frame. *Fit* (`irc::fitCells`) merges colours in a row over the budget until it fits, the least visible first, the row measured as the ascii script sends it (`irc::txtSentBytes`, #364), so a row written within the budget but sent over it is fit too; text is never changed, so a row of text the script sends over the budget (80 ★ in two colours: 402 bytes written, 483 sent) is left as it was and reported over. The changes a cell can take: a solid cell takes its left neighbour's colour (cost: squared RGB distance × 4); a half block or shade becomes solid in its foreground (distance between its colours × the quarters the background covers) or background (× the quarters the foreground covers: ▀▄▌▐▒ 2, ░ 1, ▓ 3), or takes a left-neighbour colour into one half. A change to another mIRC index of the same RGB (4 and 52) costs nothing. Changes are taken cheapest first, a twelfth of them a round as `ascii.py`'s `fit_halves` does, no two neighbours in a round, a cell at most three times (a half block into a solid, then into its neighbour's colour), the costs adding up; once the row fits, the changed cells are put back, the most visible first, wherever the row still fits without them. A row that can't be fit is left as it was (and is reported over). On the dev machine a 120-row piece with 17 rows up to 608 bytes fits to 407 in about 70 ms (about 15 ms before #364, when each try was measured as written, not as sent).
- **The row meter** (`app/irc_meter.h`): a gutter right of the canvas, outside the scroll area's viewport, gives each row's bytes as the ascii script sends them (`irc::txtSentBytes`, #364) as a bar against the budget and a number, red over the budget and amber where fit merged cells; the tooltip gives that size and the size as written, and by how much it is over. It encodes the cells as Save writes them (with iCE off a bright background is the dark colour), with the window's budget and options, re-encoding a row only when it changed, and draws each row level with the canvas row however the view is zoomed or scrolled. It maps a meter pixel to a row as the canvas maps the pointer to a cell, on the canvas's device grid (§4; `CanvasView::toView`, then `cellAt`; #404): at a fractional device pixel ratio a logical pixel two rows share is the row its top edge is in, the upper one, and each row's band (its bar and its tooltip's area) is the meter pixels that rule gives the row, so the bands tile the meter without overlapping. A tool's edit in progress (a drag, a floating selection) is measured as the canvas shows it: the composite with the edit on the active layer (`Document::previewEdit`), nothing where the layer would refuse it. File > Export IRC Text... lands that edit and writes exactly what it measures, asking before it writes lines over the budget. The budget is the document's: a project's own `ircBudget` (§6), so the meter, fit and Export hold lines to the budget `cli export --irc` uses for the same project; a flat `.ans` and a new canvas start from the default (EFnet / WeeChat, 407), and opening or starting another document takes that document's. View > IRC > Budget... (a preset, a number, or worked out from a mask and channel) sets it on the document as an undo step ("IRC Budget", `Document::setIrcBudget`), so it marks the document modified, the meter follows undo and redo, and a project save writes it. An `.ans` can't hold a budget, so on a flat file a budget other than the default leaves a flat save unsaved, as layers do (`flatHoldsAll`), until it is saved as a project. Fit, Pad and Swap Shades are not in the project format: they stay the window's, as they are flags of each `export --irc`.
- **The IRC view** (`irc_view.h`, `irc_render.h`) is the canvas as a client shows the exported lines: each line encoded as export encodes it, read back as a client reads mIRC codes (`decodeLine`: `^C` with up to two digits a side, a comma with no digit after it a glyph, `^V` swaps, `^O` and a bare `^C` reset, 99 is the client's colour, each bad UTF-8 byte one U+FFFD), and cut to the budget as the sender (`ascii.fit`, the ascii.py sender script 1.32.2 or later; §16 has what older scripts do with clusters) cuts it: the script loads each `.txt` line by encoding it again (`compact`, which is `encode_irc`: two-digit colour codes, no code inside a cluster), so every line is measured and cut as that encoding, not as written (#364); a line that fits is sent whole; a longer one the sender encodes again (`encode_irc_min`: two-digit colour codes, a space or a full block for a solid cell, whichever is shorter, the cheapest encoding of the row), sends that whole if it fits, and otherwise keeps the clusters that end within budget − 1 bytes of it, the last byte for the reset after them, and loses the rest. The re-encoding is ported byte for byte (it decides where the cut falls: a 580-byte export line keeps 56 cells cut where it is, 45 cut where `fit` cuts it), and the cells shown are the export's own. Cells IRC drops (trailing black spaces, blank edge rows) and cells cut off show the client's background (0x262626, text 0xd2d2d2); every cell is RGB in the mIRC palette. `renderIrcView` draws it as `renderCanvas` does, with a red bar a quarter glyph wide on each cut row: at its first lost cell, or at the right edge when every cell the view shows is sent and the cells lost lie past its width (a row of lone wide cells lays out wider than the canvas, #364).
