The clamp's walks along a line (`Fill::clamp`) still go on past the box through a field of one rung, to another rung or the canvas's edge, so on such a field a fill in a box costs more on a bigger canvas: a 20×10 box on an 80×H field of steel's 4 costs 2,320 visits at H 40 and 3,920 at H 120, 20 more a row (a test pins both). With `added` the function reports what the edit adds to the lint's findings (`StrokeLint`). **Known limit:** a band or ring of the gradient's own that meets the climb at a slant to the edge (a radial fill the edge cuts, a linear one at an angle to it) can join the climb's run of the same rung in an L that the lint reads as a wide rung, and the fill reports it: rare in a small box, common along a long edge; a linear fill square to the edges makes none. Also left: cells between two fields too far apart in rung for both pulls, a run of the field itself that the fill makes a step, and with iCE off a field the ladder can't reach without a bright background. A test fills a 64×32 canvas at every 15°, both shapes, ten spans from 6 to 90 columns, nine rung ranges (up, down, inside the ends, within a pair, and three rungs across a solid each way at both ends of the ramp), every ramp, iCE on and off, and another starts on the canvas's edge and up to two cells off it: the ladder lint finds nothing, but the one-glyph rule where the canvas shows fewer than three rungs or not all of the ladder (the drag runs on past it). A third fills two boxes, one against the canvas's edge and one with the field all round, on a 40×20 field of four rungs (solids and shades, dark and bright), both shapes, every 45°, two spans, five rung ranges, every ramp, iCE on and off: every finding is in `added`, a linear fill square to the edge adds none, and 138 of the 25,092 fills it paints add the L, the counts pinned as measured. A fourth fills a box five cells in from the edges of a 120×300 field, two ramps, a dark solid field and a bright shade, linear fills square to the edges, slanted linear and radial ones, four rung ranges: the square ones are clean, and 34 of the 64 others add the L and nothing else, pinned as measured. In both, every wide rung's run comes nearer the edge than its rung's distance from the field's, the climb's cells of that rung: it is the L, not a wide rung of the fill's own.
- **Bayer banding** (#160): the cell-banded fill is laid first, then its band edges are mixed in the 4×4 Bayer order (`kBayer4`, cell (x, y) taking `kBayer4[y % 4][x % 4]`). A painted cell of a solid rung (not black) with a painted cell of the next rung up or down one or two cells off along its row or column, only its own rung between, takes that rung where its order is below 6 (one off) or 2 (two off), and a painted shade cell beside a solid's cell (not black) takes the solid where its order is below 4 (`kDitherNear`, `kDitherFar`, `kDitherBand`): across an edge the neighbour's share runs 2, 6, 12 and 16 sixteenths. A cell keeps its new rung only where every neighbour it has on the ladder is one rung from it, it can be written (iCE off: a bright background can't), and the ladder lint finds nothing the cell-banded fill didn't have but the dither's own specks; a finding sends back every dithered cell within a cell of it, and the pass repeats until none is left, so a Bayer fill is never dirtier than the cell-banded one. The cells that keep a new rung, and only they, carry the dither mark (`Cell::dithered`), and the edit says so (`CellEdit::dithers`) so that `settleMarks` keeps its marks and clears the ones it writes over. With iCE off, in a selection and under the alpha lock it fits as the cell-banded fill does. Tests: the pattern on a 60×8 steel fill is pinned row by row and checked against the rules above; a sweep (every 30°, both shapes, five spans, every ramp, iCE on and off) finds the ladder lint clean on every Bayer fill but for what its cell-banded fill has, and the same cells unmarked give specks; in a selection a Bayer fill adds no finding the cell-banded one doesn't; a fill over a dithered one leaves no stale mark. A sweep of 81,120 fills on the dev machine (every ramp, iCE on and off, both shapes) made 3,267,353 dithered cells and no fill dirtier than its cell-banded one; with the marks cleared the lint found 497,665 specks among them. A fill takes about 1 ms.
- **Ramp gradient tool** (`GradientTool`, Tools > Ramp Gradient, Ctrl+Shift+G; it shares the fill's toolbox button): a drag from the start cell (a radial fill's centre) to the end cell, the fill redone on every move and shown as the pending edit, one undo step on release; Esc drops it. A left drag runs from the From rung to the To rung, a right drag the other way. It fills the active layer as `rampGradient` does (the selection, the alpha lock, iCE), on the view's ramp, and changing the ramp or the shape mid-drag redoes the fill. It doesn't read the symmetry: the fill already covers every cell it may paint, so a mirror image could only land on cells it may not. On a Pixels layer the press is declined and the status bar says why, as for every ramp tool that writes shades (Common to all brushes, below). What the fill adds to the lint (`added`) is shown in the status bar. The drag builds its lint guard (`StrokeLint`) at its first preview and judges each move against it a change at a time. A selection, ramp, rung or shape change mid-drag redoes the fill and builds the guard again, a layer switch lands the drag on the layer it was on, undo and a new document end it, and the right button's reversal lasts through all of these until the drag ends: a test each, each failing when that invalidation is skipped (#121). A move then costs the fill's box and, for the guard, the rows and columns the fill crosses, each read end to end (`LadderIndex`). In a 20×10 selection on a blank canvas that is 1,200 fill visits whatever its size (on a field the clamp's walks add more, above) and 26,875 lint reads at 80×40, 46,875 at 80×120 (about 250 a row), held by a test. On 400×2000 a move takes about 1.9 ms on the dev machine, 8.3 ms before the fill was windowed to the box. The guard's lines are not windowed: what they still cost is well inside a frame, and it would take re-judging part of a line in `LadderIndex`. Shape, rungs and banding are saved in the settings (`gradient/shape`, `gradient/from`, `gradient/to`, `gradient/banding` as `cells` or `bayer`, anything else reading as cells); the To rung starts at the ramp's top, and a rung past a ramp's top reads as the top. Tool Options offers Cell-banded and 4×4 Bayer, and with Bayer the status bar says so (`Ramp steel, linear, Bayer, rung 0 to 12`).
- **Smudge** (`brushes/smudge.h`, part B; the Smudge Brush tool, Ctrl+Shift+M, either button): each cell of the tip carries a value of its own, a rung not rounded, keyed by its place in the tip, so a wide tip drags a patch rather than one colour. At every stamp, joining lines included, a tip cell over a cell of the ramp lays `smudgeMix` (strength of the way from the cell's rung to what it carries), rounded to a rung by `smudgeRung` (the nearest, a half going away from the cell's rung, so half strength moves a cell a rung toward a neighbour a rung off) and written with `stepRung`, and carries the mix on: at full strength it drags its value unchanged, at half it fades toward what it crosses, at none it changes nothing. A tip cell starts by picking up the cell under it and lays nothing. Cells are read as the stroke has left them, with iCE off as they show. Black (ink), cells off the ramp, transparent cells (they show the layer beneath), cells the stroke may not paint (outside the selection) and the canvas edge stop the smear: the tip cell over one drops what it carried and leaves the cell. Under a mirror the dab's images are smudged together from the same cells, each with its own tip, a cell two or more images reach taking the mean of what they lay, summed in sorted order (floating-point sums of three or more terms depend on order, and a cell's mirror partners take the same values in another order), so a symmetric canvas stays symmetric; a radial image stamps on its own, in turn. The stroke's lint guard judges each stamp; a stamp it refuses gives way a cell at a time (with its mirror images), in rows from the top, each a rung at a time back toward where it was, and a cell no rung of which is clean is left. So a plane's edge two rungs from the next (8 8 8 | 6 6 6 at half strength) smears to a ladder (8 8 8 7 7 6), and a smear across a fold whose planes are further apart, or into a ladder a rung a cell, lays nothing where the step would skip. What the tip carries is the mix whether the guard took it or not.

**Detail**
- **Half-block pencil** with 4-connected stroke mode, so diagonals don't dot (the default; 8-connected is the dock's other choice).
- **Outline brush:** the edge cells under it become the line: the ramp colour next below the level inside them (`lint::outlineRung`), the darkest the ladder lint accepts. The level inside is read once a stroke from the whole stroke-start canvas, so a thin part takes the line of its body however far off, under any dab. Where the darkest would add a finding, the cells to blame step one rung lighter, down to the cell as it was; on a pixel layer, which holds no shade (§3), the steps skip the shade rungs, so a cell takes the next whole colour or stays as it was (`StrokeOptions::pixelLayer`). Each finding is blamed on its own: along its row or column (a wide run, where nothing along it is, on the changed cells across it within the runs blameRuns reads, searched among the trial's changes, so the search costs the changes and not the canvas); a speck on itself first; noise on the changed cells whose own change raises it (the lonely and counted pixels round the cell, with it as tried and as it was); one glyph on the cells that change a shade glyph. A blamed cell beside a darker blamed cell waits for it. Where no step clears noise, the one finding, the line goes back on the index a cell at a time, the cells that raise the noise least alone first, each kept where the noise stays as it was or within its limit: a line raises noise as a whole, its cells taking pixels each other's neighbours count, so no cell of it need raise the noise alone and leaving the nearest cells off first can drop most of the line (20-shades and psyop-radar). Where no step clears another finding only the fewest cells nearest it are left off, not the rest of the line. Where an image's first settle (a click, a stroke's start) writes nothing from three or more covered cells, it settles again carefully: a skip between two changed cells is first the cell more findings involve, else the darker; then each cell left as it was is tried a step darker, alone or with a stepped neighbour, kept only where the whole line still adds nothing. The outline settles again until a round, careful pass and all, writes nothing, so its line is its own fixed point; but a careful pass starts only while the outline has read under 5e7 cells (`detailCellsRead`, about a third of a second on the dev machine), since a pass that writes starts more rounds and each round is O(W + H) a changed cell: on baud-dudes-all (80×2312) a square-161 click at (60,1541) read 2.4e9 cells in 16 s with a careful pass on every round that wrote nothing, and reads 1.6e8 in 1 s with the budget. Pieces of a few hundred rows stay well under it. A fade the line would leave a speck is repaired inward, on covered cells only. No fragments: a piece of line under three cells the lint allows only alone is left off, and so is a corner where the line leaves a covered edge cell, but only from a piece of four or more, so a connected piece of three stays. A fringe takes the line in its black half, outside the shape, when the line is darker than its shape half (with iCE off written the way round that keeps the background dark, or left where it can't be). Where an outside cell lies beside a fringe along its half (left or right of a ▀/▄, above or below a ▌/▐), as at the ends of an ellipse's top and bottom rows, the shape half would still meet the black ground past that line, so the whole cell takes the line first and the half line is the next step lighter (#53). A fringe the line went into, whole or by its half, is line to the fragment rule. Every write is judged by `lint::LadderIndex` (§10, the ladder lint) with the guard identity: a finding is added by place, a wide rung unless it overlaps one of the same rung that was, noise only when over the limit and higher, so a dab that softens a finding or changes only its value is allowed. Each symmetry image (`Dab::image`) keeps its own index, and a mirror image is outlined in its own frame (the canvas mirrored back, glyphs and all) with the stroke's own unflipped dabs. The shown canvas has one more index. The stroke stamps point k of every image, then point k + 1, and once every image of a point has drawn their composite is judged as a whole: where it adds a finding, the cells to blame for it (a skip's darker changed cell, a speck and what is round it, the cells changing a shade glyph for one glyph, the changed cells across a wide run) go back to what the stroke showed, a mirror orbit at a time and in the images too, until it adds nothing; only where nothing to blame clears it are all the images' cells refused, so a Mirror X click can then write nothing. Where images overlap a cell shows the strongest dab's line; on a tie, the image whose frame has the cell in the first half across, then down, then the lower image, so a cell and its mirror cell take mirror images' lines and a mirrored canvas gets a mirrored line. Each dab settles only round its own new cells: the covered cells within 3 of them, the ones the stroke has written too, again from their stroke-start values, against the line beyond them and 8 more cells of context, so the line there comes from the covered cells rather than from the line the earlier dabs left, though not wholly: the order limit below stays (#159). Where that settle changes a cell within 2 of the window's side and the stroke covers a cell past that side within 3 of it, the window widens to 6 cells round the new ones and settles again, kept only where it writes no less of the line in the wider window than the narrow one did (without that check the slope test's reversed stroke lost (4,4)). A window the stroke has drawn in settles as an image's first settle does, carefully where the plain settle writes nothing. Where settling again would take back a cell the stroke has written or leave a lone cell of line, the dab settles only the covered cells the stroke has left as they were, and the line so far stays: a dab never takes back the line an earlier one drew, though it can settle a written cell a step lighter or darker. A dab that would add a finding adds nothing. A dab's work (`detailCellsRead`, the lint's reads and the copies included) is flat in the stroke's length and O(W + H) per changed cell; each image's first dab copies the canvas and takes its index (a whole-canvas pass). Where a mirror stroke's images meet in a finding, the composite puts its index back by swapping its own cells in and out, not by copying the canvas: a test holds the dab after a stroke's first, on a canvas 32 times wider and taller, to within twice 32 times its reads (before, the copy made it 77 times). On a RelWithDebInfo build on the dev machine a size-3 rim stroke takes 0.27 ms a dab at 80×40, 0.27 ms at 160×100 and 0.38 ms at 400×160 (50,208, 49,133 and 92,890 reads a dab; 0.23 ms and 39,742 reads at 80×40 with the window fixed at 3; 0.12, 0.14 and 0.21 ms and 18,974, 23,568 and 48,746 reads while a written cell stayed for the stroke); a size-256 click takes 13, 28 and 73 ms. Where the line along a stroke shifts with every dab the window widens every dab: a size-3 stroke along a shaded 1100×40 strip takes 17 ms and 5.1e6 reads a dab, 8.4 ms and 2.6e6 with the window fixed at 3. Work is linear in the canvas's side: Codex's wide-run square (an image's first settle, so careful) reads 172,565 cells at 40 and 991,565 at 320, and Codex's exposed wide columns 75,869 at 40 and 415,229 at 320. A larger click writes no less on the same shape at the sizes 3, 9, 31, 61, 91, 121, 161, 201 and 256: downfall-of-numenor and byzantine-eagle, and lrh-show, durin-gate, avatars-generic-2, 20-shades and psyop-radar, which are over the noise limit already (the size sweep in `tests/test_outline.cpp`). Between other sizes it is not sure where the noise is over its limit: cells the noise fallback keeps apart can be pruned as fragments, so psyop-radar's steel at (20,13) writes 127 cells at size 73 and 109 at 75. A sweep of three clicks each on 304 of funkpower's pieces dips in 14 of 894 sweeps at those nine sizes (15 before the noise fallback), every one adding no finding. Guarantees: a dab adds no finding to the shown canvas; a stroke's dabs again in the same stroke change nothing. Limits: a second stroke of the same dabs over the result adds no finding but can change cells (51 of 2,400 generated partial strokes; 40 with the window fixed at 3, 91 while a written cell stayed for the stroke), and where the careful budget ran out so can a second click (the baud-dudes-all click above: 14 cells after 799); the line still depends on the order of the dabs where a written cell lies past the window a dab settles (3 cells round its new cells, 6 where it widens) and stays as an earlier dab settled it, where settling again would take back line, and where a dab adds nothing for a finding it would add: 40 of 715 generated strokes write other cells in another order (55 with the window fixed at 3, 95 before #159), and 385 of 2,400 two-dab strokes on the ramps of the pieces in `tests/data/brushes`, a dab within 3 cells of the other, sizes 2-7 (425 with the window fixed at 3), most of them on 20-shades and avatars-generic-2, which are over the noise limit already and where a dab's noise is judged against the line before it. It shows at two neighbouring dabs, so a drag and the same drag the other way can differ: on the permutation test's blob a joined drag from (2,1) to (5,4) lines the bottom edge (4..6,4) as █ one way and as ▒, two rungs lighter, the other. The order limit is accepted, not a bug (#159, closed as this limit): each way found to remove it breaks a rule above, and the one change that breaks none only narrows it. Letting a later dab take back line (settling the whole covered set every dab with no fallback left 2 of the 715 strokes differing) breaks "a dab never takes back the line an earlier one drew", and settling the whole set breaks the flat per-dab cost too. Making the settle monotone in the covered cells means dropping what takes line away when cells are added: the fragment, corner and light-lone rules and the lint's blame (112, 88, 68 and 149 take-back events on one run of the order test), which is to drop "no fragments" and "a dab adds no finding". Settling once at the stroke's end breaks "preview is the commit" and the per-dab lint guard on the shown canvas. Running the careful pass on every dab is that change: 40 to 32 at the default seed (seeds 1-6 from 44, 40, 45, 59, 51, 38 to 43, 36, 41, 46, 41, 38), for 7.2e6 cells read a dab on the 1100×40 strip against 6.0e6, the rest coming from the kept line and the refusals. The tests hold the limit under fixed upper bounds, not at the measured counts, so it can grow within them: the order property test (`tests/test_outline_property.cpp`) allows `differ * 8 <= strokes` on any seed and `differ <= 45` at the default seed, where 40 differ; the permutation test in `tests/test_outline.cpp` allows per path at most 20 differing orders on the diagonal, 15 at the canvas edge, 14 on the slope neighbours, 1 on each two-dab sub-stroke (curve and loop) and none on the rest; and the two-dab test there (`an outline stroke on a piece writes the same whichever way round`) holds 18 strokes on four pieces to the same cells either way round. No test holds the 385 of 2,400 two-dab strokes. A mirrored canvas gets a mirrored line from clicks and from unjoined and joined strokes. The careful settle runs on an image's first settle and on a window the stroke has drawn in only, so a later dab of a stroke that meets a skip like the crescent's away from its line can still be left unlined. Seams (#51): two shapes on the same ramp that touch are one shape to the outline, so by default no line runs between them and they merge. With *Outline the selection* in Tool Options (`OutlineShape::Selection`) the shape is the ramp's cells in the selection and every other cell is outside it, so the selected shape's edge runs along the seam and takes the line there on its own side, the outline colour of the level inside it, as against black; the shape beside it is left alone. Until the selection can take a lasso or wand mask it is a rectangle, so it lines only a seam that runs along the selection's sides: where the seam curves, the other shape's cells inside the rectangle are part of the selected shape and are lined at the rectangle's border (#100). With no selection, or one over the whole canvas, it is the outline as ever. The selection is the rule rather than a colour boundary (a jump of two or more levels would line every hard shadow terminator, and two shapes of one colour have no boundary to find) or a flood from the first dab (it needs a boundary rule too, and would leave the line off wherever the flood doesn't reach); a layer can mark the shape once layers do (§5). The seam is settled against the lint like the rest of the line: beside a flat shape or one falling away from it the seam is the full line (W, then w, then W is no skip), but beside a shape that climbs away from it (W, w, then W▒w W▓w W) w is a skip, and the seam takes the next lighter rung, W░w. The lint reads rows and columns and can't tell a seam from a gradient falling through a line (the crescent's W▓w W░w K W▓w is the same sequence), so it has no exemption for seams; one waits on a mask or layer the lint can read. Preview is the commit, one undo step a stroke; selection and alpha-lock hold. Tools menu, Ctrl+Shift+E, Tool Options' ramp and *Outline the selection*.
- **Rim/highlight brush:** brightens only cells on the light-facing edge of the region under the cursor, by one rung, lit from the Brush dock's light direction (`BrushSettings::lightDirection`).
- **Texture stamps** (`brushes/texture.h`, the Texture Stamp tool, Ctrl+Shift+P): `lumps()`-style scales, foliage and hoard, and brick and noise. The kind, density and seed are kept between runs (the kind by name, so a kind added later moves no saved one; a name not known reads as scales). The texture is a field over the whole canvas fixed by the seed (0–99999), so overlapping dabs, a drag and a second stroke all uncover the one texture with no seam where they meet. Lumps are ellipses on a jittered grid whose spacing is set by density (lumps' area over the area, 0.3–2.5; 1.6 by default). The seam is rung 0: the gaps between lumps, a lump's cells beside a lump drawn over it (lower down, then further right), and its rim (past 0.75 of the radius) away from the light. Inside the seam each lump is a ladder down from a two-cell peak at its lit point, one rung a cell, so no two lumps' lit cells touch along a row or column. A cell lighter than all its on-canvas neighbours (three or more), black and the top rung aside, takes its lightest neighbour's rung; then, in what that leaves, a cell darker than all of them takes its darkest neighbour's. Each takes a neighbour's rung, so it makes no new speck and no skip, and the stamp is lint-clean (no skip, speck or non-neighbour shade) on every named ramp, kind and density tested, mirrored or not, on canvases from 1×1 to 81×41, odd and even. Scales peak at the top rung, foliage leaves at the top or one or two under it, a hoard's coins two under it with about one in three glinting at the top. Packed lumps are mostly shadow: about 55% of cells are seam at density 1, 70% at 2.5. With iCE off the top is the highest rung with every rung under it writable. The light is the dock's light direction. With a mirror symmetry on, the field is folded about the mirror axes (the canvas centre column, row or both): every cell reads it at its fold in the left (top) half, whichever image paints the cell, so the stroke and its images are the exact mirror image (light mirrored too) even where they overlap, and strokes with the same mirror agree where they overlap. The fold changes only what lies right of (below) the axis: off the fold's own column and row the left (top) half is the plain field, and the speck rule judges the folded picture, so the stamp stays lint-clean across the axis. So a mirrored stroke and an unmirrored one differ right of (below) the axis. Radial images can't be exact on the cell grid (turns other than a half are made on screen and rounded), so a radial image reads the plain field at its own cells: overlapping images agree, but the lumps aren't turned with the images. **Brick** is a running bond on black mortar (rung 0, the seam): a mortar row over each course, a one-column mortar joint between bricks, every other course shifted half a brick. Density sets the brick's size: its rows are 3.6/√density (at least two) and it is 5.2 columns long a row, so 16×3 at 1.6, 10×2 at 2.5 and 36×7 at 0.3. The seed moves each joint a column either way or not and each brick's face a rung either way or not, about 0.62 of the way up the ramp. A brick is a plane: its two columns and one row on the light's side a rung lighter (the lit corner two), its far edges falling a rung a cell into shadow (three columns and two rows, fewer on a small brick). The across and down profiles add, so neighbours in a brick are a rung apart, and the two-column lit edge leaves no cell lighter than both its neighbours along a row. A mirror's fold reflects the profiles, so where the fold crosses a brick's slope (most often where two folds meet, or a fold meets the canvas's edge) a cell can be darker than all its neighbours; the speck rule raises it. **Noise** is a mottled stone with no seam: seeded value noise (smoothstep between lattice values) at three scales, a drift, a mottle and a grain (lattices 72, 24 and 9.6 columns over the density), plus the mottle's relief lit from the light (the mottle less the mottle a fifth of its lattice toward the light), about the middle of the rungs from 2 to the top and rounded to rungs. It is read once per block of two cells side by side, square on screen, so every cell has a twin beside it and none can be a speck; a left cell with no right one (an odd canvas's last column, or the centre column under a mirror's fold) joins the block to its left. Value noise of lattice L climbs at most 1.5/L a column, so a term of amplitude c moves at most 3c/L between neighbouring blocks (two columns apart on screen either way); the terms share 0.95 of a rung a block (drift 0.3, mottle 0.35, grain 0.1, relief 0.25), so neighbouring cells are at most a rung apart, and a coarse field's swing is cut to the ramp's range. That bound is the price: a fine grain is low in contrast. Being full cells, both kinds are far under the lint's noise limit. Like the rim light it is an accent: the lint judges where it meets what was on the canvas.
- **Glyph spray** (`brushes/spray.h`, the Glyph Spray tool, Ctrl+Shift+Y): stars, haze and smoke, each a field over the whole canvas fixed by its seed (0–99999), as the texture stamp's is, so overlapping dabs and a drag uncover the one field, and a second stroke takes cells only toward it, never past it, so nothing piles up. The tool keeps its kind, density (1–100%, 30% by default) and seed in the settings (`spray/kind`, `spray/density`, `spray/seed`) and shows them in Tool Options. *Stars*: one candidate cell in each 8×4 block, jittered inside it so no two are closer than five columns or three rows, a star with the density's chance; the glyph is one of `. · ∙ + * ☼` (all CP437), weighted toward the small dots, in the pen's foreground. A star lands only on open ground (craft's "four pixels off every shape"): the cell and every cell within four columns and two rows read as one flat colour (a space, a full block or a shade of one colour, not blinking; a star already there counts as its background), read as the stroke began, so which cells a stroke may star doesn't depend on the order of its dabs. Not on a bright ground with iCE off, nor on ground of the pen's own colour. The right button turns any of the six glyphs under the brush into a space on its background, pre-existing ones included (a `.` or `*` drawn before the spray goes too). Stars and their wipe go through the stroke's lint guard: each star is judged with its mirror images as it lands, and one that would add a finding to the stroke's start (noise on a busy canvas) is held back; each dab tries again the held stars and wipes within four columns and four rows of a cell of its footprint (or an image's; for a round tip, of the round, not its bounding box), row by row, until a pass lands none, so a held star or wipe in reach that a change in the same dab frees lands in that dab. Noise is read over the whole canvas, so stars compete for its budget: where it binds, which stars land depends on the order the stroke tries them, and the same dabs in another order can land a different set. *Haze* and *smoke* lift ░▒ along the ramp: one octave of seeded value noise, haze a slow wave (lattice 20 columns by 3 rows), smoke tall wisps (4 by 10) curled sideways by a second noise. The density is the share of the canvas covered, by a quantile of the field's values over a fixed sample; the cover is cleaned of lone cells and pin holes, a cell all of whose eight neighbours are covered lifts two rungs (▒) and the rim one (░), and a ▒ with no ▒ beside it drops to ░. The left button lightens a cell to its colour's solid rung (the one its rung rounds down to) plus the lift, if that is lighter; the right darkens to the solid rung it rounds up to, minus the lift. The target is absolute, so going over a cell again changes nothing. The top colour isn't lightened, black isn't darkened, cells off the ramp, blinking or transparent are left, and with iCE off a cell goes only as far as its writable rungs run (`stepRung`). Haze and smoke go through the stroke's lint guard (`StrokeLint`), and the guard decides what lands: after any dab the canvas has no ladder lint finding the stroke's start didn't have, and a cell moves only toward its rung of the field, never past it. After each dab the cells the stroke has reached and not yet taken to the field, within four columns and four rows of a cell of the dab's footprint (or an image's), are judged together, and the ones a finding blames step back a rung toward where they were (with their mirror images) until nothing is new; then each cell still short is tried alone a rung further, row by row, and the round repeats until nothing moves. Every keep moves each of its cells nearer the field, so the round ends, and when it does no cell in reach can go a rung further by itself (with its images) without a finding. A dab tries no other held cells: those further off wait for a dab near them, even when a cell this dab keeps is what frees them, so a dab's work is its neighbourhood's however long the stroke and never a chain across the canvas. What is held back at the stroke's end stays. A cell left at the field stays there under a second stroke, and a second stroke moves only cells the first left short, toward the field and never past it; it can move some, because the guard judges each stroke against its own start and its baseline rules (a wide rung the start had, one glyph, noise) don't add up across strokes (stars included: the noise a held star would add can be within a second stroke's start). A repeat that is always a no-op would need a stroke judged against something other than its start. So the density sets the field, not how much of it lands: that depends on the dab's shape, where it sits (a cell on an edge or in a corner has fewer neighbours to be a speck against), the order the stroke meets the cells, what is already on the canvas and the lint rules, and at high density on a flat canvas much of a stroke can be held back. For instance, at 100% on a blank 40×20 canvas, where the field is all ▒, a 3×3 dab in the middle lands nothing, while a 2×2 dab in the top left corner lands three ▒ and a ░. Under a mirror symmetry the field is folded about the axes as the texture stamp's is, so the field a stroke and its images uncover is its own mirror image, and the dab's images are painted and judged together (on a flat canvas, for instance, a mirrored drag paints the exact mirror image); a star whose mirror partner would be within four columns (two rows) across the fold is dropped. Radial images read the plain field at their own cells. Selection, the alpha lock and size limit the cells as for any brush; strength plays no part.
- **Clone stamp** (`CloneTool`, Tools > Clone Stamp, Ctrl+Shift+K; `brushes/clone.h`): Ctrl+click (either button) sets the source cell and paints nothing; the view draws a marker there. A stroke copies whole cells (glyph, both colours, attributes, transparency) from the source plus the distance from the stroke's first dab, through the core stroke, so size, tip, the selection and the alpha lock limit the cells it writes, as for any brush; strength and the ramp play no part. It reads the canvas as the stroke began, so a source overlapping the stroke copies what was there, not what the stroke just laid; a source cell off the canvas copies nothing. Aligned (Tool Options, on by default, kept between runs) keeps the first stroke's distance for every later stroke until a new source is set; with it off every stroke starts again at the source. A stroke dropped with Esc sets no distance. The source and the distance belong to the document: opening a file or a project, or New, forgets them whichever tool is active, and a press then paints nothing until a new Ctrl+click. While dragging, the marker follows the cell being copied to the pointer's cell, aligned or not; otherwise it sits on the source (the view has no hover tracking to show it before the press). Ctrl+click picks the source; Alt+drag moves the active layer (§5, §14). Under symmetry each image copies from that image of the source at that image of the distance (the copy is not flipped, so a mirror image of an asymmetric source is the source again, not its reflection); under the mirror modes the images are handled together, each cell taking the nearest image and the first copy kept; on a tie (a cell on an axis, or a dab on the axis of an odd canvas, where the images' dabs meet) the image whose frame has the cell in the first half across, then down, then the lower image, as the outline brush settles its overlaps. So a symmetric canvas stays symmetric, odd sizes and dabs on the axes included, with one limit: a cell on an axis is its own mirror and copies a cell off the axis as it is, so it can show a half block that the mirror would turn; radial copies through each fold rounded to the cell, on the default strongest-dab rule. Its own layer only (no sample merged). A cloned cell is a drawn cell, not a moved one, so it carries no lost-blink mark of the source's (`settleLostBlink`). No lint guard: it is an accent tool for repeating detail, and it copies cells the ladder already passed.
- **Symmetry** (mirror X/Y, both, radial).

**Common to all brushes**
- Respect layer alpha-lock and selection masks.
- Tablet pressure through `QTabletEvent` maps to size or strength.
- Live preview of the stroke before commit; one undo step per stroke.
- A Pixels layer can't hold a shade or any glyph but █ ▀ ▄ and a blank (§3), so a tool whose cells are shades declines a press on one, and its status bar text says why: the shade brush (░▒▓), the ramp shade, form, soft and smudge brushes, the rim-light brush, the texture stamp, the glyph spray's haze and smoke, and the ramp gradient (`DragTool::paintsShades`, #157). So does a tool whose glyphs it can't hold (`DragTool::unheldGlyphs`, #164): the glyph spray's stars, either button (the right button takes stars off, and the layer has none), and the glyph brush's left button when its glyph is one the layer can't hold, or when mirror mode would write one of its images that way (radial symmetry at 4 or 8 folds turns ▀ ▄ on their side, ▐ ▌; a mirror only flips them) (`pixelsUnheldGlyphs`, `core/` `brushes/drawing.h`, which the Frame tool and replay share, #436); its right button still erases. The spray's refusal follows its kind and the glyph brush's its glyph and the symmetry, and a setting changed mid-drag that the layer would refuse ends the drag, as the press would have been declined: the spray's kind, the symmetry, and the glyph brush's glyph picked with F1-F10 or the glyph bar (which otherwise redoes the drag with the new glyph). A glyph set switch picks no glyph, and a change of active layer or of a layer's kind lands the drag on the layer it was editing first. Before, the edit was dropped without a word when a cell of it was a shade or such a glyph, and landed when every cell was a whole colour (the ramp shade brush at full strength). A text layer is painted as a cells layer. The outline brush paints it with its shade rungs left out: where the darkest line would add a finding, a cell steps to the next whole colour or stays as it was, so the line it shows is the line that lands (before, a click that stepped a cell to a shade, K▓k, was dropped whole on release, its whole-colour cells too). The despeckle brush paints it as well: a speck takes a copy of a neighbour's cell, and on a pixel layer every cell is a pixel cell or clear. Typing isn't a press: a character the layer can't hold is refused at the edit (§3), still without a word.
- A cell changes once a stroke, with the strongest dab that covered it (below). The form, soft, despeckle and smudge brushes are the exceptions: the form brush lays the form near each dab again as the shape grows, the soft brush shades the stroke again round each dab, and the despeckle and smudge brushes read cells as the stroke has left them (a smudge builds up: going back over a stroke pulls further), so a cell painted earlier can take a new tone (a speck an earlier dab cleared can change again when a later dab clears its neighbour). None of them is a pure function of the footprint: a form dab's take-backs keep what earlier dabs laid, a soft stroke never takes paint off (Soft, above), a despeckle fix depends on the fixes before it, and what a smudge lays on what its tip picked up before, so the order of dabs can change a tone; replaying a stroke's dabs paints what playing them once does for the form and soft brushes, and the same dabs in the same order paint the same for all four. The form and soft brushes count every tone from the canvas as the stroke began, so nothing compounds, and for every brush the preview is what is committed.

In the core (`brush.h`): a stroke is a list of dabs joined by 4-connected lines, with a square or round tip (a round tip's rows count as two columns, and every size keeps at least the rows of a smaller one). The tip is 1 to 256 cells wide: a larger size, set or reached through pressure, is taken as 256, far more than an 80- or 160-column piece needs, so a joining line walks at most a few hundred points beyond the canvas. The unclipped footprint leaves out cells past the ends of int. Only the part of a joining line within reach of the canvas (the canvas grown by the tip's half width) is walked, in 64-bit arithmetic, so a dab far off the canvas costs nothing. The walk is the whole line's own cells: it starts from the point where the line comes within reach, with Bresenham's error term worked out there rather than stepped to, so a stroke paints exactly what stamping every dab of the line would. A joining line is walked from its lesser end (the higher on screen, then the further left) whichever way the stroke runs, stamping back to front when it runs the other way, so a line back over the same two dabs covers the same cells (Bresenham's line from A to B and from B to A can differ by a cell). A cell changes once a stroke, from its value when the stroke began, with the strongest dab that has covered it: a stronger dab (or a larger footprint reaching it) paints it again from that original value, and a weaker or equal one leaves it. So one pass of the shade brush moves a cell one rung however often it is crossed, and pressing harder deepens a cell rather than adding a pass; a build-up toggle may come later. A dab on the same cell as the last stamps again, so pressing harder without moving grows the brush or deepens the cells under it. A stroke gives a preview canvas and one `CellEdit` (the editor's undo type, each change with its before and after), and that edit is the undo step. Pressure scales size (size × p) or strength (rungs × p, rounded, never below one), so pressure to strength changes nothing at the default of one rung a pass; there is no randomness. Until layers exist, the cell's transparent flag stands in for alpha-lock. Symmetry (`StrokeOptions::symmetry`) is part of the stroke: every dab is stamped with its images (`symmetryDabs`), each image joined to that image of the last dab by a 4-connected line (the images' stamps go in turn, point k of every image and then k + 1, so a brush sees each point's images together), under the same once-a-stroke, strongest-dab rule, so where images overlap (a stroke across the centre) the one that covered a cell with the stronger dab paints it, and on a tie the lower image (the stroke itself first) keeps it, so a cell and its image take the same strength. A mirror or the half turn walks its joining line from the image of the end the stroke's own line is walked from, not from its own lesser end; Bresenham's steps don't depend on which way a line runs, so a mirrored drag is cell-exact, its cells the reflections of the stroke's, even where the reflection makes the other end the lesser (#125). A turn that is rounded walks its own line from its own lesser end. A dab carries which image it is and whether it is flipped (`Dab::image`, `flipX`, `flipY`); the tip is flipped with it, so an even-sized tip, which reaches a column further right than left (a row further down than up), paints the exact image, and the glyph brush flips its glyph the same way. Mirror X images column x at width − 1 − x, mirror Y row y at height − 1 − y, mirror X and Y makes both and the half turn; all three are about the canvas centre, in integer maths. Radial makes N turns (2–8) about the canvas centre or a set cell. Cells are twice as tall as they are wide, so radial turns on screen: a cell's offset from the centre is taken with its row doubled, turned, and the row halved again, and rounded to the nearest cell, halves down and right (floor(v + 0.5)); so a quarter turn takes two cells across to one cell down. The sines and cosines of the quarter and half turns are exact, and a half turn is the exact both-ways flip, its glyph flipped both ways. A quarter turn (at 4 or 8 folds) turns the glyph brush's glyph as the selection's Rotate does (`rotateGlyph`, clockwise on screen, `Dab::turns`), so ▀ turned a quarter clockwise is ▐; the tip is not turned. Other turns keep the dab's glyph and tip as they are (CP437 has no turned half blocks to match a third or a sixth of a turn), and at 3 or 5–8 folds rounding can leave a turned line with gaps or doubled cells where the turned dabs' lines join. Half-block pixels are already square, so the pixel tools (`symmetryPixels`) turn pixels about the centre in pixel rows with no aspect; a set cell is the centre of that cell, between its two pixels. An image off the canvas paints only what its tip reaches on it; a pixel image off the canvas is dropped. Each image is clipped on its own, so a dab or a pixel off the canvas still has the images that land on it. The images and the lines joining them are kept in 64 bits (`symmetryDabs` leaves out an image that lies beyond int, since a dab can't hold it; the stroke still joins it in 64 bits), so a stroke near the ends of int turns and joins as it would anywhere. The shapes and fill take their pixels through the same images one by one, so at those fold counts a turned shape can gap too. A shape tool rasterises its shape as far as its images can reach the canvas (`symmetryReach`: the canvas, and for radial the box round the canvas turned back by each fold, a pixel wider each way), so a shape dragged past the edge keeps the turned images of its part off the canvas. Typing and paste mirror only in mirror X (the old mirror mode, `CanvasView::mirrored`); the other modes repeat brush strokes, the pencil and the shapes only.

The rim-light brush is in `brushes/detail.h`. It reads the shape under the brush: the cells on the stroke's ramp other than the ground (the ramp's first colour when that is black), as they show (so with iCE off a bright background counts as its blinking dark colour), and the half blocks a shape tool or the pencil leaves at a shape's rim, two ramp colours or one and black. Black, RGB cells, other ramps' colours and blinking half blocks are outside. A half block with one half black is a fringe. A fringe is always an edge cell, and a full shape cell is one when a cell among its eight neighbours on the canvas is outside the shape or a fringe. Off the canvas is not outside: a shape running off the frame has no rim against the canvas edge, and nothing beyond the edge counts towards a normal. A fringe can't go a rung lighter (a half block has one colour a half, and a rung is a shade glyph between two colours), so the rim light leaves fringes as they are, and the rim falls on the full cells in from them: a full edge cell is lit when its own rim normal (below) faces the light, and nothing lights a cell whose rim normal faces away. Nor is a cell lit when the neighbour a step towards the light (a step along each axis the light comes from, so from the north-east the cell up and to the right) is a full shape cell while the neighbour a step away is outside or a fringe, whatever its normal sums to. A neighbour off the canvas is neither. That rule checks only one pair of neighbours, along the light's own step. A cell is also lit only past outside beside it on the light's side (#54): an outside sub-pixel (below) in one of its eight neighbours whose offset from the cell's centre, on screen, has a positive dot product with the light's direction (never zero: the offsets are odd multiples of a quarter column across and of half a column down). However the outside that reaches a cell sums, a cell whose outside all lies behind it is not lit: with a filled ellipse on pixels (3,3)–(6,9) and a filled rectangle on (8,7)–(10,9), (5,3)'s only outside beside it is the black half of the ▀ at (6,4), south-east of it, so it isn't lit from N, NE, W or NW, though the outside that reaches it, which runs on past (6,4) to the north-east, sums to a normal that faces NE (a dot of about 0.61). It came first for the column of a shape two columns wide that faces a one-column gap (three wide across a ▌/▐ half-column gap), whose window normal points across the shape; the rim normal now faces that column into the gap, so it lights from the gap's side, and the rule still keeps the top and bottom cells of such a narrow shape from being lit from across it. A shape drawn with the Ellipse tool whose top row is fringes is lit from the north on the row under them; the cells under its side fringes, which face west and east, are not.

The rim-light brush steps a full edge cell one rung lighter (`stepRung`, so iCE off is handled as for the ramp shade brush) when it faces the light. Its outward normal is taken on a grid of 2 × 2 sub-pixels a cell, a sub-pixel half a column wide and a column tall on screen: a full cell is four sub-pixels in the shape, ▀ and ▄ split a cell top and bottom, and ▌ and ▐ split it left and right. A sub-pixel is outside when its cell is outside the shape (the ground and every cell not of the shape), or it is in a fringe's black half; a fringe's coloured half is inside. The window normal (`edgeNormal`) is the sum of the offsets from the cell's centre to the outside sub-pixels of the cells within three columns' distance (cells at dx² + 4dy² ≤ 9: three columns each way on the cell's row, two on the rows above and below), each weighted a half, in a window round on screen so no direction is favoured, normalised. Weighted so, an outside cell adds twice its centre's offset and a ▀/▄ fringe's black half the offset of its half-block pixel, so full cells and ▀/▄ fringes have exactly the normals of the half-block pixel grid (a cell two pixels tall, a pixel a column square), and a shape of full cells the normals it would have taken a cell at a time. The rim light reads the rim normal (`rimNormal`, #54): the same sum over only the outside that reaches the cell, each region of outside sub-pixels in the window (joined by a side or a corner) that touches the cell's own sub-pixels, or, where none does (a full cell under a row of fringes whose coloured halves lie against it), each region with a sub-pixel in one of its eight neighbours. Outside past a narrow shape's far side, or past the coloured halves of fringes beside the cell's own outside, doesn't turn it: a column beside a gap faces the gap, a counter's inner wall faces the counter (with full cells on columns 10–14 and rows 3–5 and (12,4) empty, (13,4) lights from W, NW and SW only), and the full row of a bar three pixels tall, two columns or more in from its ends, faces down even with another shape a black pixel row under it (nearer the ends, see the known limits). With the fringes' black halves counted, light can't cross a fringe's black half onto the shape beyond it, a fringe on the canvas's first or last row has its own black half outside, and the full row of a bar three pixels tall under a row of fringes faces down, but for the cells at its ends (see the known limits). The window reaches a row either side of the cell, and a fringe row is the shape's last, so a shape whose fringes lie on the canvas's first or last row lights as it would further in. A ▌/▐ fringe's black half is outside in the same way, so a full cell under a ▌ or ▐ whose black half lies against black cells above faces north. A ▌/▐ column is not an empty column to the normal, as its coloured half is inside: with full cells on columns 2–3 and 5–7 and a ▌ on column 4, the window normal of (5,2) points east, where across an empty column it cancels; its rim normal, which reads only the gap, points west either way. With the rule above, the cells beside a ▌/▐ column between two rectangles (each one to five columns wide, one to eight rows tall) light from every point as across an empty column, but for their top and bottom cells, which can light where across an empty column they don't. Because off the canvas is not outside and the window reaches three columns, a shape with cells on the canvas's first or last three columns can light differently from the same shape further in, where the window finds outside cells there. With ▐ fringes on column 0 and full cells on 1–6, (1,1), (1,4), (2,1) and (2,4) light differently from W, NE and SE than they do a column in; with full cells on 1–3, the window normal of column 1's middle cells faces east, not west (their rim normal, which reads only the ▐'s black halves, faces west, so they light from the west as a column in does); and a stem a column wide is lit down its length from NE, E and SE on column 1 or 2, where from column 3 in only its ends light. Up and down the window reaches a row, so only a shape with full cells on the first or last row can light differently. The compass is on screen too, and a cell faces the light when its normal is within about 73° of it (dot product above 0.3), so the lit arc of a disc is centred on the light, to within about 6°: on a disc 41 columns across lit from a diagonal, the steep part of the rim near the light lights two cells wide, its inner cell seeing the light past the corner of the row above, while the steep part at the arc's far end lights one, its inner cell's only outside being the corner below, behind the light (cells-only normals would tip the arc about 19°). A small block reads as rounded: with the light from the north-west a block lights its top row and left column but not their far ends, which face north-east and south-west. It ignores strength and pressure (it is always one rung), and is one pass a stroke under the strongest-dab rule, so crossing a cell again in the same stroke changes nothing. It is an accent: it lights an edge cell one rung without regard to the ladder lint, so it can leave findings such as a rung skip or a speck, and the lint is the check, by default. Tool Options' *Keep the lint clean* (off by default, kept in the settings as `rimLight/keepLint`, #54) holds it to the lint instead: each stamp is judged by the stroke's lint guard (`StrokeLint`, §10 form brush), taken at the stroke's first dab and kept in the stroke's brush state, so a cell whose step would add a finding to the stroke's start (a rung skip, a speck) stays as it is. The stamp lights every cell the accent would light and, when the guard finds something, takes back the cells within a cell of each finding (or all of them when none is that near) as the form brush does, then tries each one taken back alone, in row order, while one lights; a cell the stamp tries is lit when lighting it alone adds nothing. A cell left unlit is tried again only by a later stamp that covers a cell beside it that it would light, so a cell that would be a speck alone can light with the next one along the rim; a stamp looks up only the eight neighbours of its own cells among the cells refused so far, so its work doesn't grow with the stroke. Under a mirror (X, Y or both) a cell and its images (its orbit: every cell the mirror makes of it, both axes under X and Y) light together or not at all, and a cell lights only when every image of it would: each unpainted by the stroke, inside the selection (and not alpha-locked), and lit by the rim-light rule from the same light, which is not mirrored. An orbit with an image outside the selection, or one the light doesn't reach (from N under mirror Y, a top-row cell's image on the bottom row faces S), stays as it is, so a symmetric canvas stays symmetric on every axis of the mirror. Off, the brush is byte for byte the accent it was.

Known limits of the rim light:
- Canvas edges: off the canvas is not outside, so a shape with cells on the canvas's first or last three columns, or full cells on its first or last row, can light differently from the same shape further in (above).
- ▌/▐ gaps: the cells beside a ▌/▐ column between two shapes light as across an empty column but for their top and bottom cells, which can light where across an empty column they don't (above).
- Thin bars by their ends: with ▄ fringes on rows 1 and 3 and full cells on rows 2 and 4 (two bars three pixels tall, a black pixel row apart), a full-row cell of the upper bar at either end or a column in from it can face up or sideways: within its window the outside below joins the outside past the upper fringes round the bar's end, so all of it reaches the cell. On bars on columns 5–7, (6,2)'s rim normal is (0, −1): it is lit from N, NE and NW and not from S. Two columns or more in from both ends (bars one to twelve columns long, the lower one shifted up to two columns or left out), the row is lit from S and not from N, NE or NW.
- Keep the lint clean leaves diagonal rims unlit: on a staircase edge the rim lights one cell a row, and each such cell is lighter than every neighbour, a speck, so only the straight runs light (on the tests' random shaded shapes, without a mirror, it lights about four in five of the cells the accent lights). On a flat shape of █ with no ░▒▓ the one-glyph rule caps it at a few cells.
- Keep the lint clean under radial symmetry is judged a stamp at a time, not as orbits. (Under a mirror it lights the images of every stamp, as despeckle does; a mirror's joining lines are the exact images of the stroke's own, so those are the images' own stamps and it lights only cells the accent's stroke reaches.)
- Keep the lint clean under a mirror lights an orbit only when the light reaches every image, and the light isn't mirrored: with the light from a point the mirror doesn't keep (N under mirror Y, NW under mirror X), only cells whose images also face it light, so a flat top row under mirror Y lights nothing from N, and under mirror X from NW only rim cells that face both NW and NE (near the top) light.
- Keep the lint clean lights all it safely can only among the cells each stamp tries: after a stamp, every cell it tried (the cells it covers that the accent lights, and the cells left unlit before beside them; under a mirror each with its images) is lit or would add a finding lit alone. It does not hold across the canvas. A cell left unlit is tried again only when a later stamp covers a cell beside it that it would light, so a change further off that makes it safe doesn't light it: on two flat blocks, rung 3 with one cell at rung 2 and rung 8, the rung-2 cell can't step while the blocks show only ▓ and █ (one shade glyph), and once a stamp on the other block brings in ░ it stays unlit until a stamp comes back beside it. Two cells that would each add a finding alone and nothing together light only when one stamp tries both.
- Grazing cells on rotated ellipses: the rule can leave a cell whose normal faces the light just over the threshold. On an ellipse with semi-axes 9 and 3 columns turned 35°, (5,3)'s normal has a dot of 0.316 with NW, but (4,2) is full and (6,4) a fringe, so it isn't lit from NW. The ellipse's own normal where its edge crosses the cell has a dot of about 0.17 with NW, so leaving it unlit is defensible.

The ladder (`ramp.h`) holds palette colours only; an RGB cell is off every ramp. A shade can be written either way round (B▓A shows as A░B). The brush writes the way that keeps the background dark (0–7) when only one way does, so a piece needs no iCE colours for it; otherwise it keeps the way the cell was written, lighter on top for new shades. Two bright neighbours need a bright background either way. With iCE off the ramp brush never writes such a rung: it reads each cell as it shows (a bright background is its dark colour, blinking, so off the ramp), stops at the last rung short of its target that keeps the background dark, and leaves the cell when there is none (`stepRung` with `ice` false). `cli shade` opens the file as the editor does (`openAsEditor` in `ansi.h`, which `Document::read` calls too: a file without the iCE flag that holds a bright background opens with iCE on, which stays on through the save even when the shading removes the last bright background, and its blinking cells lose their blink, a loss the save counts unless the glyph is blank) and shades with the document's iCE colours, so a non-iCE piece stays non-iCE; `--ice` turns them on first and the save says so, and it refuses to write a piece whose blinking cells would lose their blink to that unless `--allow-lossy`. Step counts saturate at the ramp's ends.

The ladder lint (`lint.h`) measures pixel `noise` (at most 0.02, from smaug's 0.033 → 0.016 despeckle), with each row's trailing black spaces trimmed as in the written file, and adds the ladder rules:
- Shades may only join ramp neighbours; a shade of two colours of the ramp that aren't neighbours is flagged whether or not it blinks, and other ramps' colours aren't judged.
- No shade rung may be wider than 3 cells along the gradient (rungs are 1–3 cells wide *along the gradient*, so a band reads as a turn, not a stripe). Only a step of a gradient is judged: a run of a shade rung, across a row or down a column, with a lower rung at one end and a higher one at the other. Its width is the shorter of that run and the rung's longest run the other way, in cells. A flat field of one shade and the plane at a gradient's end aren't judged; black is ink or the ground, not the gradient's dark end, so a plane that runs into black is a plane at the end, as at the canvas edge.
- No rung skips. Each row and each column is read as a sequence of levels (runs of one rung); a jump of more than one rung between two levels is a skip when the level before it or the one after it goes on the same way, so three or more levels climb or fall together (bands 3, 5, 8). Two solid colours side by side are judged like any other levels, so solid DarkGrey then solid White at the top of a climbing gradient (1 2 3 4 then 12) is a skip. An outline that turns back (10, 11 then 8), two levels meeting (a fold, two planes: solid DarkGrey against solid White with no third level, or White against a w▒K face) and black against anything (ink, seams, the silhouette: black and cells off the ramp end a sequence) aren't skips. The one end level left out of a sequence is the outline brush's line: a level at either end (against black, off the ramp or the canvas edge) that is one cell wide, solid, and in the ramp colour next below the darker colour of the level beside it, so DarkGrey round bands 9 to 12 is clean. Along a diagonal the line is a 4-connected staircase, two cells on a row. Where the level inside changes along the rim, the line may go on in another line colour (any solid colour but black, against the outside), as a lit rim does (craft `buildings.md`). Every other end level is a rung like the rest, a lighter one (a highlight) included, so a single White cell after 1 2 3 4, and 8, 10, 12 between black, are skips.
- No specks (a lone non-black, non-glint pixel reads as dirt): a cell lighter or darker than all of its neighbours on the ladder, judged only where it has three or more, so the corners of a gradient and the ends of a band aren't. Black cells (ink, a rivet, an eye) and the ramp's top rung (a glint, the highlight) are never specks. Pixel noise can't see shade cells, so this is the speckle rule for shading. Known gap: stripes of two shades a column or a row each are not specks (every cell has a neighbour of its own rung), and noise doesn't see them either; an eye for regular repeats is what catches them for now. Known limits the other way: the one-cell peak of a ball that stops below the top rung is a speck (one), and a one-wide diagonal line one rung off its field is all specks. The ramp gradient's Bayer dither is exempt (#160, `lint::ditherExempt`): a cell the dither laid (it carries `Cell::dithered`) is not a speck while every neighbour it has on the ladder is one rung from it, as the dither lays them. The exemption is the mark, not the pattern: a lone cell by hand or from any other tool has no mark and is a speck as before, beside a dithered fill or inside it, and so is a dithered cell whose neighbours are redrawn further off. `checkLadder`, `LadderIndex` (each speck slot) and the despeckle brush (`speckFixes`) all apply it, so the Lint dock, `cli lint`, the guards and despeckle agree. A drawn cell keeps the mark only while it looks the same (`settleMarks`, `Stroke::set`); a move or copy carries it; CharsOnly and ColoursOnly composites clear it, since the cell they make is not the dither's. A project saves the marks (`"dither"`, §5), so a dithered fill reloads exempt. An `.ans`, XBIN or any other art file has no place for them, so a dithered fill saved as one and read back (by the editor or `cli lint`) lints its dithered cells as specks: those bytes are the same as a hand-placed dither, which this rule calls specks, and guessing the dither back from the pattern would exempt hand specks that happen to fall on it. Keep the project to keep the exemption.
- A form needs more than one shade glyph.
- No fallback specks (#71; `LadderOptions::fallback`, off by default): a ▀/▄ cut into a shade cell where neither half shows the pixel's colour or one the shading round it shows there. The lint reads the canvas alone, with no layers, so it infers the shade a cell was from the cells above and below it and the ends of its run of equal cells along the row (black is ink, not shading): a half is judged against the rung of the cell it faces, or the rung two of those three agree on, or else within one rung of each. It fits when it shows a colour of one of those rungs (solid, ░'s background, ▓'s foreground, either of ▒'s; with iCE off and the other half bright, also their dark counterparts), has fallen when it shows another colour one of those rungs could fall back to when a pixel cuts it (either colour of a shade glyph's step, and with iCE off their dark counterparts; a solid rung has none), and was drawn otherwise. The cell is a speck when every half judged has fallen and every half not judged goes on into the cell it faces (that cell's facing half shows the same colour: the pixel is the edge of a shape there), and is judged only with a shade glyph among those cells. A cell with a drawn half was drawn there: a bevel over its outline, a frame's border, a sun's stripes (PR #99's review: cocktober's steel row 67, battlestation's and outrun-sunset's sunset rows 3 and 12, 76, 44 and 32 findings when any colour that didn't fit counted). A lone pixel cut into the shading, its other half fallen too, can't be told from a drawn one, nor can one whose other half faces ink, the canvas's edge or a cell off the ladder, so none of these is flagged. On the 335 real pieces (`tests/data` and funkpower's) linted against all ten ramps it finds 252, down from 4,072; 220 of them are a black outline drawn as a row of halves against shading, which reads as fallen because black is the low colour of the ramps' first step and has to stay a fallback colour for the sky's specks below. On PR #68's scenes plotted flat, with iCE on, it finds all 26 of the sky's specks and 8 of the wall's 12, and nothing else; composited, none. Known gaps: a lone pixel cut into the shading (above), a cell the plot made a black space, whose free half shows the pixel's own black, and a cut cell on the bottom row with nothing below to read, and half-block texture pixelled by hand beside shade glyphs, the other 32 of the 252 (#110: spruce-island's ▀ DarkGrey on Grey under ▓ at steel 84,21, music-of-the-ainur's ▀ Yellow on LightCyan at fire 109,12), whose halves show colours the shading beside them falls to, so they read as fallen though every pixel was drawn. With iCE off a free half fallen to the dark counterpart of the shading's colours passes, as the composite's does, so it finds 10 down the sky and 4 on the wall, each a speck by PR #68's counts too. The covered half not showing its pixel's colour (PR #68's F1) can't be told from the canvas alone: a canvas has no pixels, only cells. `LadderIndex` keeps them a change at a time, a changed row judged again whole and the cells above and below a changed cell, O(W) each. Off by default because the brushes' guards don't place them yet (the outline brush's blame, `brushes/detail.cpp`, has no rule for them; its size sweep holds with them on); the lint panel (Phase 4) turns it on.

A brush that guards against the lint asks what its dab adds, not what the canvas has. `LadderIndex` holds a canvas's whole-canvas findings and moves with it a change at a time. `apply(after, changed)` returns what the change adds as a guard judges it: a wide rung unless the canvas had one the same way (across or down), on the same line, of the same rung, whose run shares a cell with it; a skip unless the canvas had one the same way, on the same line, between the same two cells, or between the same two rungs with each of its levels sharing a cell with the old one's (`LadderIndex::Skip::matches`), so a level grown or shrunk at the boundary moves the skip and it is the skip that was, while a skip of another pair of rungs a cell over is added, and so is one whose level moved past the old one's (#66); any other finding whose place (`LadderFinding::Place`: kind, cell and other cell) had none of that kind; one glyph where the canvas didn't use one glyph only; and noise when it is over `maxNoise` and went up. Past the rungs those rules name, the message and the numbers in it (a rung's width, the rungs of a skip that stays between the same two cells, the noise) aren't part of the match, so a dab that narrows or widens a wide rung at either end, joins two runs or splits one, or lowers the noise, adds nothing; a guard won't flag a finding that gets slightly worse in place, and the lint panel still shows it. A band repainted to another rung is added, and so is a step that moves from a row to a column at one cell. The whole-canvas list (`findings()`, `checkLadder`) compares findings on every field. `added` asks without moving (an apply and the update back, so a guard that keeps most dabs applies and puts the cells back on a refusal), and `addedFindingsOnce(before, after, ramp, changed)` is the one-off that builds an index of `before` (whole-canvas cost; a stroke keeps one index). `wideRungs(across, line)` lists the held canvas's wide steps along one row or column in order, O(W) or O(H), for a guard that compares with an earlier canvas than the one held; `skips(across, line)` and `skipsAdded(across, line, was)` do the same for skips. Both rules are judged against the dab's canvas, and neither is transitive (a level that slides a cell per dab ends up sharing no cell with where it started), so a stroke's guard reads them against the stroke start's lists, not the canvas before the dab, as far as it goes: `StrokeLint` judges skips on the rows and columns within two of a change, and wide rungs on the rows and columns through a changed cell where the start had a wide rung; the Outline brush's composite judges skips that way, but wide rungs only against the composite before it (`apply`), so a run of one rung that slides a cell a composite, each overlapping the one before, is never added, however far it ends from the start's run (steel, a rung-5 run moved from columns 1–4 to 5–8 a column a composite: no finding, where the start's lists would add one on each row). No Outline gesture is known to slide a run so; if one turns up, the composite should read wide rungs as `StrokeLint` does. `maxRung` below 1 is refused (`LintError`), since a one-cell run's steps across and down end at the same cell. `apply` refuses a canvas of another size, and a debug build also refuses one that differs at a cell `changed` doesn't name; an index belongs to one canvas and is taken again after an edit it wasn't told of. Each place has one slot (a cell's shade or speck, a run's start across and its start down for wide steps, so a cell has two, a line's skip list in order along it, and the canvas's one-glyph and noise findings), and a change re-judges only the slots it can reach; on a changed line the old and new skips, and the old and new wide steps (each slot keeps its run's rung), merge in one pass along it. The one-glyph and noise findings are judged on the whole canvas, never on a crop: the index keeps the glyph tally and each pixel's share of the noise (counted, lonely) and updates them a cell at a time, so both are exact. Each row's run widths are bucketed so the longest run the other way is a few word reads, and a change costs O(W + H) reads per changed cell (the end-level rule reads two cells across a line, so the rows and columns within two of a change judge their runs and skips again), plus, for noise, the change in a row's trimmed length (it scans back over blanks to find the new end). Taking the index is a whole-canvas pass, about a third of a second at 400×5000, so a stroke on a very tall piece starts with that stall. The cost is counted in reads (`cellsRead`) and tested to grow linearly with the canvas; a randomized test diffs `apply` against two whole-canvas lints over tens of thousands of changes, a hundred indexes taking 200-400 changes each with undos among them.

`cli shade` runs one pass over a selection and prints the check of its bounds; it exits 0 with findings (`lint` exits 4, §9). `--ramp` takes a name or palette indices; `--ice` turns iCE colours on. The selection is `--box`, `--mask <piece>` (a piece the size of the input whose non-blank cells, as transparent paste reads them, are the shape; another size is a usage error), both (the mask's cells in the box) or, for the rung step only, the whole canvas: the other modes need one. The pass is one mode (#107): the ramp brush's rung step (`--darken`, `--steps`; the default), `--form dome|cylinder|plane` (the form brush, the selection its shape, with `--glint` for the Brush dock's Glint, off unless asked for), `--despeckle`, or `--rim` (the rim-light brush as the editor's default sets it: an accent, not held to the lint). `--light` (a compass point, NW by default) is where the form and the rim light come from. Form, despeckle and rim are one full-strength dab of a square tip covering the selection, as one dab over a selection in the editor; a selection over 255 cells wide or tall takes a dab per 255-cell square. The form keeps its lint guard, so a pass adds no ladder finding the input didn't have, and despeckle's guard does the same.

## 11. Workspace layout

The GUI follows GIMP and Photoshop: tools and their settings live in docks beside the canvas, not in the menu bar. The menus keep every command and shortcut, but the docks are where you work. The Tools toolbar the toolbox replaced is gone.

| Dock | Where | Holds |
|---|---|---|
| Toolbox | left | One icon button per tool in the Tools menu, or per group of tools, laid in rows as wide as the column fits (seven at the default 360 pixels, five at 256, two at the narrowest) and as tall as those rows need: the column's width sets the rows, and the rows set the dock's height. The buttons are the Tools menu's own actions, so each has the tool's existing shortcut (Ctrl+B half-block brush, Ctrl+L line, Ctrl+J fill and so on) in its tooltip and the toolbox adds no shortcuts. Five groups share a button: the brushes that lay a colour or glyph down (half-block, shade, glyph, colorize), the ramp brushes (ramp shade, form, soft, despeckle, smudge), the detail brushes (outline, rim light, texture stamp, clone stamp, glyph spray), the shapes (line, rectangle, ellipse) and the fills (fill, ramp gradient). Clicking a group's separate arrow opens its flyout; right-click also opens it. Clicking the icon selects the group's current tool. The button shows the last one picked, from anywhere. To a screen reader a group's button is a drop-down button named after its current tool, with two actions, each listed once: Press, which picks that tool as a click does, and Show Menu, which opens the flyout; the flyout is its one child, so its tools can be reached from it. The active tool is shown pressed. A tool in no group gets a button of its own (the Text tool's, Ctrl+Shift+X). The icons (#224, `app/tool_icon.h`): the generic tools (keyboard, select, move, line, rectangle, ellipse, fill, eyedropper, text, clone stamp, glyph spray) use Tabler Icons (MIT, `third_party/tabler-icons/`, built in as Qt resources); the ANSI-specific ones (the half-block, shade, glyph and colorize brushes, the ramp brushes, outline, rim light, texture stamp and ramp gradient) are drawn in code on Tabler's 24-unit grid and 2-unit round stroke. An icon engine draws each icon when asked, at the size and device pixel ratio asked for (QIcon hands the engine the size in device pixels before Qt 6.8 and in logical pixels from 6.8; the engine takes either), in a palette's colours: each toolbox button has icons of its own drawn in the button's palette, so a palette set on the toolbox reaches them, and the Tools menu's actions are drawn in the application palette. ButtonText on Button for Normal, Active and On, the Disabled group's ButtonText for Disabled, HighlightedText on Highlight for Selected; fills and ramps are tones between the two, lit sides toward the lighter. Colorize keeps its red, green and blue, outlined in the foreground colour. On a palette or theme change (PaletteChange, ApplicationPaletteChange, ThemeChange) the toolbox gives every button and every tool action a new icon, so the buttons and the Tools menu repaint. A test holds every tool's icon to 3:1 against Button on a light and a dark palette (and against Highlight when selected), and the toolbox's icons to 3:1 against the toolbox's own Button when its palette differs from the application's. |
| Tool Options | left, under the toolbox | Only the settings the active tool reads, under the tool's name: size, tip, strength (the ramp shade, form, soft and smudge brushes), pressure, ramp, light direction, the form brush's form, rim light and glint, the rim-light brush's *Keep the lint clean*, the soft brush's hardness, the outline brush's *Outline the selection*, the texture stamp's kind, density and seed, the clone stamp's Aligned, the glyph spray's kind, density and seed, the glyph brush's glyph, the ramp gradient's shape (linear or radial), From and To rungs (0 to the ramp's top) and banding, alpha lock (the active layer's, §5), sample merged (fill and the eyedropper), the half-block pencil's 4-connected toggle, symmetry mode with radial's folds and centre, Filled Shapes for the rectangle and ellipse, for the select tool the paste mode, for the Move/Transform tool the active reference layer's X, Y, Width, Height, Keep aspect and Scale (§4) and, for the Text tool, the face picker, case, letter and line spacing, alignment, wrap width and colour (§15). Keyboard mode has none. Filled and the paste mode follow their menu items both ways. A tool with no entry shows every brush setting. Short settings share a row (`app/flow_row.h`, #236): size and tip, pressure and strength, light direction and form, rim light and glint, the gradient's shape and banding and its From and To rungs, the texture stamp's and glyph spray's density and seed, *Outline the selection*, alpha lock and sample merged, symmetry and folds, the tool's name and the help button (stacked beside the picture while it shows, the picture going under them when the column is too narrow for both), and for the Text tool big style and colours, colour and case, wrap width and alignment, letter and line spacing, and the face picker's search and type filter. The rows sit 4 px apart. A row's settings sit side by side while the column is wide enough and wrap under each other when it isn't, so the narrowest default column (256) never scrolls sideways, help shown or not (a long tool name wraps beside the help button); a setting the tool doesn't read hides with its label, and a row with none shown takes no room. The forms holding the rows are `FlowForm`s, which work their row heights out afresh at each new size, because Qt's form layout can reuse heights worked out for another width and draw a wrapped row over the next one. |
| Glyph bar | bottom | The current F-key set as ten clickable cells (a click sends the canvas the F-key itself, so it does exactly what the key does with the active tool: in keyboard mode it types the glyph, with the glyph brush it picks the brush's glyph) and a dropdown of the sets the typing policy offers (16, or 20 under Unicode). The status bar keeps its F-key line. |
| Preview, Palette | right | As before. The palette shows the pen as GIMP's overlapping foreground/background swatches, with a swap arrow (Colours > Swap Colours, no shortcut unless one is given in Preferences > Shortcuts) and a reset corner (Ctrl+D, as before); the two tooltips name the key their Colours item has. |
| Layers | right, between the palette and the Library | The layer panel (§5): the layers top first with visibility, name, alpha lock and kind, and New Cells Layer, New Pixels Layer, Delete, Raise and Lower under them.
