A short hint distinguishes glyphs and shades from half-block drawing; the add buttons and each kind's tooltip explain what it holds, compatible tools, compositing and export. The Layer menu shows the add items' tooltips too, and their status tips give the hint's half for their kind. Under those, while the active layer is a generator, its parameter form and Run (§7): a layer's own properties live here, beside the panel that edits the rest of them, not in Tool Options, which follows the tool. |
| Library | right | §12. |
| Lint | right, under the Library, tabbed with the IRC View and in front | The whole lint (§9) of the canvas under every named ramp and the craft pairs (as `cli lint` with no `--ramp`) or the one ramp picked: a row per finding (cell, kind, ramp, message) and the summary. Clicking a row, or Enter on it, puts the cell cursor on its cell and scrolls the canvas to it. |
| IRC View | right, a tab behind Lint | The IRC view (§8) of the lines File > Export IRC Text writes (the cells with the document's iCE setting, View > IRC's Fit, Pad and Swap Shades) at 1×, at the document's letter spacing, cut at the document's budget, the row meter's (View > IRC > Budget..., saved with a project; 407 bytes, EFnet via WeeChat, on a flat file or new canvas until it is changed), and how many rows are cut. It follows the canvas's scroll, both axes, always, with no toggle (the maintainer's ruling on #394): on every scroll, zoom or size change of the canvas it scrolls so that its left column and top row are the canvas viewport's, by cell coordinates (the canvas zoomed, the dock at 1×; the view's column x and row y at the canvas's column x and row y) and clamped to its own scroll range. The canvas's cell is the one under the viewport's top-left corner on the canvas's own device grid, at any device pixel ratio. The rule is about coordinates, not content: a lone wide cell takes two columns in the view and pushes the rest of its row a column right, so the view's content there can sit a column right of the canvas's. It is one way: a scroll of the dock by hand (something the user did to it: the bars, their context menu's items, Scroll here included, the wheel, the keys, a pan gesture, a value set through the bars' accessible interface by an assistive technology; a value set by code, such as its own lining up, a clamp into a new range or `ensureVisible`, is not one) holds, through edits, a resize and a hide and show, until the canvas next scrolls (by any amount, a part of a cell too), zooms or changes size; the canvas only centred again in a resized viewport, as when a side column is widened, is none of those. Otherwise it lines up again whenever its range changes, so a dock shown or resized after the canvas scrolled is lined up. Its toggle is in View > IRC under the Row Meter's, as well as in the Window menu. |

- Both side columns resize by dragging the separator beside the canvas, with their widths saved in the layout. The default widths are 360 logical pixels on the left and 360 on the right, as space permits: every tool's options fit 256 without scrolling sideways, and the wider column wraps the help in Tool Options in fewer lines. The initial size request is bounded by the screen work area. When no current saved layout exists, the defaults are applied on first show and follow subsequent window sizing, including a window manager fitting the window after show, until a column is manually resized. Window > Reset Layout applies the defaults again and resumes this sizing policy. The canvas viewport keeps a floor of 640 logical pixels, enough for an 80-column piece at 1×, after allowing for the frame, scrollbar and IRC gutter. If the full defaults would cross that floor, the left column gives up its width above 256 first, then the right column shrinks to its native minimum, and only then does the left column go under 256 (so a 1366-pixel screen gets a left column of about 290 and a 1280-pixel one 256); if even the minimums cannot leave 640 pixels, both columns use their minimums. A column's native minimum counts only the docks open in it, so a closed or floating dock doesn't hold it open; opening or closing a dock in a side column while the defaults are being followed applies them again, so a closed Layers dock gives its width back to the canvas and reopening it brings its minimum back. A column is sized and measured through its first open, docked dock in front (the Toolbox, or Tool Options while the Toolbox is closed or a tab behind it; the preview, or the next dock down while it floats), never through a closed dock or a tab behind another, whose stale widths Qt no longer lays out. Only the user sizing a column ends the policy: dragging a separator so that a column's width changes, or a horizontal `resizeDocks` on a side dock (`MainWindow` has its own, so a width asked for from outside is the user's). The window watches its own mouse presses for the drag, since `QMainWindow` has no signal for it. Nothing else is a manual resize, however it moves a column: Qt widening or narrowing one for a dock's minimum, bringing a tab forward, a window resize while the defaults for an opened or closed dock are still queued, a dock opened and closed again in one turn, and a drag of the separator between two docks in a column, which moves heights only. After any of them the columns keep following the defaults. These are starting widths, not fixed constraints: manual resizing and a current saved layout retain the user's widths. A restored layout keeps its widths exactly unless they would push the canvas viewport below the 640 floor (#244), as a layout saved from a wide window with 360/360 columns does on a 1280×720 screen. Then both columns shrink only as far as the floor needs, each in proportion to its saved width and never below its native minimum, the other column giving what one at its minimum can't; if even the minimums cannot leave 640 pixels, both columns use their minimums, as the defaults do. The clamp runs when the window first shows, and again for each size the window takes before it is first exposed, since a window manager fitting or maximizing the window on mapping sizes it after `show()`; each of those starts from the saved widths, so a window widened then gets them back. Once the window is on screen a resize is the user's and is never clamped, and the clamp never makes the restored layout follow the defaults. Window > Reset Layout restores these size-aware widths as well as the dock positions and visibility. The palette keeps enough width for every swatch but can expand with its column; the 1× preview scrolls independently of the column width.
- Default heights (#225, #226) are applied with the widths and follow the same rule, per dock area. In the left column the Toolbox dock is its rows and title bar, nothing more, and Tool Options takes the rest of the column (from 1920×1080 up every tool's settings and help show without scrolling at the test platform's 9 pt font and at the 10 pt a desktop gives by default, #236); the Toolbox dock doesn't grow with the window (it can still be dragged taller), so a taller window is all Tool Options', and a narrower column that gives the Toolbox another row applies the heights again. In the right column the palette takes its size hint; the preview the 1× height of an 80×25 piece (its frame and horizontal scrollbar included), at most a third of the column; Layers its controls with six layer rows; Lint, and the IRC View in its tab, the Lint panel with eight finding rows; the Library takes what is left (the lists ask for those rows, `app/row_tree.h`, so a panel at its default shows them and its controls without scrolling). The glyph bar takes its size hint. When the column is short of every target, the Library gives way first, then Lint and the IRC View, then the preview, each down to its minimum, and Layers only when all of those are at theirs; the window minimum is still the 660 pixels below. At 1920×1080 this leaves the Library at its minimum and Lint under its eight rows; at 2560×1440 every target fits and the Library has about 250 pixels. A dock moved into a column from elsewhere keeps its height there. The heights are applied again when the window is resized, when a dock opens or closes, after a separator drag (for the areas the drag didn't end) and on Window > Reset Layout. Only the user sizing a dock's height ends them, for that dock area only: dragging a separator between two docks in a column ends that column's, the glyph bar's separator ends the bottom area's, and a vertical `resizeDocks` ends the area of each dock it names. A drag of the separator beside the canvas changes widths only and leaves the heights followed. A current saved layout keeps the user's heights and follows none.
- No single-key tool shortcuts: letters type in keyboard mode, which stays the default. The toolbox uses the Ctrl shortcuts the tools already have.
- Quick tool keys (#149): the shortcuts are user-assignable instead, in Edit > Preferences > Shortcuts, under the same rules. Single-key tools only outside keyboard mode would make one key draw or type depending on the active tool, and a modal key layer would add the mode switch §3 avoids; neither fits keyboard mode as the default. The page lists every action that can have a key (`bindableActions`: the sheet's actions with an objectName of their own, not separators or submenus) under its menu, with a search field. An action a document's policy hides is one of them (#400): it carries the `bwOfferedWhen` property (`kOfferedWhenProperty`, `app/shortcuts_dialog.h`), a phrase saying where it is offered, and is listed and keeps its key whether it is shown or not. The glyph sets 17–21 carry "in IRC and UTF-8 documents", since `MainWindow::updateGlyphSet` shows the first `glyphSetCount(view_->typingPolicy())` sets and the policy is Unicode "under the IRC target or a UTF-8 .ans encoding" (`typingPolicy`, `core/include/blockwright/target.h`). While it is hidden its row reads "<name> (hidden: offered only in IRC and UTF-8 documents)" in grey, and its tooltip and the Keyboard Shortcuts sheet's say that its key is kept and works there; the sheet is modeless, so an open one adds or drops that note when the policy shows or hides the action. A key is a conflict whether the action that has it is shown or not: a hidden action's key is refused to another action as any other's is, a hidden action is refused a key another has, and at startup a kept key for a hidden action wins over another action's default and is dropped when an earlier action already keeps it, as for a shown one. Qt fires a hidden action's key only once it is shown, so the key does nothing in a document that hides it, and the glyph-set line's tooltip names only the keys of the sets offered; for the action picked, a key field taking one key, No Key, Default and Reset All. A key is refused, with the reason under the list, when keyboard mode types or edits with it (no Ctrl or Meta; F1 to F10 and Shift+F2 to F10, the glyph keys; Shift+F1 stays What's This), when the desktop takes it (Alt with an F-key, Ctrl with an F-key but no Shift, Ctrl+Alt with an F-key, Ctrl+Alt+Del and Ctrl+Alt+Backspace), when Ctrl+Alt is held with a character (AltGr on Windows), or when another action has it there; an action's own default keys are always allowed back (the platform's Undo keys include Alt+Backspace and the Undo key). Nothing changes until OK. Only keys that differ from the default are kept, in the settings under `shortcuts/<objectName>` in portable text, an empty value for none. At startup a kept key wins over another action's default, which loses it; a kept key that is refused or that an earlier action already keeps is dropped. Keyboard mode's editing and cursor keys (the arrows, Home, End, Return, Enter, Backspace and Del) do nothing with Ctrl or Meta held, as Ctrl+arrows already step the pen, so a chord of one an action is given takes nothing from it. The tooltips that name a key (each tool's, Tool Options' size, symmetry and Filled, the palette's swap and reset, the glyph-set line's for every set key) and Edit > Delete's menu text, which names the tool's Del after any key the action is given, are rebuilt when it changes, and an open Keyboard Shortcuts sheet is reloaded on OK. The View > IRC actions (Row Meter, IRC View, Budget..., Fit Lines to the Budget, Pad Lines, Swap Shades) have no default keys, by the maintainer's ruling (#396): they are occasional commands, so they are left unbound, and a user binds them in Edit > Preferences > Shortcuts. Audit: Ctrl+[ and Ctrl+] already step the brush size; X (swap colours) stays unbound by default, since X types, and Swap Colours can be given a key; Space-drag panning is out, since Space types, and a middle-button drag pans the canvas in its scroll area instead (any tool, not while another button's drag is on). The pan is a drag of the device that pressed it, as a tool's is: the other device's events, a pen's included, are refused while it lasts, and the undo key or a lost focus ends it where it is. The canvas's own drag cancel still matches the platform Undo key as before.
- Help > Keyboard Shortcuts... lists every action with a key, grouped by its menu (or its dock, for one in no menu), with a search field that filters by name or key; Tab goes from the search field to the list, whose rows the arrow keys and Page Down move through. It reads the window's actions each time it opens, so it can't drift from them: those in its menus, those added to the window or a widget in it (whoever owns them) and those it owns, each once. The window has one sheet, hidden when closed and reloaded when opened again. It has no key of its own (F1, the standard help key, types a glyph in keyboard mode), and no two of those actions share a key: the guard test reads the same list as the sheet.
- `blockwright --list-actions` (#209, `app/action_list.h`) prints every action as JSON and exits, for the docs site's shortcuts and menu pages and the steps files #208's replay reads. The list comes from the window, not a table kept beside it: a window is built offscreen and never shown, under scratch settings (`ScratchSettings`) so the keys are the defaults and nothing is written, and the dump is the sheet's own walk (`shortcutEntries`) with the unkeyed actions kept, in menu order then docks then "Other", restricted to the actions Preferences > Shortcuts can rebind (`bindableActions`), which take in the actions a policy hides (#400): the dump's window has a new ANSI document, which hides glyph sets 17–21, and they are listed all the same, after set 16. Each action's id is its objectName, the stable name `shortcuts/<id>` already keys; renaming one breaks kept shortcuts and steps files, so ids don't change. Each entry has the id, menu (and path), text, portable shortcuts, any key its menu text names that isn't its own (Edit > Delete's Del), status tip, tooltip, checkable and checked, and for a tool its tool id and #153 help id (`tools/<id>`), and for an action a policy can hide where it is offered (`offeredWhen`, "in IRC and UTF-8 documents" for sets 17–21; absent for the rest, so the format is otherwise unchanged). Standard keys (Quit, Undo, Redo, New, Open, Save, Save As) come from Qt's platform theme, and offscreen's has the Windows keys (no key for Quit, Ctrl+Y for Redo), so in this mode `standardKeys()` (`app/shortcut_settings.h`) gives a generic Linux desktop's instead (Qt's X11 and GNOME scheme, which the window gets on xcb or Wayland with no theme or GNOME's), and such an entry also names its `standardKey` for pages about other platforms. The running app still takes Qt's keys; a test holds the table to what Qt gives the window under the X11 scheme. The table follows the Qt the app runs on (`QLibraryInfo::version()`), not one Qt's: Qt 6.10 added the dedicated keys (New, Open, Save, Shift+Save, Undo, Redo, Exit), so on Qt 6.7 to 6.9 (Debian 13 has 6.8) Undo is Ctrl+Z and F14 and Redo only Ctrl+Shift+Z. It is a table rather than read from Qt because a scheme's keys are reachable only through `QPlatformTheme`, private API the app doesn't link. The desktop's `QT_QPA_PLATFORMTHEME` and `QT_STYLE_OVERRIDE` are dropped in this mode, so the dump is the same bytes on every machine and gtk3 can't fail it for want of a display. Tests hold the keyed entries to a real sheet's rows and order, and every menu action (bar File > Open Recent's and Help > Samples' entries, which are files, not actions) to the dump.
- Dock positions, sizes and visibility, and the window's geometry, are saved with `QMainWindow::saveState` and `saveGeometry` in the settings when the window closes, and restored when it opens. Every `QSettings` in `app/` comes from `appSettings()` (`app/settings.h`): the named native settings, or an INI file `setSettingsFile()` names. The app tests name one in their scratch directory and stop before building a window if the app would use any other file, since `QSettings::setPath()` is ignored for the native format on macOS and Windows. Qt's own settings (the QtProject store a `QFileDialog` writes) are moved into the scratch directory with `setPath()` where it works; on macOS and Windows they can't be, so the tests that build a `QFileDialog` are skipped there. ctest runs the app test binaries under a HOME and XDG profile in the build tree and checks that a whole run leaves an empty one empty; the xcb binary, which needs an X server, runs under `tests/xcb_test.sh`, which gives each run an empty profile of its own (`XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` included) and fails the run that leaves anything in it. The wrapper is written into the test's command, not left to `CROSSCOMPILING_EMULATOR`, which CMake drops under CMP0158 and from a `$<TARGET_FILE>` command. `app_tests_all_guarded` fails when a test binary built on the app has neither; for the xcb one it reads the commands ctest will run (`--show-only=json-v1`), so a test whose command line runs it without the wrapper fails it. It reads only those command lines, not the files they run: a helper script that runs the binary is out of its reach, so a test whose script runs it goes through the wrapper itself, and review, not the guard, holds it to that. It doesn't parse shell, so it fails closed: a test whose command names the binary's file (`blockwright-app-xcb-tests`) as a whole name anywhere in an argument (at the start or after a character outside POSIX's portable file-name set, such as `/`, `=`, a quote or a space, and at the end or before one) has to start with the wrapper. That refuses mentions that run nothing (`backend=<name>`, `backend='<name>'`, `/build/<name>/data`) on purpose; a longer name that only contains it (`/build/<name>-data/`, `lib<name>.so`) doesn't count. A test that needs to name the binary runs through `${BW_APP_XCB_TEST_ENV}`; any other test doesn't name it. A secondary token-reading guard (`tests/settings_guard.cpp`, held to the fixtures in `tests/data/settings-guard/`) checks `app/`, `core/`, `render/` and `cli/` for possible `QSettings` construction outside `app/settings.cpp`: direct construction, value template arguments in any position (including cv-qualified and elaborated `class QSettings` arguments, and after earlier arguments that contain `->`, a comparison `>`, or `<` inside nested `()`, `[]` or `{}` groups (comparisons, shifts and `operator<` / `operator<<`)) and defaults, aliases (including cv-qualified `::QSettings` and `class QSettings`), locally defined macro type arguments in any position, Qt global-static macros and base classes in any position, with or without access or virtual specifiers. Only `*` and `&` exempt type arguments and aliases, including a later template argument of a base (`struct Cache : QHash<QString, QSettings*>`): spelling out holders such as `std::unique_ptr<QSettings>` or `QPointer<QSettings>` is deliberately reported, and factory callers use `auto`. The template-return exemption is limited to declarations with an unqualified function name and a parameter list immediately followed by `;`, including `std::unique_ptr<QSettings> appSettings();`; suffixes such as `noexcept`, `const`, `override` or `= delete`, and helper definitions, are reported. Direct function return declarations, comments, literals, includes and preprocessor `__has_include` header operands are exempt. Unterminated ordinary literals stop at an unescaped newline; raw strings and digit separators remain intact. This guard does not resolve types or expand macros; constructions hidden through `decltype` and `remove_pointer_t` remain a review responsibility. A layout version drops a saved layout when the docks change (5 since the default dock heights, #225 and #226; 4 since the resizable columns gained explicit default widths; older saved dock arrangements are dropped once so a narrow column saved under the palette's former fixed-width constraint does not persist; the window geometry is kept). Window > Reset Layout (no shortcut) docks every dock where the table puts it and shows it: on the right, top to bottom, the preview, the palette, Layers, the Library, and Lint tabbed with the IRC View, Lint in front, so the panel an edit reads from stays beside the panel that checks it.
- The Lint and IRC View docks follow the document a quarter second after each change, and only while shown: a closed dock, or one in a tab behind another, catches up when it is next shown. The lint takes about 20 ms on a 120-row piece, but its time grows with the canvas (about 2.5 s at 400×5000), so both docks run their work off the GUI thread (`QtConcurrent::run` on a copy of the canvas and the options; the IRC View renders its image there too) and show the result back on it. The dock shows its last result until the next is in; a refresh while one runs drops that one's result and starts again on the document as it is then, and a change while one runs (an edit, open, New, replace, a format or budget change) drops its result too, so only a result of the document as it is is shown. On a canvas whose work takes longer than the gap between edits (400×2000 lints in about 1.3 s; an edit every half second) no result is ever current, so a dock behind the document for more than half a second (`kStaleShowMs`) says so under its result, "Linting…" or "Updating…", and the note goes as soon as it has caught up; an edit caught up sooner shows none, so typing on a small piece doesn't flash it. A result that finishes behind the document is still dropped, as above, not shown in its place: the note marks the one up as behind, where a fresh-looking result one edit old would not (#122). A new IRC budget is redrawn at once, not after the quarter second. The IRC View dock follows View > IRC > Fit, Pad and Swap Shades and the document's iCE setting the way Export does (§8): it shows the lines Export would write with those options. Turning Fit, Pad or Swap Shades on or off redraws it at once, and an iCE change redraws it as any format change does. The Lint list holds the first 1000 findings in reading order (the summary counts them all and says so), so filling it never holds up the window.
- The Window menu lists every dock as a checkable item. The toolbox, Tool Options, glyph bar, Layers dock, Lint and IRC View can be closed; the preview, palette and Library can't, so they are always shown and listed checked and disabled.
- Every toolbox button and option has a tooltip, with its shortcut where it has one. A tool's tooltip adds the first sentence of its help and its status tip is that sentence.
- Help > What's This? (Shift+F1, the platforms' standard key for it) puts the window in Qt's What's This mode; a click then says what the part clicked does, and Esc or a click on a part with nothing to say leaves it (`app/whats_this.h`). A tool's action, and so its toolbox button (a group's follows the tool it shows) and Tools menu item, shows the tool's whole help from the store as rich text. A setting in Tool Options shows its tooltip, found when it is asked for (a spin box's edit field by its spin box, a form row's label by its field), so a tooltip that changes with the tool's state is the one shown; a test checks that every setting each tool shows has one, form-row labels and composite fields such as the Text tool's face picker included. Shift+F1 is taken from keyboard mode, where it typed F1's glyph as F1 does; Shift+F2 to F10 still type theirs, and F1 is still a glyph key. During a drag the canvas keeps Shift+F1, as it keeps the undo key: the mode would take the drag's release and leave the drag stuck, so mid-drag it is F1 with Shift held, as Shift+F2 to F10 are (the glyph brush re-picks its glyph and the stroke goes on). In the mode, a pen that isn't drawing leaves its tablet events unaccepted, so the mouse click made of them is the mode's: a stylus tap paints nothing and says what the canvas does or leaves the mode, as a mouse click does.
- Help text (#153, `app/help_text.h`) is one store: a Markdown file per tool id in `app/help/tools/` and per layer kind in `app/help/layers/`, built into the app as resources. Its first paragraph is a plain summary, then how the tool behaves and what each of its options does, written from what the code does. The toolbox tooltips, the Layers dock's kind tooltips and add-button tooltips, the Tool Options help panel and Help > Manual all read it, and the docs site can take the same files. A test fails when a registry id has no file or a file has no tool.
- Tool Options shows the active tool's picture beside its name and the help under them, above the settings, in a panel the "About this tool" button folds away; it starts open and remembers its state (`toolOptions/showHelp`). The panel links to Help > Keyboard Shortcuts.
- Help > Manual... opens an offline manual built from the same store: each tool with its shortcut and help, then each kind of layer, with a button to the keyboard shortcuts. It is no substitute for the docs site's manual (§7, #57); Help > Manual opens that instead once it is published. Each tool's picture is under its heading.
- Tool pictures (#153, `app/tool_illustration.h`) are made at runtime by the real tools, not drawn or kept as image files. Each tool has a short script: a starting document of 16 × 6 cells (a shape for Fill, art for Select, Move and Clone, a lit disc for Outline and Rim Light, specks for Despeckle, stripes for Smudge), the pen, brush, ramp and options it needs, and a stroke or two, given as mouse presses, moves and releases in half-block pixels and typed keys. It runs on a new copy of the tool, a private document and a private canvas view, through the same events the canvas gets, and the app's canvas renderer draws the result. A stroke usually covers part of the start, so the untouched part is the "before". The settings point at a scratch file for the run (`ScratchSettings`), so the tool starts from its defaults and what it or the view writes is dropped; the open document, its undo stack, the window's tools and the settings are untouched. The palette and the VGA font are fixed, so a picture is made the first time it is asked for and kept for the process. A tool that changes no cells (the eyedropper) has no picture and is listed with the reason; a test checks every registry id has one or the other.
- The default window, including after Window > Reset Layout, fits 1280×720 and 1366×768 screens. Its minimum height is at most 660 logical pixels, reserving 60 pixels of a 720-high screen for the title bar, borders and desktop panel; the initial window height leaves that allowance in the available work area. Layers, Library, Lint and IRC View scroll their whole contents when short, as Tool Options already does, so forms, buttons and status text stay reachable. They scroll up and down only: each is at least as wide as its content and its vertical scrollbar, so the right column's native minimum shows the whole panel, and a wider generator form widens that minimum. The dock column widths and saved layout still follow the rules above.
- The start screen (#206, `app/start_screen.h`) shows on a launch with no file or project on the command line, before the main window: it comes up as a window of its own, centred on the screen the window's saved geometry is on (the one its logo is sized for), and the main window shows only when it closes, whichever way. Until then File > Open's and Open Project Directory's dialogs and the "Could not open" box are the screen's, so they come up over it. One named opens in the window instead and the screen stays away; with "Show at startup" off the window shows at once. From File > Start Screen... it is a modal dialog over the window. Either way it is laid out the same: the large logo on black at the top, then the actions in a left column and the sections to the right of it, side by side, so one more section makes the screen wider, not taller. The logo is the Qt resource `:/branding/logo-large.ans` (at most 80×20 cells) drawn by the app's own renderer, at 2× when the doubled logo and the rest of the screen fit the screen's work area less 60 pixels and 1× otherwise, with the `--version` text under it; when the resource is missing or doesn't parse, the plain text wordmark "Blockwright" stands in. New (N, or Enter anywhere but on another button, where Enter runs that button as Space does) makes a canvas of the width and height fields (80×40 by default, presets 80×25, 80×40 and 80×50) with iCE on or off, through `MainWindow::newCanvas`, which refuses a size outside the format's limits or too large to display and leaves the document as it was. Open... (O, Ctrl+O) and Open Project Directory... are File > Open's and File > Open Project Directory's own code. The screen closes on any `Document::reset` while it is up, so whatever opens a document closes it: its own actions, and a file the system hands the app after launch (a `QFileOpenEvent` on macOS, from the Finder or the Dock); it stays up when the dialog is cancelled or the open fails. Recent lists the last 10 files and projects opened or saved, newest first, each once, kept as `recent/files` in `appSettings()`; `MainWindow::openFile` records every open and `Document::fileWritten`, emitted by `save` and `saveProject`, every save. An entry whose file is gone is shown disabled with a Remove button. File > Open Recent lists the same entries, a missing one disabled. Esc or closing the screen gives a new 80×40 canvas, as a launch without the screen did; a document opened while it was up has closed it already, so Esc never clears one. File > Open Scene... records nothing in the recent list: it opens a new untitled project, and a scene is opened only behind its trust question. "Show at startup" (`startScreen/showAtStartup`, on by default) is under the actions; with it off a launch goes straight to the 80×40 canvas, and File > Start Screen... still brings the screen back, asking to save a modified document first, as New does. The actions are a list of buttons and the sections a row of titled panels, so Tutorials (#210) comes as one more entry. Samples (#207, §12) is one: beside Recent, when any samples are installed, a button for each by title (an `&` in it shown as itself, not a mnemonic), its summary as the tooltip, opening it as Help > Samples does; a title wider than 260 pixels is cut with an ellipsis and the tooltip then starts with the whole title; one that can't be read is disabled with the reason. With an 80×20 logo at 1× and a full recent list the screen is under 660 pixels high and 1280 wide however many samples there are, so it fits a 720-high screen (#183): the recent list and the samples each scroll in a fixed height. The samples scroll up and down only, and their column is as wide as its entries and its vertical scrollbar, even squeezed to the screen's minimum, so the scrollbar covers no entry and a cut title's ellipsis stays in view.

## 12. Library

A Library dock of drop-in parts for building scenes: people, trees, sky, sun and moon, water, fire, smoke, buildings, frames. Pieces keep needing the same parts: a sky, a person or figure, tree lines, and sun, moon, stars, smoke, fire, waves and towers, each drawn again from scratch.

**Three kinds of entry:**

| Kind | What it is | Phase |
|---|---|---|
| Stamp | A fixed piece of art: an `.ans` with a SAUCE title whose blank cells (a space or NUL on black without blink, exactly the cells transparent paste skips, §4 Selections) are its transparent area, so black inside the part is drawn as black █; plus a `.json` with category, tags and the IRC bytes its widest row costs. | 2c |
| Text style | A saved text-style stack (§15) with a name, category and a thumbnail of a sample word set in it. Double-clicking one applies it to the selected text layer; dragging one onto the canvas makes a new text layer in it. | 5 |
| Smart prefab | A generator (§7) with a parameter form: sky gradient (ramp, time of day, horizon row), tree line (far/mid/near, density, seed), sun or moon (radius, phase, glow ramp), stars (density, seed), mountains, water, smoke, fire, standing figures. Dropping one adds a generator layer. | 5 |

- **Dock:** a thumbnail grid (rendered at 1× and cached) with a category list and a search box. Dragging an entry onto the canvas, or double-clicking it, places a stamp as a floating selection in transparent paste mode at the cursor (the selection paste path (§4 Selections), so move, flip, rotate and Enter to commit work unchanged), and a smart prefab as a new generator layer. A stamp goes on an unlocked, shown cells layer, a rasterised text layer, or a pixels layer when every cell it writes is a half block, a full block or a blank (█ ▀ ▄, a space, NUL or NBSP: what fitLayerEdit lets onto a pixel layer). The one exception is ▌ ▐: a quarter turn of the float makes them ▀ ▄ (`kTurn4`, `core/src/selection.cpp`), so a stamp whose only glyphs the layer can't hold are ▌ ▐ floats, and its landing is refused until it is turned (§4 Selections, #433; `pixelsHoldTurned`). The maintainer ruled this way because a turn is the float's own tool for it, and a refusal at placement left no way to use a vertical half-block stamp on a pixel layer; the check moved to the landing, which is the only point where the cells written are known. A stamp holding any other glyph the layer can't hold, with or without ▌ ▐, is still refused before it floats. Placing a stamp first switches to the Select tool, so the tool before lands what it holds (the Text tool's emptied new layer goes) and the layer then active is the one checked. If it can't take the stamp, the stamp is refused, before anything floats (a block already floating stays), and the status bar says why, as the text style's refusal does: "Could not place <path>: the layer is locked", "…: the layer is hidden", "…: a live Text layer is drawn from its text", "…: a Generator layer is drawn by its generator", "…: a Reference layer holds a picture, not cells", "…: there is no active layer", or "…: a Pixels layer holds no ░▒" naming each glyph of the whole stamp it can't hold (#433). A layer changed while the stamp floats drops it (§4 Selections), and every landing checks what it would write onto the layer then active.
- **Sources:** a bundled read-only library installed to `<prefix>/share/blockwright/library/`, and a user library in the app data directory. Selection > Save to Library… saves the selection as a stamp in the user library, asking for name, category and tags. With nothing floating it saves what the visible layers show under the selection (`Document::canvas()`, the composite): the layers below show through the active layer's clear cells, a hidden layer is left out, and the stamp is opaque wherever a visible layer paints, so hide a layer to keep it out of the stamp. Copy instead takes only the active layer's cells (§4 Selections). The two differ on purpose (#434): a stamp is what you see, while Copy edits the layer you are working on. With a selection floating both take the float, whatever lies under it: the cells lifted off the active layer, or the block pasted or placed, which holds the cells it was copied or saved with, from whichever layer (a float off a pixel layer is saved as §5 Editing says). Selection > Save as Frame Style… reads the same cells as Save to Library. A stamp is CP437; one whose cells CP437 can't hold (a wide glyph, a cluster, a glyph with no CP437 byte) is saved as UTF-8 with a byte order mark and read as UTF-8 only by that mark and its sidecar (#336; §4, Library implementation notes). The bundled library is CP437 only.
- **Scene Starter** (File > New from Scene…, Phase 5): pick dawn, day, dusk or night, a horizon row and which bands to include (sky, sun or moon, stars, mountains, tree lines, ground, water), and it creates the canvas with one generator layer per band, in order, each editable afterwards.
- **What ships:** only funkpower's own generic parts: trees, plants, land, buildings, water, sky, smoke, fire, things, symbols and frames. Nothing piece-specific (a part cut from a piece is kept only when it reads as a generic thing), nothing that shows a real person, logo or character, and nothing that copies someone else's art. Most come from `library-maker/make_stamps.cpp` (`bw-make-stamps <library dir>`, outside the default build), which writes each one in half-block pixels, trims it to its part and writes it as Save to Library does, signed funkpower with a fixed SAUCE date, so a stamp is remade rather than edited. The rest are the frames and parts cut from funkpower's pieces (the same rule as golden files).
- **The bar:** `tests/test_library_bundled.cpp` checks that every stamp scans clean, is signed and filed under its category, is trimmed with a blank cell on its outer ring (or is a frame with an open middle) and fits the IRC budget, and that it lints clean (`blockwright-cli lint`'s rules) but for trailing black, which is its transparent area, and that it is CP437: no byte order mark, no `encoding` key, and saved again from its cells it is the same two files but for SAUCE's author and date (#336). The stamps cut from pieces before the maker are pinned at exactly the finding counts they have: one that improves fails the test until its pin is lowered.
- **Smart prefabs come from one shared kit:** the sky, tree, figure, sun/moon, star, smoke, fire and water functions, which Phase 5 wraps as generators.

### Samples (#207)

Layered example projects that come with the app, to open and take apart: what each layer kind, blend mode and lock does, shown in a finished piece rather than described. `core/include/blockwright/samples.h`, `app/main_window_samples.cpp`, `samples/`.

- **What a sample is:** a directory project (§6), `samples/NN-<name>/` in the source, installed as it is to `<datadir>/blockwright/samples/NN-<name>/`; the `NN-` prefix orders them. Its `notes.md` is its documentation, in a fixed shape the tests read: a `# <title>` heading (the name the app lists), a one-paragraph summary (the tooltip), a `## Layers` table (layer, kind, mode, locks, what it does; top first, and it must match the stack), a `## Variants` table of other looks the same layers make (`show`/`hide <layer>`, `raise <layer> above <layer>`, `lower <layer> below <layer>`, separated by `;`), and a "Try this" list of things to do with it. A layer name may hold spaces and the word a change splits at: `raise fog above above day sky` splits at the ` above ` whose two sides both name layers of the stack, and a change that could split two ways is refused. A table whose header has fewer columns than the format's (five for Layers, two for Variants) makes the notes unreadable: the sample is listed disabled with the reason, never read past its rows. SAUCE author funkpower, SAUCE title the notes' title.
- **Remade, not edited:** `samples/maker/make_samples.cpp` (`bw-make-samples`, outside the default build unless the tests are built on a POSIX host) writes each one with the core's own ramps, brushes and text styles, runs its generators and takes its snapshots, so a sample is remade rather than edited and is funkpower's own work by construction. Its output is checked in; the build only installs it. Every byte it writes is the same on any machine: the cells go in raw zstd blocks, the showcase's reference sketch is `samples/maker/sketch.png` copied as it is (a PNG encoded by the maker came out in other bytes from another zlib), and nothing else it writes passes through a compressor or an encoder whose output depends on a library version. A sample is never packed to `.bwp` at build time: that would need the CLI on the build host, which a cross build doesn't have.
- **The bar:** `tests/test_samples.cpp` reads every bundled sample and checks the notes against the stack, that each finished piece among them is its `.ans` in `tests/data/pieces/` cell for cell, that every variant lints clean (`blockwright-cli lint`'s rules) and every row of it fits the IRC budget, and that the samples between them use every layer kind, both blend modes, both locks, a hidden layer, a layer off the canvas, a layer smaller than the canvas placed by its offset, a baked copy of a text or generator layer, a variant that only reorders layers and changes the composite, an upper bright pixel keeping its colour over a different bright one under iCE off (§5), and a variant. `samples_install` checks that they install byte for byte, with nothing else, and lint clean with the installed CLI. `samples_remade` runs the maker into a scratch directory and checks that the samples checked in are what it draws now, the same files under the same names and each byte for byte, so a maker or notes change that wasn't regenerated, or a sample file edited by hand, fails.
- **In the app:** Help > Samples (filled each time it opens) and the start screen's Samples section list them by title. One opens as an untitled copy (`MainWindow::openSampleFile`): unmodified, so closing it untouched asks nothing; untitled, so Save asks where and the installed sample is never written; off the recent list, which is for the user's own files; and with its generators untrusted, as any project's are (§7). The folder is `$BLOCKWRIGHT_SAMPLES` when set, else found from the real binary as the bundled Library is.
- **What ships:** `01-layers-showcase`, "Harbour at night": thirteen layers covering every kind (cells, pixels, text with its baked copy, generator, reference), both modes, both locks, a hidden day sky, an off-canvas quay, a moon placed by its offset, a half-cell grey cloud over it, three snapshots and five variants. `02-ddos-king`, "DDOS KING": a finished piece, a crowned king in shades on a throne of server racks with packet lightning round him, 80x64 with iCE on. Its twelve cells layers (backdrop, throne, cables, king, sceptre, head, lightning, finials, packets, title, border, signature) are kept as they are in `samples/maker/ddos-king/`; the maker reads them, so the sample is made again the same way on any machine, and changing them is a deliberate change made in a PR that refreshes the files. The maker adds the sample's own parts: the backdrop locked, two hidden variant layers (margins, black blocks that keep the frame's rows and edge column when the border is hidden, and cable tips, a half block more yellow on top of four cable runs, so that with the lightning hidden the yellow doesn't meet the blue sky straight on, which the lint reads as a gradient that skips rungs; the lightning never touches those cells, and the tips take their colour from the composite of the layers under the lightning), three snapshots (the throne, the figure, finished) and three variants (no lightning, no border, the title only). The composite is the piece's `.ans` cell for cell. `03-still-life`, "Still life": the brushes tutorials' showcase, seven cells layers. A cylinder, a box and a ball, each on its own layer smaller than the canvas, shaded inside an alpha lock with the light from the north-west (the Form brush's cylinder and dome on the violet and sea ramps, the box's three faces set rung by rung on the wood ramp without its black), over a locked dark grey wall and a locked grey table. The shadows they cast sit on a layer of their own between the table and the objects, the table's rungs taken down a rung at a time, and a rim light from the east on a layer over them: a band along each object's right edge, a rung dimmer each cell in, kept only where it is lighter than the shading. The band is drawn by the maker, not by the Rim Light brush: the brush lights one cell deep a stroke, and with Keep the lint clean on it left these dark sides almost unlit when the sample was made. It has iCE on, three snapshots (flat, shaded, lit) and three variants. `04-generator-ridges`, "Ridges at dusk": the generator piece, nine layers. Two generators ship inside it under `scripts/<id>/`, each a short `sh` and `awk` script with a parameter form and no randomness of awk's own (a 32-bit LCG from a seed), so they draw the same cells everywhere: stars (chars-only, over the black rows of a locked sunset sky) and ridges, far to near, each a rung darker on the sunset ramp than the one behind it and two or three rows under it, so the composite stays a ladder. A second ridges layer with other parameters (seed 12, four ridges, sharp peaks) and a baked copy of the first sit hidden beside it, and a locked lake and shore and pixel pines and a cabin sit on layers of their own over them. It has iCE on, three snapshots (two smooth ridges, the parameters kept, finished) and four variants (another seed, baked, no stars, generated only). `05-character`, "Traveller": a character on layers of its own, nine layers. A traveller with a staff and a pack, facing left into a light from the upper left, drawn in half-block pixels by the maker from a letter-a-pixel sprite, split into an outline (the silhouette grown a pixel, black, under the rest), the body in flat colours with its lit side (alpha-locked), the face, the highlights and the shadow it casts on the grass. All five are one size and in one place, smaller than the canvas, over a locked sky, hills and meadow. There are no layer groups, so a hidden copy of the five merged onto one layer ("figure (merged)") moves the whole figure with one Alt+drag; moving each part by the same distance gives the same picture. The sprite leaves no gap a pixel wide between its parts (the staff, the arm, the legs), where the scene would show as a speck, and the tip of its shadow is a single cell of half-block green, where two would look like a tuft in the grass and read as a broken line a cell short of the next one, so the whole piece lints clean wherever the figure is moved on the meadow (`tests/test_samples.cpp` moves it to every place it fits). It has iCE on, three snapshots (the scene, the figure in flat colours, finished) and five variants (no outline, no highlights, the silhouette, one layer, the scene only). `06-blockwright-logo`, "Blockwright logo": the program's logo (#205) taken apart into six layers, and shown together they are `branding/logo-large.bwp` colour for colour (`tests/test_samples.cpp` compares them). The maker draws the wordmark from a letter-a-pixel sprite: the letters as a pixel layer in two flat colours, light cyan and cyan at the foot (alpha-locked); the drop under them, the same sprite moved one column right and one pixel down in blue, kept out of the counters, with three pixels trimmed; and the ▓ ▒ ░ shade cells on a cells layer of their own over the letters, since a shade glyph needs a whole cell. The mark's gold block and three white blocks are cells layers smaller than the canvas, placed by their offset, over a locked black backdrop. It has iCE off, as the logo does, three snapshots (flat, the dark foot and the drop, finished) and four variants (no shading, no drop, the wordmark only, the mark only). `07-battlestation`, "Battlestation": a finished piece, a night desk seen from behind the chair, lit only by a wide monitor running a tiling layout, with a home-lab rack beside it, 80x40 with iCE on. Its nine cells layers are kept as they are in `samples/maker/battlestation/`, and the maker takes the screen's light off three of them (the wall, the desk and the rack's side and top edge toward the screen go from the blues to black, or the rack's own dark grey, glyph for glyph; a space left on black takes the grey a `.ans` gives it) and puts that light on a colours-only layer, glow, over them: its cells are the colours of the lit layers wherever the lit composite differs from the dark one, so hiding it puts the room in the dark without moving a glyph, and showing it gives back the piece's `.ans` cell for cell. The wall is locked, the screen alpha-locked, and a hidden margins layer (one black block in the bottom left corner) keeps the rows under the stand when the Screen only variant hides the desk, since IRC trims blank lines at the end of a piece. The screen has bars of colour where words would be, so nothing on it can be read. It has three snapshots (the dark room with the monitor on, the desk's hardware, finished) and three variants (no glow, lights off, the screen only). `08-brain-on-irc`, "This Is Your Brain on IRC": a finished piece, one egg frying in a pan seen from above under the caption, the yolk a speech bubble with a # pressed into it, 80x32 with iCE on. Its source layers are in `samples/maker/brain-on-irc/`: the lettering (caption, tagline, signature), the egg white, the smoke and the sizzle are taken as they are, and the lettering sits under the pan, which changes nothing, since no layer above it shares a cell with it. The pan and the yolk there are outlines; the maker fills each flat (dark grey and yellow, alpha-locked) and runs the Form brush over a copy of it with `paintRegion`, which dabs every cell row by row, left to right, and so paints as one drag in the app does that runs along each row and back along it before stepping down a row, the drag the notes' steps give: a cylinder on the steel ramp at 100% for the pan, a dome on the skin ramp at 65% for the yolk, light from the north-west, Glint on, Form rim light off. The flat copies stay hidden under the shaded ones, so the steps can be repeated and the Raw egg variant can show the egg flat. A wide shape is a log to the cylinder, so each band of the ring is white along most of its upper edge and black along most of its lower one. The brush takes back a change the lint would report, so eleven cells along the upper and lower edges keep the flat dark grey, and a stroke that reaches the cells in another order, or several strokes, comes out different in a dozen cells or more. The brush paints every cell it covers, so the # and the highlight are pixel layers over the yolk, and the sizzle is a pixel layer of its yellow and white halves, so the rest of its cells show the pan, with pixels added at three of the places where its arcs step diagonally, which on the solid pan the noise measure counts as lone pixels. Two cells the lint calls specks, one on the pan beside the sizzle and one at the yolk's tail, are set to the shade of a neighbour; the test holds the rest of each layer to a fresh pass of the brush. The background is locked, and a hidden margins layer (one black block in the top left corner) keeps the rows above the smoke when the No caption variant hides the lettering, since IRC trims blank lines at the start of a piece. It has three snapshots (the egg and the pan flat, after the Form brush, finished) and two variants (raw egg, no caption). `09-wtc-0903`, "wtc-0903": a finished piece, Lower Manhattan at 9:03 on 11 September 2001 seen from across the East River, the second plane going into the South Tower and the North Tower burning behind it, 100x48 with iCE on, the first sample wider than 80 columns; every row fits one IRC line at the efnet-weechat budget, in every variant. Its source layers are in `samples/maker/wtc-0903/`, twelve cells layers taken as they are; the maker sets their locks and modes: the sky locked, the two towers alpha-locked, the reflections colours only. The towers are drawn in shade and the morning sun on their east faces is a layer of its own, sunlight, over the plume and the fire, so it holds only the cells they leave showing; the North Tower layer carries the cells under the plume and the fire in the sun already, so the face stays lit with them hidden. Hiding sunlight changes 286 cells, all on the east faces, and raising the plume above it puts the smoke across the South Tower's top. With all of them shown it is the piece's `.ans` cell for cell. It has three snapshots (the sky and the towers in shade with the plume and the fire, the sunlight, the fireball and the plane, finished) and four variants (before 8:46, sunlight off, the fire only, smoke in front). Next: a sample per tutorial (#210).

### Frame styles (#345)

A border drawn to any size from a small piece, so a frame is chosen and dragged rather than drawn by hand. `core/include/blockwright/frame.h`, `app/frame_tool.h`.

- **A frame style is a nine-slice:** a small source canvas cut by four insets (left, right, top, bottom) into four corners, four edges and a middle. Edges may be several cells thick. Each edge is a pattern that repeats along its length, anchored at the corner it starts from, so dashes and stripes tile rather than stretch, and the far end may cut a pattern part-way. The middle is drawn only when the style has a fill; otherwise those cells are transparent and leave what is under them. A style has a minimum size (the corners, plus any declared minimum middle); `drawFrame` returns nothing below it.
- **Keep background:** a cell of a style may keep the background under it: the Frame tool writes its glyph and foreground and leaves the colour already there, in either colour choice, so a frame dropped on a coloured scene doesn't cut a black band round itself. Over a clear cell of the layer it takes the colour the visible layers below the active one show there (`Document::shownBelow`, as the fill does), never one from a layer above, which would stay baked in once that layer is hidden or moved; on a Pixels layer it leaves the pixel its glyph doesn't cover, set or clear. The line styles (their gaps too), heavy block, thin half-block and the shaded ring keep it, since their background is no part of the look; bevel, hazard stripes and the button paint their own, since theirs is.
- **JSON form:** `id`, `name`, `insets` [l, r, t, b], `fill`, `min` [w, h], and `rows`, `fg` and `bg` as strings, one character per cell (colours as hex palette digits, `.` in `bg` for a transparent cell), and an optional `keepBg` in the same shape (`1` for a cell that keeps the background, `.` for one that doesn't; left out, none does, as in styles written before it). Unknown keys, ragged rows, a source with no cells, wide glyphs, bad insets, a source larger than a frame style's (`kMaxFrameSourceColumns` × `kMaxFrameSourceRows`, 100 × 100: a frame is a border, and a source that size already holds corners a screen wide), a `min` larger than a canvas (`kMaxColumns` × `kMaxRows`) and JSON past `kMaxFrameStyleBytes` (1 MiB; the largest source written out is under a tenth of that) are refused. Each inset is bounded by the source limit before two are added, so `valid()` never overflows whatever a file holds, and the list sizes and string lengths are checked before any row is copied. A library `.frame.json` larger than the byte limit is refused from its size before it is read (from `fstat` on the opened file on POSIX, from the path on Windows), or as it grows while being read (`library::readRegularFile` with a limit), and the scan lists any frame style it can't read, running out of memory included, as a problem; nothing a file holds escapes the scan as an exception. `drawFrame` refuses a size past the canvas limit before it allocates anything. Bundled styles, Library stamps and user styles share the one model.
- **Frame tool** (Tools > Frame, Ctrl+Shift+I, in the shapes group): a drag draws the chosen style to fit, as one undo step, grown to the minimum away from the press when it is smaller. It follows the selection, the alpha lock and mirror mode, the glyphs mirrored per image. On a Pixels layer it refuses a style holding any glyph the layer can't (corners, edges or fill), and says which. Tool Options picks the style from thumbnails (the style drawn at its minimum but at most 16 × 8 past its corners, fitted to the icon as a stamp's thumbnail is, so a declared `min` never sizes the picture) and the colours: the style's own, or recoloured to the pen. The right button erases the cells the frame would cover.
- **What ships:** eleven styles drawn in code: single, double, double across, double down, heavy block, thin half-block, shaded (three cells deep on the sides and two on the top and bottom, which a cell's 2:1 shape makes look even), bevel, hazard stripes (solid yellow corners, which meet the stripes in step wherever an edge is cut), dashed and a double-bevel button. No rounded style: CP437 has no arc corners. Each lints clean and fits the IRC budget at every size from its minimum to 40×14 and at 80×24 (`tests/test_frame.cpp`), which covers every phase of every repeat.
- **User frame styles** (Selection > Save as Frame Style...): a dialog takes the name, the four insets, fill and keep background, and previews the style drawn at twice the selection's size (at most 40 × 16 past its corners, fitted to 320 × 256 pixels); `frameStyleFromCells` cuts the selection. Blank cells become transparent, as transparent paste treats them; keep background marks every cell the style draws. It refuses what the JSON form can't hold rather than changing it: RGB colours, blink, a glyph that isn't one code point one cell wide, and a selection larger than a source may be. The open middle isn't checked when the style has no fill, since it is never written. The style is written to the user library as `<slug>.frame.json` (`library::writeFrameStyle`); saving never replaces a file, so the same name takes `-2`, `-3`. The scan collects `.frame.json` files from both libraries, bundled first, and reports a broken one as a problem; no stamp may be named `<x>.frame.ans`, so a stamp's sidecar is never a frame style. The Frame tool and Tool Options list them after the bundled styles, by the id `library:<path>`, the path's native bytes percent-encoded but for `/` (`frameStyleId`, from `stampKey`), so two file names that aren't UTF-8 never share an id; saving picks the new style. A library style chosen last time is picked again once the library has loaded; if it has gone, the tool falls back to the first bundled style until it returns, unless a style is picked meanwhile: picking one, the fallback shown included (the picker's `activated`), makes it the choice kept. Drawing goes through the same stroke as the bundled styles, so undo, the selection, the alpha lock and mirror mode hold.
- **Resizable stamps** (#345 slice 2; `core/include/blockwright/stamp_slice.h`, `app/select_tool.h`): a stamp's sidecar may carry `"insets"`, after `ircBytes`: `[l, r, t, b]` for a frame, which resizes in width and height, or `[l, r]` for a band, which resizes in width only and keeps its height. Each is a whole number of cells from 0 to `kMaxRows`, checked before it is made an int; the insets on an axis must leave at least one cell between them (each inset is compared with the size before the two are added, so nothing overflows), and a resizable stamp can't hold a wide character, which a repeat could cut in two. A sidecar that breaks any of these, and one over 1 MiB (`kMaxSidecarBytes`, read through the opened handle and refused before it is read whole), is a problem in the scan, never an escaped exception; so is a stamp whose read runs out of memory. A placed stamp with insets floats with handles on its outline, eight for a frame and the two on its ends for a band; a drag on one moves that side to the cell edge nearest the pointer, where the button is let go included, the opposite side staying, clamped to the minimum (the insets' sum, at least one cell) and to `kMaxColumns` × `kMaxRows`. Each re-cut cuts the stamp as saved as `drawFrame` cuts a style (`frameSourceIndex`) with the middle drawn (a frame style's `fill`), but with no frame style's source limit, so a stamp as large as a canvas resizes; shrinking and growing back loses nothing; blank cells stay transparent, since a stamp lands in transparent mode. The float's flips and rotations turn the cells as they stand, and the next re-cut cuts the stamp as saved and then turns it the same way, since a repeat of several cells cut part-way would otherwise start from the other corner (a band on its side resizes in height only, up to `kMaxRows`: the limits are on the size as it shows, though the cut before the turns may be past a canvas's on an axis; that slice lives only in the float, since a sidecar can't hold it). Neither side as it shows may pass a canvas's, the one that doesn't resize included: a band longer than `kMaxColumns` turns on its side as any float does, but its handles don't resize it, and neither do a frame's whose corners alone are wider than a canvas once it is on its side (an axis whose minimum is past its maximum is left as it is, never clamped). The list of turns is kept to the shortest that replays the same (`addTurn`, at most `kMaxTurns`, 4): what it does is one of the eight orientations of the cells and how far round its cycle a bracket glyph has stepped, since CP437 has no turned bracket and `( [ ) ]`, `< « > »` and `{ ≤ } ≥` step round a quarter turn at a time and two for a horizontal flip; sixteen lists, which a test checks replay every glyph as the sequences they stand for. Nothing is an edit until the float is stamped, which is one undo step; focus lost or the undo key during the drag puts the float back as the drag found it (its cells, place and slice, so a turn or arrow-key nudge made during the drag goes too), and a nudge or turn the drag carries on through moves the side that stays with the float. What the drag holds to put the float back goes when the drag ends, however it ends. A stylus release the view missed lands at the pixel where the pen was last seen, not its cell's centre. Save to Library… asks Resize: Fixed size, Width only (a band) or Width and height (a frame), with the four insets (1 each to start; left and right up to `kMaxColumns`, top and bottom up to `kMaxRows`), and `encodeStamp` refuses insets the cells can't be cut by, saying why; saving a placed resizable float offers its slice. The four bundled frames carry insets (line, double line and bevel `[1, 1, 1, 1]`; hazard `[4, 8, 2, 3]`, whose middle is six stripe periods across and three down), and a test re-cuts each at its minimum, odd sizes and 80×24: lint clean, the IRC budget kept, the corners whole, the middle blank. An older build refuses the `insets` key and lists such a stamp as a problem rather than placing it at a size it can't change.
- **Next:** a frame layer that stays resizable after it is placed, perhaps, and resizing a float from the keyboard.

## 13. Phases

Estimates are focused working days.

| Phase | Scope | Est. | Done when |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0. Core + CLI | Cell model, CP437, ANSI parse/write, SAUCE, PNG render, `cli render/convert`; golden tests: the 12 `funkpower/` pieces in `tests/data/golden/` round-trip, and renders of those and 7 more pieces match 22 libansilove references (`tests/data/render/ansilove/`, 3 of them at 9 px) | 3–4 d | Every `funkpower/` piece in `tests/data/golden/` round-trips byte-identical where possible (the rest are listed in `NONCANONICAL` with why) and renders the same as libansilove |
| 1. Viewer + keyboard editor | Qt window, atlas renderer, zoom, 1× dock, keyboard mode, F-key glyph sets, palette, undo, open/save `.ans` | 4–5 d | Can open, type into and save a piece |
| 2. Moebius parity | Half-block, shade, custom-glyph, colorize brushes; line/rect/ellipse; fill; eyedropper; selections (move, flip, rotate, transparent/under paste); mirror; guides 80×25/40/50; reference image; SAUCE dialog; iCE; 8/9 px | 8–10 d | A piece can be drawn from blank without leaving the editor |
| 2b. Advanced brushes | §10, all of it | 6–8 d | A shaded sphere can be painted by hand that passes the lint's speckle and ladder checks |
| 2c. Workspace + stamps | §11 docks (toolbox, Tool Options, glyph bar, saved layout); §12 Library dock with stamps, Save to Library, a first bundled set of stamps | 3–4 d | Every tool and its options can be picked without the menu bar; the dock layout survives a restart and Window > Reset Layout restores the default; a tree from the bundled Library can be dropped onto a canvas and moved before it is committed; a selection saved with Save to Library reappears in the dock after a restart, and placing it skips its blank cells and is one undo step. Offscreen GUI tests cover each |
| 3. Layers + project format | Layer kinds, compositor, layer panel, project zip/dir, snapshots | 5–6 d | Shade under a pixel layer composites with no specks |
| 4. IRC tooling | Fit-to-budget, IRC export, live row meter, IRC view, lint panel (4a, done: budgets, fit, pad, the meter, Export IRC Text; 4b, done: the IRC View and Lint docks, #15; 4c, built and then removed: Publish to Library and Send to WeeChat, #16, §9) | 4–5 d | A 120-row piece exports with every line ≤ 407 bytes and the meter agreed beforehand |
| 5. Generators + text | External generator layers with param forms; the Text tool, font faces and text styles with prefab presets and whole-cell big modes (§15); smart prefabs and Scene Starter (§12) | 13–16 d | An existing layered Python scene opens as editable layers; a night scene with moon, stars and tree lines can be started from File > New from Scene…; the text fixtures in §15 pass: every bundled preset on every fixture word in each bundled pixel face and the test TDF fonts, iCE on and off, lint-clean, at most 80 columns with nothing clipped and at most 407 bytes a line; each saves, reopens, undoes and bakes to identical visible cells |
| 6. Packages + macOS + Windows | Linux distro packages (AUR, Debian/Ubuntu `.deb` and a Launchpad PPA, Fedora `.rpm` and a Copr), AppImage/Flatpak, .dmg, MSI, CI matrix, XBIN/BIN | 6–8 d | On Arch, Debian 12, Ubuntu 24.04 and the current Fedora (x86_64), `pacman`/`yay`, `apt` and `dnf` install it from the published repositories and it launches; the AppImage and Flatpak launch on Ubuntu 24.04; signed-or-not .dmg and MSI builds install and launch on macOS and Windows; the CI matrix builds and runs `ctest` on all three; XBIN/BIN round-trip tests pass |
| 7. Documentation site | Separate repo `funkpower/blockwright-docs`, published at https://blockwright-docs.pumpin.online/: tutorials from a blank canvas to an IRC post covering every tool and brush, and a manual of every menu, dock, tool, option, CLI command and file format; samples rendered by `blockwright-cli` from `.ans` files; a `cli replay` for step images and an action dump for the shortcuts and menu pages (#57) | 12–16 d | `cli replay` and the action dump ship in this repo, and the docs build uses them for its step images and its shortcuts and menu pages; every tutorial and manual page listed in the docs repo's `PLAN.md` (§2, §3) for a feature on `main` is published, samples rendered from `.ans`, checked against the dev build |
| 8. Later | Embedded Lua, animation/ANSImation, Amiga fonts, MCP server, 24-bit editing (the IRC target's mIRC 16–98 are its first part, #350 slice 1) | — | — |

v1 = Phases 0–4 including 2b and 2c: about 7–8 working weeks.

Phase 2b's done-when is a test, and it is met (`test_shading_brushes.cpp`, "Phase 2b done-when: a sphere by hand, then a despeckle pass"): a ball 40 columns across is painted with the form brush in hand-like rows of dabs over a round mask, pressure rising and falling, then given a despeckle pass, and the ladder lint finds no skips, no rung wider than 3, no specks and no non-neighbour shades. Of part B the ramp gradient is in, its core function and the GUI tool, cell-banded or 4×4 Bayer (#160), and so are the smudge brush, the clone stamp and the glyph spray (stars, haze, smoke); of the detail brushes' texture stamps the lumps family (scales, foliage, hoard), brick and noise are in.

## 14. Decisions

- Name: Blockwright.
- IRC encoder: native C++ in `core/`, one encoder.
- Generator output: `.ans` on stdout.
- Hosting: git.pumpin.online (`funkpower/blockwright`); AGENTS.md's public-repo rules apply to everything in it.
- Default canvas: 80×40.
- Alt+drag moves the active layer (2026-10-26). The maintainer’s desktop binds window move and resize to Super, leaving Alt+drag free. On desktops whose window manager takes Alt+drag the canvas never sees it; a menu action or alternative chord can follow later.
- XBIN palettes and fonts (#21, 2026-10-30) are read and written back but not drawn: drawing them needs the renderer and every view to take a font and palette per document, which is its own change. Until then saving one to a format that can't hold them is a reported loss (§8).
- Reference images are layers (#178, 2026-10-26), saved with the project, not a session-only view overlay: a picture traced from belongs to the piece, and a layer gives it a place, visibility and undo like any other. The compositor skips them, so they are never exported.
- Reference layers move and resize in canvas pixels (#178, 2026-10-30): Alt+drag moves one as it moves any layer, and a Move/Transform tool (Ctrl+Shift+J) takes a plain drag to move the active layer and drags from corner handles to resize a reference one, aspect kept unless Shift is held, with numbers in Tool Options. The handles belong to that tool so that Alt+drag with any other tool stays a plain move.
- A reference layer draws in its place in the stack (#178, 2026-10-30), under the visible art layers above it and clipped by the halves of cells they cover, not over all the art: raising or lowering it in the Layers dock decides whether the art or the picture is on top, as for any layer. The art above covers it whole, not at an opacity (layers have none), and the compositor still skips it, so the stack order changes what the view shows and nothing that is exported.
- Every PR gets an adversarial Codex review before merge; when Codex is unavailable a Claude reviewer that didn't write the change stands in (AGENTS.md).
- Documentation: a separate repo, `funkpower/blockwright-docs` (tutorials and the manual), served at https://blockwright-docs.pumpin.online/; it is the last scheduled phase, and a page describes only what is on `main`.
- Packages are published and maintained as funkpower, with funkpower's own address (funkpower's maintainer address, the only real email address tracked files may carry, and only in the packaging fields AGENTS.md lists): AUR, Launchpad, Copr and every other account, the maintainer fields and the signing key. Nothing a package or source tarball ships carries a real name.
- Messages with a count (#304, 2026-11-03): the app installs no `QTranslator`, so it doesn't use `tr(s, nullptr, n)`, which without one only puts the number in and shows `line(s)` as written. Each such message gives its English singular and plural, each through `tr`, and `plural()` (`app/plural.h`) picks one by the count; the `plural_strings` tests fail on a count passed to `tr` or `QCoreApplication::translate` (whatever the source text, a literal, a table entry, a member or a call, and whatever the disambiguation: `nullptr`, `0`, `NULL` or a string; a call through `this->`, `qApp->` or `instance()->` is one, but a member of another object or a variable named `tr` declared with its type on the same line is not, #320; a declaration with its type on the line before or ending in `>`, and a call to a lambda or variable named `tr` with `0` or `nullptr` second, are flagged) or a `(s)` in a string in `app/`, `cli/` or `core/src/` (a line that is only a `//` comment is left alone), and `plural_strings_fixtures` proves the check on a fixture per form. The CLI and core write English directly and pick the form by the count the same way. Shipping translations would mean a `.ts`/`.qm` per language and Qt's numerus forms, which can replace `plural()` then.
- A scene imported byte for byte (#308, 2026-11-03): a project zip was already deterministic (every entry dated 1980-01-01, written in a fixed order), but import-scene put two things in it that change from run to run or machine to machine: the scene's absolute path in each `scripts/<id>/generator.json`, and today's local date in SAUCE. `import-scene --bake` writes each layer as cells (`bw::bake`) and drops its `scripts/<id>/`, so the project no longer names the scene or the Python; the layers can't be run again, which is the point of a built artifact. `--author NAME` (1 to 20 printable CP437 characters, not only spaces, which SAUCE's space padding would read back as no author) and `--date CCYYMMDD` (a real day from 00010101 on: year 0000 is refused, as the app's SAUCE dialog refuses it, QDate having no year 0) set SAUCE's fields; only these options check a date, and a record read from a file keeps whatever date it carries; when the scene has no title, and so no SAUCE record, either one makes a record (`Sauce::fresh` with the project's iCE). `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH` (the reproducible-builds variable: digits, seconds since 1970-01-01 UTC, at most the end of 9999; leading zeros count for nothing, so the value is checked, not the digit count) stands in for today, taken as the UTC day so the time zone doesn't matter; `--date` wins over it, an empty value counts as unset, and on its own it only re-dates a record the title made, never makes one. Only import-scene and replay read it (replay since a steps file's saved captures from a start with no SAUCE record stopped matching the day after they were saved, below): `Sauce::fresh` keeps the local date for the app's new pieces and the other commands. A bad `--author`, `--date` or `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH` is a usage error (exit 2) checked before the scene runs. These are CLI options only; the app's Open Scene… keeps the live generator layers it is for. iCE is the scene's to say, not the caller's: an optional `"ice": false` in the `--list-layers` JSON (absent is true, so existing scenes don't change) rather than an `--ice off` option, so Open Scene… in the app gets it too and a scene can't be imported with the wrong iCE. Left out: writing the scene's path relative to the output (it would still differ with the output's place and break a moved project); without `--bake` the absolute path stays as before. The `.bwp` format doesn't change.
- Replay's captures on any day (2026-11-08): the Sample merged steps file (#419) starts from `tests/data/replay/layered.bwp`, a project with no SAUCE record, so every capture got `Sauce::fresh`'s record dated the day it was replayed, and its saved captures (dated 2026-11-07) failed CI from the next day on; the tour and the canvas-size captures start from a piece with a record and never did. replay --help already promised the same bytes every run. Rather than give the start file a record (which would leave the next start without one to fail the same way), `blockwright-cli replay` takes `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH` for today as import-scene does, through `bwcli::replay::document`, and the replay tests compare saved captures through that function with the day they were saved on. A replay without it still dates a new record today, as the app dates a new piece. A golden set whose start has no record is saved with the day the tests pin: `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH=1794009600 blockwright-cli replay tests/data/replay/sample-merged.json -o tests/data/replay/sample-merged` remakes Sample merged's (2026-11-07), and a new set from such a start is saved with the same value; a plain replay saves today's date and the tests reject it.
- Every merge to main installs a dev build on the dev machine (`blockwright-dev`, `blockwright-cli-dev` and a "Blockwright (dev)" launcher entry).
- Local TheDraw collection (#177a): use `~/tdfiglet/fonts` when the fonts-folder setting has never been set and that directory exists; Clear stores an explicit empty value. Scan catalogues on a worker, publish on the GUI thread, and construct faces there lazily when used. No collection font is bundled until its licence is checked.
- Bundled fonts: IBM VGA 8×16 and, for the `block` text face (§15), IBM PC BIOS 8×8, from VileR's Ultimate Oldschool PC Font Pack (CC BY-SA 4.0, compatible one way with GPLv3), in `third_party/oldschool-pc-fonts/` and credited in `NOTICE`. `LICENSE`, `NOTICE` and the fonts' licence and README install to `<prefix>/share/doc/blockwright/`, and `blockwright --version`, `blockwright-cli --version` and the GUI's About box credit the fonts.
- Bundled icons (#224): Tabler Icons (MIT), only the eleven SVGs the toolbox uses, unchanged, in `third_party/tabler-icons/` with the upstream licence and a README giving the version and checksums; credited in `NOTICE`. The licence and README install to `<prefix>/share/doc/blockwright/tabler-icons/`, and `blockwright --version` and the About box credit the icons (the CLI draws none).
- Logo and app icon (#205, 2026-11-01): drawn in Blockwright, kept in `branding/` as projects with the flattened `.ans` beside them, and every PNG rendered from an `.ans` by `blockwright-cli render` through `branding/render`, never edited by hand. The large logo (at most 80×20) is the start screen's and the README's, the small one (at most 40×6) About Blockwright's, both built into the app at `:/branding/`; the plain-text logo (`logo.txt`, 7-bit, at most 72 columns) is compiled into both binaries for `--help` and `--version`, which keeps the version as its first line for the scripts that read it; the GUI's second line is the Qt it runs on (`Qt 6.12.0`), whose standard keys `--list-actions` lists. The icon is one 32×16-cell piece for 128 px and up (512 and 1024 are its 2× and 4× renders) and a 16×8-cell redraw for 16, 32 and 48 px, where the large one’s detail turns to mush; the black outside its rounded tile is made transparent, and the Windows `.ico` is built from the same PNGs.
- Windows (#20, 2026-10-31): cross-built on Linux with llvm-mingw (UCRT, libc++; `cmake/toolchains/llvm-mingw-x86_64.cmake`) against the official `win64_llvm_mingw` binaries of Qt 6.8.3 (6.8 is a long-term-support release), and packaged as a per-machine MSI with msitools' wixl (`scripts/package-windows`, `packaging/windows/`). Its `THIRD-PARTY.txt` names Qt and links its source through `@QT_VERSION@`/`@QT_MINOR@`, filled in at staging from the bundled Qt's `Qt6ConfigVersion*.cmake` (`packaging/windows/third-party`, through `packaging/qt-third-party`, which the dmg shares), as the AppImage's are from `qmake`, so the LGPL source link follows the Qt the MSI carries; `packaging_qt_links` accepts only that template in every `packaging/*/THIRD-PARTY*` file. zlib, zstd and libzip are built from source and linked in statically; Qt and the llvm-mingw runtime ship as DLLs beside the programs, llvm-mingw's libc++ rather than the older one in Qt's bin. Every program embeds a manifest making UTF-8 the active code page and opting in to long paths, so narrow paths and `std::filesystem` behave as on Linux and macOS; the GUI is a WIN32-subsystem program that attaches to its parent's console for `--version` and `--help`, the CLI a console program. The MSI's UpgradeCode, `4844BEF3-DC55-4186-A9B8-D858DECB36A8`, never changes and every build has a new ProductCode; its MajorUpgrade, which allows same-version upgrades, removes the earlier install of an older version or another build of the same one, and refuses a downgrade. Each file's component GUID is derived from its path. Tests run under wine in the cross build; nothing here is checked on real Windows until the CI matrix has a Windows runner.
