The composition table (`core/src/cp437_compose.inc`) is checked in, 42 (base, mark) pairs whose NFC form is one CP437 code point, generated from Unicode's canonical decompositions as Qt normalises them; the normalisation stability policy freezes those pairs, so a later Unicode adds none, and checking it in keeps the bytes written independent of the build's Qt. A test recomputes it over every CP437 code point and prints the table on a mismatch.
- Every writer: the trailing trim of a row never drops a lead, and in UTF-8 not the continuation of a lead written whole (the columns after a wide glyph are the terminal's). Whether two cells write alike (`writesAlike`, which lost blink and lint go by) takes each cell's right neighbour, so a lead is judged as the `?` the writers give it; `writtenOver` passes them, and `settleMarks` takes a neighbour the edit leaves alone from the canvas. It runs after `settleWide`, so it judges the cells the edit finally writes (a lead the settle breaks is judged as the space it becomes), and it gives a pair's continuation its lead's marks, adding a change for a continuation the edit leaves alone; a change that ends up writing what the cell held is dropped. NUL is written as a space in an `.ans` and as 0x00 in XBIN and BIN, and `writesAlike` treats NUL as a space, as the `.ans` writer writes it, so a NUL cell and a space in the same colours write alike; until slice 3 (9fcab25 and before) it told them apart.
- IRC: the lead emits its cluster's UTF-8 bytes, the continuation nothing, and no colour code goes between them; the encoder's byte count (`utf8Length`) sums the cluster, a pair counts two columns, and the continuation costs nothing and has one way to draw. A lead and a cluster of one cell also have one way (their own glyph in their own colours, never drawn solid). `mircRow` writes a lead as the UTF-8 `.ans` writer does: whole only when the width table makes it two columns and every code point is `utf8Writable`, otherwise `?` and a space in the continuation's colours, listed as wide or as a control; a cluster with a refused code point is `?`, a NUL in a cluster a space. The fit never changes a pair's glyph and doesn't recolour either half, nor a cluster or a control: it leaves them as they are (stricter than recolouring both halves together, which no step does yet); its tie-break compares code-point sequences, not `Glyph` values. The row meter (whose cache key holds a cluster's code points, not its id), the IRC view and the sender's cut count the same bytes and columns. The view lays out each line with `decodeLine` at the canvas's width (Reading), so a wrap leaves a blank filler cell; it keeps at most a row's width of cells, and cuts each line where the ascii.py sender script 1.32.2 or later cuts it, between clusters: the script's `fit` keeps a cluster whole or drops it, and its `encode_irc` and `encode_irc_min` treat a cluster as one cell in its base's colours, never trade its glyph (a █ or a space carrying a mark stays as it is) and put no colour code inside it, re-encoding a line that arrives with one there (a mark in a cell of its own, in other colours than the cell before). The view ports those rules, and a test checks its cut against tables the script made. Scripts before 1.32.2 cut per code point (sending `e` without its marks, or 👨 without its ZWJ) and re-encode per code point (`encode_irc_min` could send a █ base as a space and put a colour code between a base and its mark); against them the view can show up to a cluster fewer than a client does, and a glyph the canvas doesn't hold is sent. One known difference with 1.32.2: the script takes Mn and Me from its Python's `unicodedata` (Unicode 16 in Python 3.14), so it doesn't join the 70 marks Unicode 17 and 18 added, which the width table makes zero-width: it can cut between one and its base, or trade a █ or space base carrying one for the other, so at such a cell the view's cut can differ from the script's by that cluster, until Python has them. Sending a `.txt` (#362 round 2): Export IRC Text and `export --irc` don't take that difference, since a line that fits under one Python and is cut under another would reach the channel cut. `joinMask` gives each code point the receivers that join it, a bit for each Unicode a supported Python has (`scripts/gen-width --receivers`: 14.0, 15.0, 15.1, 16.0 and 17.0, those of Python 3.11 to 3.15, 3.11 being the oldest upstream still supports, as 3.10 reached its end of life in October 2026; the script's README asks only for Python 3), one for the table's 18.0 and one for a later version. Each bit comes from that version's own UnicodeData: Mn or Me there, or a default-ignorable code point or a Hangul vowel or final, which the script joins whatever its Python (#362 round 3, Codex 6268: taking 18.0's categories filtered by age missed U+1171E, Mn until 15.1 and Mc from 16.0, the one code point whose category left or entered Mn and Me between 14.0 and 18.0 other than by being assigned). The later bit is the table's, or set for a code point the table leaves unassigned that isn't a noncharacter: a cheap hedge against a later version making it a mark, which no receiver through 18.0 needs and which doesn't make a line safe against whatever a later version assigns. `txtSentBytes` sizes a line once for each way the receivers cluster it (receivers whose bits agree on every code point of the line cluster it alike) and is the largest, so a line is refused when any Python 3.11 or later would cut it, and the refusal says which Unicode does and names a code point it clusters otherwise when only some do (`line 2 is 408 bytes where the script's Python has Unicode 17.0 or later, which joins U+1ACF to the character before it`, `... Unicode 15.1 or older, which joins U+1171E ...`). Older Pythons are not covered: a line is sized for them as Unicode 14.0 clusters it. The meter, fit, Export IRC Text and `export --irc` size a line with `txtSentBytes` too (#364); the IRC view cuts a line once for each way the receivers cluster it and shows the fewest cells any of them keeps. IRC text's losses are listed as a UTF-8 `.ans`'s are (`irc::unwritable`, the same `Unwritable` kinds and `lossDescription` wording, `Unwritable::lead` marking a lead written `?` and a space): the CLI's `export --irc` warns on stderr and still writes, and the app's File > Export IRC Text names them and asks before saving. There is no IRC copy action to warn in. Every existing piece encodes byte for byte as before (`tests/data/golden-irc/` doesn't change).

**Project format.** `.cells` version 2 is written for a layer that holds a continuation or a cluster of more than one code point, and version 1, byte for byte as today, for every other layer: cells as in version 1, with flags bit 1 for a continuation (code point 0; its colours, attributes and transparency equal to its lead's, never in column 0 or after another continuation; what its lead holds isn't checked against the width table) and bit 2 for a cluster (the code point field indexes the table; never with bit 1); after the cells, in the same zstd frame, u32 count and for each cluster u8 n (2–16) and n u32 code points, each a Unicode scalar value and not a control other than U+0000, in order of first use, no two alike and each used, with nothing after the table. A version 2 layer with no continuation and no cluster is refused (a writer writes version 1 for it). The layer's `dither` bitmap marks a continuation exactly when it marks its lead; a writer refuses a layer whose marks split a pair, and a continuation whose values differ from its lead's. Anything else is refused, and so is a version 2 `.cells` in a project whose version is below 4.0 (judged from its header, before its frame is decoded); the code points a project's tables hold count against a limit of 4 000 000 checked before decoding (§6: a table of B bytes counts ⌊B/4⌋, so the check needs only the frame header); every layer's table and cells, and every check of the project after them, pass before any cluster is interned: each layer's table is held aside as read and the clusters go into the pool only after the last check, so a refused project leaves nothing in it (a test reads the pool's size around refusals on a layer's own check, a later layer's and the project's). A pool that runs out of ids or memory part-way through that last step keeps the entries it took before, and the read is refused; that is accepted, as it takes about four billion clusters or an allocation failing. A table is held flat while it is read (its code points and an offset an entry, the repeat check a sort of entry numbers), so an at-limit table costs tens of MiB, not a heap block an entry; the pool takes a project's table whatever the cap (Storage), and only a pool out of ids or memory refuses one. An older reader would refuse a version 2 `.cells` or, if it read one, show different columns, so a project with any such layer is version 4.0 and every older reader refuses it by its major version, as §6 has it; one without stays at the version it would have had, readable by every build that reads it now.

**Lint.** `width-varies`, an ordinary finding (a warning, not a note), so it counts toward exit 4 (checked over `library/`, `samples/`, `tests/data/` and 638 of funkpower's pieces: no file's output or exit status changes): a wide or zero-width code point whose width differs between the Unicode versions IRC clients still run (assigned after 9.0, the version that made emoji wide, or changed since; the floor is the generator's argument, the ages from `DerivedAge.txt`), and the sequences a grapheme-joining terminal draws narrower than `wcwidth` counts (a joiner, a skin tone modifier, VS16, regional indicator pairs), named as the loss warnings name them. `lone-mark`: a zero-width code point in a cell of its own, or a lone wide cell. `bidi-control`: a bidi control (U+061C, U+200E–200F, U+202A–202E, U+2066–2069) in any cell, which UTF-8 `.ans` and IRC export write as it is and which, in a client that applies bidi (HexChat, mIRC), reorders the rest of the line. `no-cp437` names clusters.

**Slices**, each one PR with its tests that leaves `main` working; nothing makes a pair in real use until slice 6, so slices 2–5 are exercised by tests that build pairs directly:
1. `Glyph` and its factories, the cluster pool (its cap, fixed directory and checked lookup) and the width table with its generator and the pinned code points. `Cell::ch` becomes a `Glyph` and every site says what it does with one. Until slices 3 and 4 teach them pairs and clusters, every writer (UTF-8 and CP437 `.ans`, XBIN, BIN, IRC) writes a lead and any multi-code-point cluster as `?`, listed in `Unwritable` (IRC text had no loss list until slice 4, so there the `?` wasn't listed, as a C1 control's wasn't), and a continuation as a space in its colours that the trailing trim drops like one, so the columns hold and nothing crashes. No output changes otherwise (the golden tests are the check), `sizeof(Cell)` is 20, the CP437 code points are narrow, the pool is safe across threads, and the timing tests record the before.
2. The pair invariant in the core: `settleWide` in `Document::edit` and `cli shade`, the composite pass after styles, selection copy, clear, move, paste and stamp, flips, rotation's refusal, mirror, brushes and pixel layers, the eyedropper, each with a pair and a lone wide cell. The renderer draws a continuation as its background.
3. The `.ans`, XBIN and BIN writers and loss reporting: `Unwritable`'s kinds, `lossDescription`, the CLI warning, the app's save warnings, the snapshots' count, the NFC composition table for CP437.
4. IRC: the encoder, fit, row meter, IRC view, `decodeLine` and the sender's cut.
5. `.cells` version 2 and project version 4.0, with the limits and every refusal tested, and version 1 output unchanged. It follows the writers so that no build reads a pair from a file before it can write one.
6. The UTF-8 reader lays out clusters and pairs, with the cluster cap and the full pool reported; the lint rules.
7. The renderer: Unifont and the system-Unifont option, the atlas, 9 px mode, squeezed wide glyphs, the cursor drawn two cells wide.
8. Cursor, typing, Backspace and Delete, lone marks, the IME and the typing policy by encoding.

## 17. The Micron target

A third target beside ANSI and IRC (§6 `target`, #350), for pages NomadNet shows: the piece is drawn for a Micron page (`.mu`, §8 Micron) and the editor offers what a page can show. The NomadNet facts below are checked against its own parser (`nomadnet/ui/textui/MicronParser.py`, 1.5.0) and the encoder's tests (`test_micron.cpp`, `micron_nomadnet`).

**What a page can show.** A colour is `` `Frgb `` / `` `Brgb ``, three hex digits, each a channel's nibble doubled (`a50` is `aa5500`): 4096 colours, the twelve-bit colours (`micron::isTwelveBit`). The parser also reads the truecolour form `` `FTrrggbb `` / `` `BTrrggbb `` (six digits), which the encoder already writes for any colour not exact in three (`micron::colourCode`), so no colour a cell holds is rounded at export. NomadNet draws through urwid: in its `24bit` colour mode it shows `#rgb` and `#rrggbb` as they are, which is what the canvas shows; in its 256, 88 and 16 colour modes the terminal side maps every colour to its own table, the three-digit ones too, which no page can prevent. Bold, italic and underline are `` `! ``, `` `* `` and `` `_ ``. A page header is read only from the first line for `#!c=` (cache), and from anywhere in the page for `#!bg=` and `#!fg=`, which the encoder escapes. A page has no line budget.

**Storage.** `"target": "micron"` in the manifest, written only for Micron as `"irc"` is only for IRC; no version bump, since the target is a preference and not art. An older build reads an unknown value as ANSI (§6), so it opens a Micron project as ANSI with every cell as it was: the art is the same, only the palette dock and the lint differ. Cells need no new colour kind: a colour from the picker or a mIRC swatch is an RGB colour (`Color::fromRgb`), the 16 stay indexed. `parseTarget` and `targetName` take and give `micron`.

**Palette and picker.** The palette dock shows the 16 and mIRC's 16–98 as under IRC (`swatchCount(Micron)` is 99, `swatchColor` unchanged), each put in a cell as the same colour IRC puts there. Colours > Pick Foreground Colour... and Pick Background Colour... (enabled only under Micron), or a left click on the pen's foreground or background swatch under Micron, open the Micron colour dialog: three sliders and spin boxes of one hex digit each (0–F) and a three-digit field (`a50`), with a preview. It opens on the pen's colour, an indexed one as the document's palette has it (an XBIN's own, as Export Micron writes it), and returns `micron::twelveBit(r, g, b)`, so it offers exactly the 4096 colours a three-digit code writes and nothing else. OK with the digits it opened on leaves the pen as it was, so an indexed pen stays indexed and an RGB one no three digits hold stays exact. Choosing a colour sets the pen, as a swatch does, and pushes no undo step. A pen colour that is no swatch (`swatchOf` gives -1 under Micron for an RGB colour no swatch has) marks no swatch; Ctrl+Up/Down/Left/Right from it step to the first or last swatch.

**Canvas.** The canvas draws each cell as the page shows it. Under Micron the renderer keeps an RGB background as it is with iCE off (`CanvasRenderer::setRgbBackgroundsKept`), as `micron::encodeLine` writes it, and so does a tool's preview (`EditPreview`, a stroke or a floating selection), for the cells it draws and the canvas's under them; an indexed cell folds by iCE as it always has, as the encoder folds it too (with iCE off a bright background is its dark colour, and a page has no blink). Formatting draws as under IRC. Typing follows the Unicode policy (`typingPolicy`), and the glyph brush, typing and the eyedropper carry formatting, as under IRC; a new Micron document's `.ans` encoding is UTF-8, as a new IRC one's.

**Meter.** No row meter under Micron: a page has no byte budget. View > IRC > Row Meter keeps its setting and the meter comes back when the target is IRC or ANSI again. The IRC budget a project holds is kept and still printed by `info`.

**Lint.** The craft rules and the ladder check run as under any target. Off under Micron: `trailing-black` and `blank-edge-rows` (IRC trims those cells; a page keeps them, §8 Micron) and `wasted-format` (its fault is the IRC bytes the codes cost). `no-cp437` gives way to the `ans-loss` note, as under IRC, and an RGB cell is judged by the nearest of the 16 and says so, as under IRC. With iCE off an RGB background is read unfolded, as the page shows it, by the rules that compare a glyph with its background (`merge`, and the looks `gap` and `flat` compare); IRC reads it the same way, since `irc::sentCell` sends an RGB background unfolded too (#ff4040 goes as mIRC 64, not as the dark red a fold makes), so a dark red glyph on it is no merge under either target. A pixel cell's halves still fold, as `halfBlocks` reads them. `width-varies`, `lone-mark` and `bidi-control` stay: NomadNet draws in a terminal.

**Export.** File > Export Micron... and `export --micron` write the composite with each cell's colour as it is: three digits for a twelve-bit colour, the truecolour form for any other (a mIRC 16–98 colour, most of which are not twelve-bit). No colour is rounded.

**CLI.** `info` prints `target: micron`. `pack` and `unpack --target micron`, and `lint --target micron` (or a Micron project's own target) as above. `lint` of a Micron project reads the composite as Export Micron writes it (RGB backgrounds unfolded). `export --micron` is unchanged. `export --irc` of a Micron project sends it as an IRC project is sent (an RGB colour kept and written as the nearest of mIRC's 99). `convert` (to `.ans`, XBIN or BIN) of a Micron project writes as for an IRC one: an RGB colour is written as the nearest of the 16 and counted as a loss, so it needs `--allow-lossy`.

**Changing the target.** Format > Target > Micron is one undo step ("Target") as the others, and changes no cell. IRC to Micron: every mIRC colour is kept and exports exactly (the truecolour form where it isn't twelve-bit). Micron to IRC: a twelve-bit colour that isn't one of mIRC's 99 is kept in the cell; IRC export and the IRC view write it as the nearest of the 99. Micron to ANSI: as IRC to ANSI, an `.ans` save writes RGB as the nearest of the 16 and counts it as a loss. Undo puts the target back and the cells were never touched.

**Slices** (#491):
1. The target in the model, the project file and the CLI's target names; the palette and the twelve-bit picker; the canvas keeping RGB backgrounds; no meter; the lint changes; `info`, `lint`, `export --irc` of a Micron project; tests for each.
2. Lint notes for a colour that isn't twelve-bit (written in the truecolour form, for a page meant to keep to three-digit codes) and for a glyph NomadNet strips (`micron::unwritable`), so the panel shows what the page loses.
3. Colours > Snap to Micron Colours: the selection's or layer's RGB colours to the nearest twelve-bit colour, as one undo step, for a piece brought over from IRC.
4. A NomadNet view, as the IRC view (§8): the page as NomadNet draws it at 256 colours.
5. Opening a `.mu` page as a Micron document.
